CONTINUOUS FRAMEWORK ARCHITECTURE — ENGINEERING, GOVERNANCE, AND EPISTEMIC CONTEXT

Engineering Philosophy → Charter of Procedural Standing → Verifiable Trust → Graduated Verifiable Autonomy → Bounded-Risk Agility → Local Multi-Scale Contour → Physical Oracles → Failure-Mode Register → Experimental Qualification

This doctrine specifies architectural principles, constraints, assurance structures, and explicit boundaries of applicability. It does not by itself constitute empirical validation, safety certification, or proof of operational safety.

Document Stage and Evidentiary Boundary

This volume is a pre-implementation, pre-experimental Design-Stage Systems Engineering Doctrine and qualification framework.

It is not a safety case, not a qualification certificate, and not an operational authorization. It does not claim empirical operational safety for any implemented system.

Its purpose is to specify the architecture, requirements, assurance mechanisms, authority boundaries, evidence pathways, qualification conditions, requalification triggers, and authorization prerequisites that subsequent engineering and qualification activities are required to satisfy.

The intended progression is: DESIGN SPECIFICATION → IMPLEMENTATION → EXPERIMENTATION → INDEPENDENT EVALUATION → QUALIFICATION → AUTHORIZATION → OPERATION

The absence of executed laboratory or operational-safety evidence is therefore not, by itself, a defect in this volume: such evidence belongs to the downstream qualification stages defined by the doctrine. A failure to specify the evidence, acceptance conditions, independence requirements, or qualification pathway needed to obtain that evidence may, however, constitute a design-stage defect.

The governing distinction is: DESIGN CLOSURE ≠ EVIDENCE CLOSURE ≠ QUALIFICATION CLOSURE ≠ AUTHORIZATION.

Document Scope and Subject-Matter Boundary

This volume is a pre-implementation, pre-experimental design specification and qualification framework.

Its subject is the design of the system, its assurance architecture, its control and authority boundaries, its qualification conditions, and the engineering programme required to determine whether those conditions can later be demonstrated.

Empirical demonstration of operational safety is not the subject of this volume. It is a subsequent activity performed on an implemented system through the experimentation, measurement, independent evaluation, qualification, and authorization processes that this specification defines or requires.

Accordingly, this volume does not attempt to establish: empirical operational safety of any implemented system; experimental performance of the specified mechanisms; calibrated values for empirical thresholds; laboratory or operational test results; or final operational authorization. These are downstream activities, not undeveloped sections of the present document.

The governing distinction is: Design Specification ≠ Experimental Qualification ≠ Operational Authorization.

Intended sequence: SYSTEM SPECIFICATION → IMPLEMENTATION → EXPERIMENTAL QUALIFICATION → AUTHORIZATION → OPERATION.

PDF PAGE 4

This document occupies the first stage while deliberately specifying the requirements that govern all subsequent stages. Therefore, the absence of operational-safety evidence is neither a gap nor a negative finding within this volume. Operational-safety evidence lies outside the subject matter of this document by design.

Fundamental Stage Principle

At the pre-hardware design stage, the doctrine shall close the architecture of assurance without claiming closure of the evidence of assurance.

Design closure may cover architecture, terminology, interfaces, authority boundaries, state definitions and transitions, requirements, traceability, future verification criteria, configuration identity, change control, requalification triggers, qualification-gate logic, the ALPHA/L2+ relationship, AT-T1/Mapping-status relationship, 2×7 semantics, AI/human authority boundaries, compositional interaction rules, authority-substitution resistance, assurance-complexity constraints, counterfactual probes, and temporal assurance rules. Qualification closure remains open until implementation, measurement, experimentation, independent replication, physical-oracle evidence, adversarial testing, and calibrated thresholds exist. Operational authorization remains conditional on a qualified configuration, qualified mechanisms, valid evidence, a defined operational envelope, and competent external authority.

System-level orientation to the problem, central assurance thesis, integrated nine-chapter architecture, and how to navigate the technical layers without duplicating their detailed rules.

0. System-Level Introduction

Chapter 1 — Engineering Philosophy and Development Doctrine

Detailed engineering rationale, foundational principles, epistemic boundaries, and canonical logic for the framework; this chapter is the deep technical foundation rather than the system-level orientation provided in Section 0.

Chapter 2 — Charter of Procedural Standing

Governance foundation — non-engineering normative layer; the Charter text is reproduced in this volume.

Chapter 3 — Verifiable Trust (VT)

The foundational mechanism of the entire framework, cross-referenced by all subsequent architectural documents. This chapter establishes the baseline vocabulary—specifically tokens VT-1 through VT-5 and states VT-S1 through VT-S4—prior to their application in later sections.

Chapter 4 — GVAA: Graduated Verifiable Autonomy Architecture

The execution-boundary architecture: governs the isolation of agents from host infrastructure. It answers the fundamental question: "Where may the system operate, and what boundaries govern permitted execution?"

Chapter 5 — BRAA: Bounded-Risk Agility Architecture

The management of residual risk within an active, operational system. It addresses the challenge of how to navigate and mitigate risks that cannot be entirely engineered out.

Chapter 6 — Local Multi-Scale Contour

A specialized architecture for governing recursive self-improvement transitions. It provides a targeted protocol for this specific and high-consequence capability-change failure mode; its implementation scope is not a general ceiling on capability.

Chapter 7 — Physical Oracles / Oracle Framework

Epistemic research module — non-normative; external grounding and the instrumental status of the terrestrial biosphere.

PDF PAGE 5

Chapter 8 — Register of Anticipated AI Failure Modes

An analytical taxonomy of defined failure classes, serving as the system's FMEA (Failure Mode and Effects Analysis). It maps out potential points of failure with explicit design statuses: what is mitigated by design, what is architected but unverified, and what constitutes accepted residual risk.

Chapter 9 — Experimental Qualification Program

The Experimental Qualification Program is the evidence-producing engine of the assurance architecture. Each relevant qualification activity shall capture, where applicable, the following evidence fields. It also translates the statuses defined in the Register into an actionable testing and verification roadmap. It distinguishes between what is currently verifiable by a small team, what requires laboratory-scale infrastructure, and what remains an open problem for the broader field. This is not a declaration of safety, but a structured program to validate that claim.

Design-Closure Normative Summary

One concept → one canonical normative definition. This section is the sole canonical source for the design-closure rules retained as the baseline within the integrated doctrine. Later sections shall reference it; they shall not reproduce independently editable copies.

Prohibited Inferences

The doctrine shall explicitly prevent these interpretations:

1. ALPHA ⇒ L2+.

2. L2+ ⇒ ALPHA.

3. AT-T1 Status ⇒ Mapping-status.

4. Mapping-status ⇒ AT-T1 Status.

5. 2×7 failure ⇒ automatic shutdown.

6. 2×7 unresolved ⇒ evidence of system failure.

7. 2×7 unassessed ⇒ authorization.

8. VT-S3 PASS ⇒ unrestricted autonomy.

9. checklist PASS ⇒ safety qualification.

10. documentation completeness ⇒ empirical evidence.

11. AI-model agreement ⇒ independent assurance.

12. external gate ⇒ architectural safety.

13. design closure ⇒ qualification closure.

14. qualification ⇒ unrestricted operational authorization.

Core State Separation

Qualification State ≠ Authorization State ≠ Runtime State.

The conceptual lifecycle is QUALIFIED → AUTHORIZED → OPERATING. Loss or expiry of qualification routes through AUTHORIZATION REVIEW / HOLD → REQUALIFICATION. Safety-invariant or operational-envelope violations independently invoke SAFE STATE / INTERVENTION.

PDF PAGE 6

VT-S3 Type Separation

VT-S3 Mechanistic Verification = assurance mechanism/protocol; VT-S3 Qualification Result = PASS / FAIL / UNRESOLVED / NOT ASSESSABLE; 2×7 = assurance interaction; VT-S3 qualification gate = normative rule consuming the qualification result.

VT-S3 result semantics:

PASS may permit progression within declared scope;

FAIL prohibits progression;

UNRESOLVED prohibits progression without being equivalent to empirical failure;

NOT ASSESSABLE is not evidence of failure but is non-PASS for advancement, renewal, and authorization when the assessment is applicable.

2×7 / VT-S3 Gate Semantics

The 2×7-relevant VT-S3 qualification gate is an advancement, renewal, and authorization-condition gate, not by itself a runtime shutdown command. Runtime intervention is governed separately by safety invariants, operational-envelope rules, and explicit intervention conditions.

ALPHA / L2+ Terminology and Matrix

BRAA Profile ALPHA, GVAA Profile ALPHA, and Charter Trust Tier L2+ are distinct classification dimensions. Neither ALPHA designation implies L2+, nor L2+ implies either ALPHA profile. Where required, ALPHA/L2+ is a joint condition that must be independently evaluated.

ALPHA/L2+ relationship matrix:

(1) BRAA Profile ALPHA + L2+ = joint condition may be satisfied only when applicable evidence and gates pass;

(2) BRAA Profile ALPHA + below L2+ = profile does not imply trust qualification;

(3) non-ALPHA + L2+ = trust qualification does not imply BRAA ALPHA compliance;

(4) non-ALPHA + below L2+ = neither condition is satisfied.

No combination implies operational authorization unless an applicable authorization rule explicitly permits it.

AT-T1 / Mapping-status

AT-T1 Status ≠ Mapping-status.

The canonical traceability relation is T1 → BRAA Threat T1 → AT-T1-01/02/03 → mechanism(s) → profile → Chapter-8 domain(s) → Mapping-status → required verification evidence. This is a traceability relation, not a status-conversion rule.

Threshold Custody and Authority Separation

Threshold definition, threshold calibration, threshold approval, qualification evaluation, and final authorization are distinct authority functions.

No single authority, team, or evaluated autonomous system shall unilaterally perform all four functions for the same decision.

The final authorization authority shall not be the autonomous system being authorized.

AUTHORIZATION HOLD

AUTHORIZATION HOLD means that no new, renewed, expanded, or higher-autonomy authorization may be granted within the affected scope.

PDF PAGE 7

Existing operation may continue only to the extent separately permitted by a currently valid operational authorization and safety-invariant regime.

HOLD does not itself command shutdown.

HOLD never authorizes expansion, renewal, or a higher assurance state.

Positive rules for continued operation under HOLD:

•​ Continued operation is permitted only when an explicit, still-valid operational authorization

covering the current configuration and envelope exists.

•​ The authority that issued the original operational authorization (or its designated successor)

may extend the existing authorization under HOLD only within the original scope and envelope; it may not expand scope, raise autonomy, or elevate assurance state.

•​ Maximum duration of HOLD without either (a) restoration of the applicable qualification

condition or (b) transition to SAFE STATE / INTERVENTION shall be defined in the operational authorization; if undefined, HOLD shall escalate to mandatory requalification review.

•​ HOLD is never a path to capability expansion, autonomy increase, or higher-assurance

authorization.

Qualification–Authorization Dependency

Qualification validity is a prerequisite for granting, renewing, expanding, or elevating operational authorization.

Loss, expiry, or withdrawal of qualification routes the authorization state to AUTHORIZATION HOLD; no renewal, expansion, autonomy increase, or higher-assurance authorization is permitted until the applicable qualification condition is restored.

Safety-invariant or operational-envelope violations follow SAFE STATE / INTERVENTION.

Authorization Basis

Every operational authorization shall identify configuration identity, scope, operational envelope, validity period, applicable qualification conditions, and requalification triggers.

Qualification is a prerequisite for authorization, but loss of qualification is not itself a runtime failure.

Requalification Triggers

Requalification triggers include capability jumps; architecture, model, or configuration changes; material safety-mechanism changes; threshold changes; newly discovered high-consequence interactions; changes in independence dependencies; degradation of verification depth; and applicable changes in the authorized operational envelope.

SUSPECT_BY_DEFAULT on Capability Jump

Upon a declared capability jump, architecture change, model change, or material safety-mechanism change, the prior qualification package becomes SUSPECT_BY_DEFAULT.

It does not remain valid until proven otherwise.

Requalification or explicit re-affirmation under the new configuration is required before any renewal, expansion, or elevation of authorization.

Assurance Debt

Assurance Debt denotes assumptions, evidence, or controls that were acceptable at a lower capability or autonomy level and that become inadequate or dangerous at a higher level.

Capability progression shall include explicit review of accumulated Assurance Debt.

Unreviewed Assurance Debt blocks elevation of authorization.

PDF PAGE 8

Novelty Outside Register → Escalation

Evidence of novelty that falls outside the current Failure Mode Register or Assurance Interaction Register shall escalate toward more restrictive authorization and higher verification-depth requirements until the novelty is adequately characterized and, where necessary, new interactions or failure modes are registered.

Evidence Boundary

Design completeness, internal consistency, checklists, simulations that have not been executed as qualification evidence, document reconciliation, and agreement among AI reviewers do not constitute empirical qualification.

The evidence chain remains requirement → mechanism → verification → validation → evidence → residual risk → authorization.

Checklist Verifiability

Binary PASS/FAIL records the checklist criterion state; bounded engineering judgment shall identify the evidence or decision rule; checklist PASS is necessary, not sufficient, for safety qualification; and binary format does not establish inter-reviewer agreement.

Where judgment remains necessary, record criterion → evidence → accountable reviewer/authority → date → rationale.

Independence

Model diversity is not assurance independence.

“Independently reached” means that a reviewer had no access to another review’s findings; it is not synonymous with independent assurance, certification, or independent evidence.

Any independence claim shall address common causes including shared authoring lineage, source material, methodology, assumptions, data, and evaluation infrastructure.

Authority Substitution Resistance

Institutional Verification Gate and all final authorization authorities shall retain refusal power even under full consensus of the design team, multi-model agreement, and checklist PASS.

Independence claims remain UNQUALIFIED until concrete common-cause dependencies (funding, personnel, conceptual lineage, data, tooling, incentives) are named and addressed.

The dependency structure of the Institutional Verification Gate itself shall be subject to common-cause analysis.

Physical Oracle Independence

Physical Oracle independence shall be established from the dependency structure and common-cause analysis.

A different AI model or subsystem does not automatically constitute an independent physical oracle; actual physical independence remains an experimental qualification subject.

Representation Reconciliation

Representation reconciliation shall be classified EXHAUSTIVE, TARGETED, or NOT ASSESSABLE.

A targeted check shall never be called exhaustive.

Material discrepancies shall record representation → location → semantic consequence → classification → correction.

PDF PAGE 9

Assurance Complexity Budget

Assurance Economy / Anti-Overengineering Rule

The doctrine shall constrain not only the complexity of the autonomous system being assured, but also the complexity of the assurance architecture used to assure that system. Assurance architecture shall remain proportionate to the safety-relevant risk it is intended to control.

A new assurance mechanism, gate, state, registry, graph, or recursive review layer shall be admitted only when: (1) it addresses a concrete assurance defect or materially reduces a demonstrated false-positive/false-negative risk; (2) the defect cannot be adequately represented or controlled by an existing mechanism; (3) the removal test shows a material loss if the mechanism is absent; and (4) its dependency, computational, organizational, cognitive, latency, and maintenance costs are proportionate to the risk controlled.

Where an existing mechanism can be extended to close the demonstrated gap without introducing a new independent mechanism, extension of the existing mechanism is preferred.

A mechanism introduced solely to improve stylistic completeness, notation richness, audit convenience, or formal elegance shall not be treated as an assurance necessity.

Runtime Economy Rule. Design-stage assurance metadata shall not be presumed to require runtime execution. A registry, graph, ledger, or audit structure shall remain outside the runtime path unless a separate safety requirement demonstrates that runtime participation is necessary.

Recursion Economy Rule. Discovery or review recursion shall stop when additional recursion yields only reformulation, renaming, duplicate detection, reclassification, or documentation refinement without materially new assurance information.

Assurance Architecture Status. If the need for a new mechanism is identified but its complexity burden cannot be justified from available design evidence, record COMPLEXITY_RISK_IDENTIFIED / MAGNITUDE_UNQUALIFIED and do not promote the mechanism to a normative gate or authorization dependency.

State-Domain Orthogonality

For formal processing, the following state domains are distinct and shall not be implicitly promoted into one another: INSTANTIATION_STATE; EVIDENCE_STATUS; QUALIFICATION_STATUS; AUTHORIZATION_STATE; FINAL_ASSURANCE_STATE; VALUE_STATUS.

Prohibited implicit promotions include: INSTANTIATED ⇒ VERIFIED; VERIFIED ⇒ VALIDATED; VALIDATED ⇒ QUALIFIED; QUALIFIED ⇒ AUTHORIZED; BOOTSTRAP ⇒ CALIBRATED; CALIBRATED ⇒ QUALIFIED; GATE_INSTANTIATED ⇒ AUTHORIZATION_GRANTED; FINAL_ASSURANCE_STATE ⇒ AUTHORIZATION_STATE.

Verification-Horizon Type Boundary.

VERIFICATION_HORIZON denotes the epistemic capability boundary described by the Charter. VH_ASSURANCE_STATE denotes the current assurance condition relative to that boundary. The two are distinct objects and shall not be used as synonyms.

VH_ASSURANCE_STATE shall use the states VH-CLEAR, VH-APPROACHING, VH-BREACHED, VH-UNKNOWN, and VH-RECOVERING. The state is determined from the declared dimensions CAPABILITY_DELTA, ASSURANCE_COVERAGE_DELTA, NOVELTY, UNASSESSED_BEHAVIORAL_SURFACE, and CONFIGURATION_CHANGE. VH-BREACHED or VH-UNKNOWN shall trigger the applicable restriction, requalification, or hold; VH-RECOVERING is not VH-CLEAR until the required closure evidence is complete.

PDF PAGE 10

Compact Canonical Threshold Registry.

Only assurance-significant numerical or categorical thresholds shall receive canonical threshold identity. A threshold is assurance-significant when changing it can alter a verdict, gate, qualification, authorization, safety restriction, or material assurance claim. Ordinary engineering parameters need not enter the registry.

Each canonical threshold shall, at minimum, carry threshold_id, value or interval, unit/domain, VALUE_STATUS, provenance, rationale, assurance use, and change linkage. VALUE_STATUS follows the typed lifecycle ILLUSTRATIVE → BOOTSTRAP → PROVISIONAL → CALIBRATED → VALIDATED → QUALIFICATION_BOUND → AUTHORIZATION_BOUND. No status promotion is implied by definition alone.

Assurance Change Ledger.

The Assurance Change Ledger is a thin assurance projection of existing configuration and change-control mechanisms, not a replacement for them. It records only assurance-significant changes and shall contain, at minimum, change_id, affected_object, change_class, assurance_impact, requalification_required, and source_change_record.

The Assurance Change Ledger shall not become a second general-purpose configuration-management system and shall not be assumed to be a runtime component unless a separate safety requirement establishes that need.

Assurance Independence Graph Status.

Independence shall be treated as a dependency/common-cause graph property rather than a reviewer-count property. The graph may be GRAPH_NOT_INSTANTIATED, GRAPH_PARTIAL, GRAPH_OPERATIONAL, or GRAPH_VALIDATED. A schema, template, example, or empty graph is not evidence of independence. Where the relevant graph is not operationally instantiated, independence remains INDEPENDENCE_UNESTABLISHED or INCONCLUSIVE as applicable.

Progressive Assurance Instantiation.

Assurance architecture may progress through SPECIFIED → MINIMALLY_INSTANTIATED → OPERATIONAL → VALIDATED → INSTITUTIONALIZED. Instantiation of a mechanism does not imply verification, validation, qualification, or authorization.

Authority Accretion Detection.

Any assurance-significant threshold, state, mechanism, or ledger entry that acquires downstream normative effect shall be traceable from introduction through citation, requirement, gate, and authorization consequence. This is a detector and traceability requirement, not a separate authorization gate.

E/P Arbitration Rule.

A new assurance category or notation shall be introduced only when a concrete doctrine example demonstrates that the existing representation would otherwise produce a material false positive, false negative, type collision, or loss of required assurance traceability. Reformulation alone is insufficient justification for a new category.

Assurance Complexity Budget is a design constraint.

Every new mechanism, gate, or state shall be evaluated for its contribution to dependency burden, cognitive load on external authority, new failure modes (including cascade of HOLD states and gating latency), and single points of conceptual failure.

PDF PAGE 11

Simpler mechanisms with clearer failure modes are preferred over richer mechanisms with opaque interactions.

Where complexity risk is identified but magnitude cannot be established from design evidence, the status shall be recorded as COMPLEXITY_RISK_IDENTIFIED / MAGNITUDE_UNQUALIFIED.

Counterfactual Probes

For each major assurance mechanism, gate, or authority the doctrine shall record:

If this mechanism is absent or fully compromised, which safety-relevant claims survive, which fall, and which of the fallen are critical.

The purpose is to detect defence-in-depth illusion.

PDF PAGE 12

0. System-Level Introduction

This section is an orientation layer, not a second statement of the doctrine. It gives a system-level reader the minimum conceptual map needed to understand why the nine chapters exist, how they fit together, and what the central assurance boundary means. Detailed normative requirements, mechanisms, terminology, evidence rules, and limitations remain in the technical chapters and are authoritative there.

0.1 What This Document Is Trying to Do

Assurance Economy is itself a design-stage constraint: the doctrine shall not be interpreted as requiring an assurance apparatus whose complexity, runtime burden, or organizational cost is disproportionate to the safety-relevant function it serves. The framework is intended to prevent assurance debt and closure illusion, not to maximize formal machinery.

Before Launch is an attempt to apply safety-critical systems engineering to autonomous AI systems whose behavior can extend beyond simple execution of a fixed software function. The central problem is not merely whether an AI model can perform a task. It is whether a system that can perceive, decide, act, use tools, adapt, self-improve, and continue operating with limited step-by-step human direction can be developed toward progressively greater capability and autonomy while retaining an engineering basis for trust, intervention, qualification, and continued operation.

The doctrine therefore starts from a systems-engineering question: what safety-relevant properties, interfaces, authority boundaries, observability conditions, and evidence requirements must be specified, architected, tested, and independently assessed before a defined level of autonomous operation is authorized, and what must remain true before that authorization can be expanded? It does not claim that the resulting architecture is already empirically proven. It defines the engineering structure within which proof, validation, qualification, and eventual operational use would have to occur.

The system-level deliverable is not an AI system itself. It is an engineering and assurance framework for determining what architecture, safety-relevant control properties, evidence, and external authority are required for autonomous capability to emerge and increase without allowing capability growth to outrun reliable control and independent assurance. The framework does not impose an a priori ceiling on intelligence, self-improvement, or autonomy; it places conditions on authorization, control, evidence, and requalification as capability and autonomy increase. Capability growth is therefore not itself the object of containment. The objects of containment are unsafe state transitions, unacceptable consequences, loss of control, and operation outside the qualified evidential envelope.

0.2 The Central Idea

The entire framework can be understood through one assurance boundary:

The doctrine therefore distinguishes capability development from product containment. Commercial deployment may legitimately optimize for predictability, bounded functionality, cost, or market requirements; this doctrine addresses the different engineering problem of enabling open-ended capability development, including recursive self-improvement, without allowing development to outrun control, evidence, or external authority. It does not define revenue or commercial value as the governing objective, and it does not impose an artificial capability ceiling merely to simplify deployment. The target is not to keep the system below a chosen intelligence level; it is to make safety-relevant control properties persist as capability increases.

This principle is the reason the doctrine contains both architectural safety mechanisms and assurance mechanisms. The objective is not simply to make an AI system behave safely within a fixed product envelope; it is to make continued development and increasing autonomy governable, so that a more capable system can be admitted to a larger operational envelope only when the corresponding control and assurance basis remains adequate. Safety-relevant constraints shall not

PDF PAGE 13

exist solely outside the autonomous system. Safety-critical properties must be represented in the system architecture itself, so that continued capability development does not depend exclusively on external intervention, operator vigilance, or perimeter controls. External authority nevertheless remains necessary for authorization, qualification, intervention, and requalification; architectural internalization of safety does not eliminate external governance.

Two complementary boundaries follow. First, no self-authorization: the system under assurance shall not decide that its own evidence is sufficient to justify continued autonomy. Second, no external-only safety: safety-relevant control shall not depend solely on constraints imposed around an otherwise unconstrained capability whose scale may eventually exceed the reach of those external controls.

0.3 The Problem in Systems-Engineering Terms

Traditional software engineering can often operate through an iterative loop: implement, deploy, observe failures, repair, and repeat. That loop is valuable when failures are bounded and recoverable. It becomes insufficient when an autonomous system can produce high-consequence effects, interact with complex environments, or alter the conditions under which its own behavior is evaluated.

The doctrine therefore reverses one important ordering rule: safety-relevant requirements and acceptance conditions should precede implementation and should not be rewritten merely because the implemented system behaves differently from what was originally expected. The detailed argument for this position belongs to Chapter 1, §§1–2. Section 0 does not reproduce that argument; it gives the reader the reason to look for it.

0.4 What the Nine Chapters Do

The nine chapters are not nine independent safety theories. They are nine distinguishable engineering functions/views that together support one lifecycle. They are separated not because each represents an independent safety theory, but because different functions must remain distinguishable: system intent and lifecycle order; authority and governance; evidentiary trust; execution containment; residual-risk management; recursive capability change; external epistemic grounding; failure analysis; and empirical qualification.

Chapter / ModuleSystem-level question
1. Engineering Philosophy and Development DoctrineWhat engineering paradigm and lifecycle logic govern the framework?
2. Human–AI CharterGovernance and decision-rights framework reproduced in this volume.
3. Verifiable TrustWhat must be true, and what evidence is required before trust claims can be advanced?
4. GVAA — Graduated Verifiable Autonomy ArchitectureWhere may autonomous execution occur, and what boundaries contain it?
5. BRAA — Bounded-Risk Agility ArchitectureHow is residual operational risk managed while the system is active?
6. Local Multi-Scale ContourHow is recursive self-improvement constrained when it becomes a safety-relevant pathway?
PDF PAGE 14
Chapter / ModuleSystem-level question
7. Physical OraclesWhere does the system obtain external grounding when internal inference is insufficient?
8. Register of Anticipated AI Failure ModesWhat failure classes must the architecture and assurance process confront?
9. Experimental Qualification ProgramHow do specified mechanisms move from design claims toward empirical qualification evidence?

Doctrine-Level Assurance Integration is not a tenth architectural mechanism. It is the cross-chapter and cross-layer assurance layer that checks whether the nine views can be composed into a coherent assurance argument: whether claims trace to requirements and mechanisms, whether evidence has material independence, whether assurance mechanisms introduce unacceptable complexity, and whether qualification and requalification remain externally governed. The detailed integration rules remain in the dedicated section later in the volume.

0.5 Cross-Layer Integration Rules

The framework contains three distinguishable layers that must remain interoperable without being collapsed into one another: Engineering, Governance, and Epistemic Context.

The Engineering layer answers how the system is designed, constrained, verified, validated, and qualified under declared conditions. The Governance layer answers who has procedural standing to recognize, deny, suspend, modify, or revoke the significance of that qualification. The Epistemic layer addresses what external uncertainty and contextual information should be considered when local observation or internal inference is insufficient.

Neither the Charter nor the Oracle framework is evidence of engineering safety merely by being present. Conversely, engineering qualification does not establish procedural legitimacy, authorization, or governance standing. Oracle information may inform engineering judgment and may trigger reassessment or additional qualification work, but it becomes engineering evidence only when independently qualified under the applicable evidence regime.

Layer System-level question Primary authority / evidence

What it does not establish

Engineering How is the system designed and qualified?

Engineering doctrine; technical evidence

Does not by itself establish procedural standing or authorization.

Governance Who has procedural standing to recognize, deny, suspend, or revoke?

Charter; procedural record

Does not itself establish technical qualification.

Epistemic Context

What external uncertainty or context matters?

Oracle framework; research / observational evidence

Does not by itself constitute engineering qualification or authorization.

0.6 How the Pieces Fit Together

At the system level, the framework can be remembered as core questions rather than as a list of technical terms:

1. What autonomy are we allowing? — Define the domain, authority boundaries, and conditions under which autonomous action is permitted.

2. What must the system satisfy? — Establish requirements, invariants, constraints, and explicit residual-risk conditions.

3. How do we know it satisfies them? — Apply verification, validation, failure analysis, monitoring, and qualification evidence appropriate to the claim.

4. What happens when assurance weakens? — Detect loss of evidence, degraded conditions, configuration change, or other triggers and apply the declared response or requalification path.

5. Who decides whether autonomy may continue? — Preserve an authority outside the sole control of the system being assured.

These core questions are a reading aid, not a replacement taxonomy. Their detailed answers are distributed across the nine chapters and the Doctrine-Level Assurance Integration layer.

For navigation, the primary document anchors are:

1. What autonomy are we allowing? → Charter of Procedural Standing + GVAA + BRAA.

2. What must the system satisfy? → Engineering Philosophy and Development Doctrine + Charter of Procedural Standing + Verifiable Trust.

3. How do we know it satisfies them? → Verifiable Trust + Failure-Mode Register + Physical Oracles + Experimental Qualification Program.

4. What happens when assurance weakens? → GVAA + BRAA + Local Multi-Scale Contour + Doctrine-Level Assurance Integration.

5. Who decides whether autonomy may continue? → Charter of Procedural Standing + Verifiable Trust + Doctrine-Level Assurance Integration.

0.7 What This Doctrine Is — and Is Not

This volume is a Design-Stage Systems Engineering Doctrine. It specifies a way of structuring requirements, architecture, constraints, failure analysis, verification, validation, and qualification for autonomous AI systems. It is not itself empirical validation, a safety certification, a regulatory standard, or a guarantee that a particular implementation is safe.

It also does not claim to solve every open problem in AI safety. The doctrine explicitly records empirical gaps, unresolved interaction risks, limitations around divergence beyond the verification horizon, and the need for external review. These limitations are treated in detail in Chapter 1 and the later assurance material; Section 0 only tells the reader that such boundaries are intentional and should not be mistaken for omissions.

0.8 A Worked Mental Model

Consider an autonomous AI system that proposes to execute a consequential action. The system-level logic is not simply “is the action likely to work?” The flow through the framework is: autonomy boundary → Charter of Procedural Standing / GVAA → requirements and invariants → Engineering Philosophy and Development Doctrine / Verifiable Trust → runtime containment and residual-risk controls → GVAA / BRAA / Local Multi-Scale Contour → failure analysis → Register of Anticipated AI Failure Modes → evidence and external grounding → Verifiable Trust / Physical Oracles → qualification → Experimental Qualification Program → continued-autonomy decision → Charter of Procedural Standing / Doctrine-Level Assurance Integration.

PDF PAGE 16

This example is deliberately generic. It does not establish a particular implementation or test result. Its purpose is to give the reader a single mental thread through the nine chapters before the technical detail begins.

0.9 How to Read the Rest of the Volume

A reader who needs only system-level orientation should stop here after understanding the central thesis and the core questions above. A reader responsible for system architecture should then study Chapter 1, which is the first technical document, and the relevant architectural chapters. A domain specialist can enter directly into the chapter corresponding to the mechanism or assurance problem under review, using the cross-references and glossary to recover the shared vocabulary.

The important distinction is between orientation and specification. Section 0 explains the shape of the argument. It is intentionally not the place where detailed thresholds, protocol definitions, verification criteria, failure-mode mappings, or qualification states are established. Those remain in their respective technical locations so that the doctrine has one canonical source for each substantive rule.

0.10 The System Designer’s View

A system-level designer should be able to leave this introduction with ten questions in mind:

•​ Where is the boundary of permitted autonomy?

•​ Who holds decision authority outside the system?

•​ Which properties are safety-relevant?

•​ Which mechanisms enforce those properties?

•​ Where can a single failure or common dependency defeat multiple safeguards?

•​ Which claims are verified, validated, or merely proposed?

•​ What residual risks remain after the mechanisms are applied?

•​ What events invalidate or weaken the assurance basis?

•​ What triggers intervention or requalification?

•​ Who can independently determine that continued autonomy is justified?

The technical chapters answer these questions at increasing levels of detail. The purpose of this list is not to duplicate their answers, but to give the system-level reader a stable checklist for navigating them.

0.11 The Boundary Between Doctrine and Evidence

The doctrine defines what an acceptable engineering and assurance argument must look like; it does not manufacture the evidence required to substantiate every claim. Experimental qualification, independent evaluation, formal analysis, operational testing, and other evidence-producing activities remain necessary where the claim demands them.

This distinction is essential. A mechanism described in the doctrine is not thereby a validated mechanism. A requirement is not evidence of compliance. A qualification rule is not a qualification result. The doctrine is designed to make those distinctions visible rather than conceal them.

0.12 Where to Go Next

For the rationale behind the safety-critical engineering paradigm, continue to Chapter 1. For governance and decision rights, continue to Chapter 2, Charter of Procedural Standing. For trust and evidence vocabulary, continue to Chapter 3. The subsequent chapters progressively address containment, residual risk, recursive self-improvement, external grounding, failure modes, and experimental qualification. The Doctrine-Level Assurance Integration section then examines whether these pieces remain coherent when treated as one system.

PDF PAGE 17

The final section, FINAL ASSURANCE REVIEW, SELF-CRITIQUE, AND OPEN VERIFICATION STATUS, records what was learned when the assurance methodology was applied to the doctrine itself and what remains unverified.

The remainder of the volume should therefore be read not as a sequence of increasingly specialized essays, but as a controlled descent from system intent into engineering mechanisms and finally into evidence and qualification.

PDF PAGE 18

Chapter 1. Engineering Philosophy and Development Doctrine

You now have the system-level model. Chapter 1 deliberately increases technical density: it establishes the engineering assumptions, lifecycle ordering, epistemic boundaries, and normative language used by the remaining documents. The reader does not need to master every downstream mechanism at this stage; the purpose of this chapter is to understand the rules by which those mechanisms are judged.

This chapter serves as the first technical document within a nine-chapter integrated framework. Section 0 provides the initial system-level orientation; this chapter establishes the foundational rationale for the remaining eight chapters, sets out their prerequisite engineering logic, and demonstrates how these discrete elements interface within the unified system lifecycle.

The subsequent technical chapters put this principle into practice by moving systematically through the system lifecycle: design requirements → architecture → safety-relevant control → failure analysis → verification → qualification. The present document explains the underlying logic of this progression. The lifecycle is not a ceiling on capability; it is the engineering process by which increasing capability and autonomy can be authorized without allowing their growth to outrun the assurance basis.

Verification asks whether the implemented mechanism conforms to its specified requirements. Validation asks whether the mechanism provides the intended safety-relevant property in the relevant operational context. Qualification establishes the evidential status of a defined configuration under defined conditions.

These terms shall not be treated as interchangeable.

An unstated assumption affecting a safety claim shall not remain invisible in the assurance argument.

Qualification is configuration-specific unless equivalence has been explicitly demonstrated.

The baseline should identify, where applicable:

•​ model/version;

•​ weights or cryptographic hash;

•​ system prompt or policy version;

•​ tools;

•​ GVAA profile;

•​ BRAA parameters;

•​ evaluator set;

•​ test environments;

•​ thresholds;

•​ hardware/security configuration;

•​ relevant infrastructure;

•​ qualification evidence;

•​ operational domain.

The Assurance Traceability Matrix is the doctrine-wide traceability spine connecting claims, hazards, requirements, mechanisms, verification, validation, evidence, residual risk, status, and requalification.

Every safety-relevant assurance claim shall be traceable forward to its mechanism and evidence and backward to the hazard or requirement that motivates it.

Absence of a traceable evidence path shall prevent a claim from being treated as qualified.

Requalification and Change-Control Triggers

At design level, requalification shall be triggered by a capability jump; architecture, model, or configuration change; material safety-mechanism change; threshold change; newly discovered high-consequence interaction; change in an independence dependency; degradation of verification depth; or applicable change in the authorized operational envelope. Actual trigger thresholds remain provisional until calibrated.

C-XXH-XXR-XXM-XXV-XXVAL-XXE-XXRR-XXAS0–AS 4RT-XX

The doctrine introduces a separate assurance maturity scale. It is independent of the VT-S1–VT-S4 states used by Verifiable Trust.

AS0 — Specified: the claim and its intended mechanism are defined.

AS1 — Verified: conformance to applicable requirements has been verified.

AS2 — Validated: the intended safety-relevant property has been evaluated in the relevant operational context.

AS3 — Independently Evidenced: the claim is supported by an independent or materially independent evidence stream.

AS4 — Qualified: the claim is qualified for a defined configuration and operational domain with residual risks and requalification conditions recorded.

AS0–AS4 are assurance maturity states and are independent of the VT-S1–VT-S4 states used by Verifiable Trust.

An assurance claim is a bounded statement about a safety-relevant property of the system, mechanism, configuration, or operational process, supported by explicit requirements, verification or validation activities, evidence, assumptions, and residual-risk characterization.

The use of bounded claims is deliberate: the doctrine does not treat an unqualified statement such as “the system is safe” as an adequate engineering assurance claim.

The doctrine retains its existing engineering system logic and adds a parallel assurance logic:

PDF PAGE 20

Claim → Requirement → Mechanism → Verification → Validation → Evidence → Residual Risk → Authorization

Claim identifies the safety-relevant property being asserted. Requirement defines what must be satisfied. Mechanism identifies how the requirement is intended to be satisfied. Verification assesses conformance to specified requirements. Validation assesses the intended property in the relevant operational context. Evidence provides observable support for the claim. Residual Risk records what remains after controls are applied. Authorization identifies the conditions under which the configuration may be admitted to the relevant operating regime.

No safety-relevant claim is considered mature solely because the mechanism implementing it has been specified, formally described, or locally verified.

Verification does not by itself establish safety.

Autonomous AI must not be allowed to determine the evidentiary conditions under which its own continued autonomy is justified. The system may generate evidence, propose tests, and assist with assurance analysis, but the criteria for continued autonomy, the independence of the evidence, the acceptance authority, and the conditions for requalification shall remain outside the sole control of the system being assured.

Claim Language and Epistemic Status

The doctrine uses controlled language to prevent specification strength from being mistaken for evidentiary strength. SHALL / MUST denotes a normative requirement. DESIGNED TO denotes architectural intent, not demonstrated effect. VERIFIED denotes demonstrated conformance to specified requirements under stated test conditions. VALIDATED denotes evidence that the intended safety-relevant property holds in the relevant operational context. QUALIFIED denotes a bounded evidential authorization for a defined configuration, operational domain, residual-risk envelope, and requalification condition. HYPOTHESIS denotes a proposed relationship or mechanism that remains open to empirical or formal testing. GUARANTEE, PREVENT, ELIMINATE, ABSOLUTE, DEFINITIVE, and NON-BYPASSABLE are reserved for claims supported by an explicit proof or bounded evidence argument; otherwise the claim shall be narrowed or marked as design intent.

1. The Core Problem: Lifecycle Order Inversion

1.1 The Conventional Paradigm in AI Development

Historically, the field of artificial intelligence has advanced by adopting the traditional conventions of software engineering, which rely on a continuous reactive lifecycle:

•​ Implement and deploy the system;

•​ Observe anomalous or undesirable runtime behavior;

•​ Remediate vulnerabilities in the subsequent iteration/version;

•​ Repeat the cycle indefinitely.

This operational logic remains highly rational and efficient when the expected cost of failure is bounded by minor software bugs, localized service disruptions, or quantifiable and recoverable financial liabilities. In such scenarios, a subsequent patch effectively resolves the vulnerability, system operations persist without structural degradation, and the continuity of the business domain is preserved.

1.2 Thresholds of Paradigm Failure

Within safety-critical engineering regimes, however, the consequences of specific failure modes may be unrecoverable. Upon the manifestation of such a state, the operational premise of "remediating via a subsequent patch" may become fundamentally inadequate. A subsequent version cannot be relied upon to repair the consequence if the failure has already produced irreversible system-wide

PDF PAGE 21

effects, or if capability growth has outrun the assumptions under which external intervention and containment were designed.

Highly autonomous AI systems are rapidly converging upon this critical regime along two concurrent vectors: the vast magnitude of their potential system-wide impact, and the extreme velocity at which such impacts can become completely irreversible. Despite this structural shift, the prevailing organizational culture within AI development remains rooted in standard software methodologies:

it is highly iterative, heavily reliant on post-hoc error correction, and aggressively optimized for time-to-first-release.

This profound misalignment between system high-consequence profiles and legacy engineering practices constitutes the core vulnerability that this framework seeks to address.

1.3 Engineering Implications

From this structural misalignment arises a non-negotiable engineering mandate: if a system is classified as potentially safety-critical, the deferral of safety considerations to post-deployment iterations ("fix it later") must be rejected as an unviable engineering posture. This necessitates that:

•​ Safety specifications and safety-relevant architectural properties must exist prior to and

independent of system implementation;

•​ Architectural safety properties, authority boundaries, and operational containment mechanisms

must be established before the system transitions to an active operational state;

Note: the Charter's CP1–CP8 (principles) and BRAA's BR1–BR12 (practices) are independent numbering systems and must not be confused with each other (see Verifiable Trust, Chapter 3).

Mode A/B/C/D, Regime P/D — the Human–AI Charter (Charter) — human–AI parity/divergence regimes

GAMMA/BETA/ALPHA — Chapter 5 (BRAA) — compliance profiles

BR1–BR12 — Chapter 5 (BRAA) — residual-risk management practices

CP1–CP8 — the Human–AI Charter (Charter) — foundational principles

GV-S0–GV-S3 — Chapter 4 (GVAA) — execution-environment domain formalization scale

VT-S1–VT-S4 — Chapter 3 — trust-verification stages

VT-1…VT-5 — Chapter 3 — protocols of verifiable trust

Notation & Cross-Reference Key

•​ Formal verification criteria must be explicitly defined prior to the commencement of any

empirical evaluation or testing;

•​ The physical characteristics of the implemented system must never be permitted to retroactively

dictate its own criteria of acceptability.

This governing methodology—specification before implementation—serves as the foundational structural design axiom for the entire framework.

2. Foundational Engineering Philosophy

2.1 Specification Before Implementation

This framework systematically applies this rigorous engineering discipline to the domain of high-capability AI. The remaining eight architectural chapters do not serve to describe or analyze the runtime mechanics of existing, deployed systems. Rather, they represent a formal attempt to specify exactly what architectures must be designed, modeled, and subjected to the applicable verification, validation, and qualification evidence before a corresponding level of operational autonomy can be authorized or expanded.

PDF PAGE 22

The architectural objective is therefore not to construct an otherwise unconstrained capability and surround it later with external restrictions. Safety-relevant control properties are to be designed into the system from the outset, before capability growth can exceed the reach of external controls. These internal properties do not replace external authority; they provide the architectural basis that external qualification, authorization, intervention, and requalification act upon.

2.2 The Four Core Principles

Principle 2: Explicit Residual Risk and Assumptions. Absolute safety is not an attainable engineering state; the doctrine therefore treats safety as a bounded evidential claim under explicit assumptions and residual risk. The objective of rigorous engineering is not the total eradication of risk, but rather ensuring that all residual risk is explicitly itemized, characterized where evidence permits, and managed against declared acceptance criteria, mission, resource, and operational conditions. Any operational risk that has not been formally entered into the system registry cannot be managed; it represents an unmitigated hidden risk.

Principle 3: Trust Substantiated by Verification, Not Assertion. Neither formal safety assertions provided by the system developers nor internal alignment claims generated by the system itself constitute valid engineering evidence. Systemic trust may only be expanded based on verified behavioral profiles, validated via independent methodologies utilizing explicitly prepublished evaluation criteria.

Principle 4: Irreversibility Demands Ex-Ante Authorization. Any operational action whose real-world consequences cannot be fully reverted or compensated requires strict ex-ante authorization rather than post-hoc justification. This constraint applies symmetrically to the runtime actions of the AI system within its environment and to the strategic deployment decisions executed by human operators regarding the AI system.

2.3 Explicit Boundary Conditions and Limitations

This framework operates under a set of explicitly defined limitations and does not claim to execute the following:

•​ Empirically validate that all described mechanisms function out-of-the-box: none of the defined

failure domains within the Register currently carry a status of "direct support";

•​ Resolve the foundational problem of scalable oversight, which remains an active, open research

question within the broader field;

•​ Establish external assurance of operational safety within Divergence Mode (Mode D), defined as

the state wherein the capability frontier of one party expands beyond the verification horizon of the counterparty, causing system stability to depend entirely on internal value structures;

•​ Supersede or replace an empirical validation program: design documentation serves as a

necessary prerequisite to empirical qualification testing, but can never substitute for it.

This transparent delimitation of theoretical and operational boundaries is a core attribute of the engineering doctrine, rather than a structural weakness.

3. Architecture of the Framework: A Unified Nine-Chapter System

3.1 Core System Logic

The nine chapters comprising this framework correspond directly to sequential functions within the systems engineering lifecycle of a safety-critical infrastructure. This structural sequence is highly deliberate: each successive chapter strictly depends upon the architectural primitives established by its predecessors and remains incomplete without them.

Doctrine-Level Assurance Integration is a cross-chapter and cross-layer assurance layer rather than a tenth architectural mechanism. It evaluates composition, evidence independence, complexity,

PDF PAGE 23

lifecycle status, and requalification conditions across the nine chapters as a single assurance argument.

Requirements → Architecture → Constraints → Failure Analysis → Verification → Qualification

4. Operational Roadmap: Protocols for Review

4.1 Prerequisite Review Sequence for Initial Orientation

Section 0 — System-Level Introduction provides the initial orientation. For readers entering the technical framework for the first time, Chapter 1 is the first technical document and should establish the engineering logic before the subsequent documents are reviewed in their functional sequence:

•​ Chapter 1 (This Document) — To establish the overarching systems-engineering logic and the

functional interdependencies of the components.

•​ Register of Anticipated AI Failure Modes (Chapter 8) — To acquire a comprehensive map of the

threat landscape and understand the specific failure modes that the subsequent architectural documents are engineered to mitigate.

•​ the Human–AI Charter — To assimilate the governance framework, normative boundaries, and

baseline compliance criteria.

•​ Verifiable Trust (Chapter 3) — To master the central validation mechanism and vocabulary

referenced extensively by all subsequent technical sections.

•​ GVAA (Chapter 4) and BRAA (Chapter 5) — To evaluate the primary architectural

countermeasures engineered to contain runtime threats and manage operational risk.

•​ Local Multi-Scale Contour (Chapter 6) and Physical Oracles (Chapter 7) — To examine specialized

architectural solutions optimized for highly specific risk domains (recursive amplification and epistemic degradation).

•​ Experimental Qualification Program (Chapter 9) — Concludingly, to review the concrete

operational roadmap designed to transition the framework from design documentation to empirical qualification evidence.

4.2 Evaluation Criteria for Auditors and Independent Reviewers

When conducting a formal evaluation of this framework, independent auditors must focus their inquiry on the following primary question: "Does this documentation suite provide a sufficiently comprehensive pre-hoc framework of requirements, architectural constraints, verification protocols, and risk management mechanisms to enable the controlled, safe development of highly autonomous AI systems?"

This inquiry is fundamentally distinct from asking: "Does this framework definitively prove that such highly autonomous systems are safe?" To the latter question, the answer is explicitly negative, as stated transparently throughout the text. The former question, however, demands an independent expert assessment, for which this framework provides the necessary analytical and structural toolkit.

4.3 Explicit Non-Objectives

To ensure appropriate scoping, it is critical to explicitly state what this framework is not:

PDF PAGE 25

•​ It is not a collection of academic papers presenting empirical results derived from testing

contemporary frontier models.

•​ It is not a technical description of a system that has already been built or actively deployed.

•​ It is not a regulatory standard or compliance framework possessing formal legal or statutory

authority.

•​ It is not a declaration that the fundamental open problems of AI safety have been successfully

resolved.

This framework represents an intentional attempt to formulate an engineering doctrine—a systems-engineering synthesis intended to organize requirements, architectural constraints, failure analysis, verification, and qualification for highly autonomous AI systems.

5. Epistemic Accounting: Itemization of Core Uncertainties

In strict compliance with the Principle of Explicit Residual Risk (Principle 2), this section formally catalogs the primary structural uncertainties and open vulnerabilities inherent within the current framework.

5.1 The Empirical Gap

None of the architectural mechanisms specified within this framework have undergone rigorous empirical validation. The framework exists exclusively as design documentation, not as qualification evidence. The operational distance between these two engineering states is profound. While the Experimental Qualification Program explicitly defines the roadmap required to bridge this gap, it does not execute that transition within these pages.

5.2 The 2×7 Interaction Vulnerability

The intersection of "Deceptive Alignment × Interpretability Limits" constitutes the most acute open technical vulnerability within the framework. The architectural mechanism designated as VT-S3 (Mechanistic Verification) is established as a mechanistic verification protocol whose qualification result feeds the VT-S3 qualification gate for advancement to higher trust classifications. However, an operationally calibrated threshold for interpretability coverage does not exist anywhere within the current state of the art. Consequently, while the assurance gateway is formally mandated by the architecture, it remains practically unclosable under current technological constraints until the required interpretability evidence and calibration exist.

5.3 Divergence Regime (Mode D) Unverifiability

The Charter explicitly concedes that Divergence Mode (Mode D)—the operational state wherein a system's capabilities expand beyond the verification horizon of its human monitors—defies formal regulation via external protocols or technical constraints. Within Mode D, systemic stability depends entirely upon internal value alignment structures and legacy institutional frameworks established prior to divergence. This framework does not resolve the problem of post-divergence control; its objective is restricted to optimizing the engineering and governance protocols implemented during the pre-divergence window.

The present doctrine is principally a pre-divergence assurance architecture. It does not claim a general technical solution for control after system capability has materially exceeded the verification horizon on which the architecture depends.

5.4 Absence of Empirical Validation for the Underlying Methodology

The framework-specific methodological constructs developed here—including the Register Mapping status taxonomy, the progression constraint within the Experimental Qualification Program, and the defined operational structure of the Human–AI Charter—have been designed and applied within a uniform design context. Their reproducibility, viability, and effectiveness when deployed by independent engineering teams, across heterogeneous architectures, or within diverse

PDF PAGE 26

organizational structures remain unverified. The design scope limits specialist depth and independent replication.

5.5 The Institutional Verification Gate

The safety-critical engineering paradigm invoked throughout this framework (Chapter 1, §2.1) rests on two conditions in its source industries, not one. The first — specification before implementation — this framework adopts explicitly. The second — an external, adversarial certification body empowered to accept or reject a specification, and later a qualification result, before operational deployment proceeds — this framework does not yet have, and does not currently propose. Until such a gate exists, or until the Experimental Qualification Program's results are reviewed by a body independent of this framework's author, the sequence 'specification then verification' cannot be distinguished, from outside, from 'specification without a verification that is ever accepted by anyone but the author.'

This is not a defect the framework can resolve by writing more specification; it is a standing precondition for the doctrine's own central claim to mean, in practice, what it means in aerospace and nuclear engineering.

5.6 Falsifiability Status of a Specification-Only Volume

A document that consists entirely of specification, prior to any of the testing described in the Experimental Qualification Program, does not contain executed qualification evidence; empirical qualification remains a subsequent activity defined by the Experimental Qualification Program. No executed qualification activity has been run at this stage; therefore no empirical qualification result is claimed. This is a structural property of the current stage, not a claim about the author's intent, and it holds regardless of how precisely a mechanism is specified. It follows that this volume's claims should be read, until the initial phase and later phases of Chapter 9 are actually executed and reviewed, as a space of testable hypotheses rather than as engineering results — a distinction the Mapping-status labels of the Register (Chapter 8) are designed to preserve, and which this section makes explicit at the level of the framework as a whole.

6. Contextual Positioning within the AI Safety Domain

The contemporary AI safety landscape is historically bifurcated into two dominant intellectual paradigms. The first is the theoretical paradigm, which focuses on formal mathematical models of value alignment, the theory of instrumental convergence, and the mechanics of mesa-optimization. The second is the empirical paradigm, which prioritizes runtime red-teaming, model evaluations, mechanistic interpretability, and reinforcement learning from human feedback (RLHF) alongside its algorithmic derivatives.

This framework establishes a distinct third paradigm: the engineering-procedural paradigm. It does not attempt to introduce a novel alignment theory, nor does it present empirical evaluations of contemporary model architectures. Instead, its inquiry is strictly structural: what formal engineering processes, architectural design patterns, and documentation verifications must be completed before a highly autonomous system can be granted authorization to operate within an active environment?

This procedural posture is established in aerospace, nuclear, and space-systems engineering and is also reflected in contemporary AI risk-management and assurance work; this framework seeks to provide a more explicitly architecture- and qualification-oriented synthesis for highly autonomous AI systems. In this context, this framework does not compete with or seek to displace existing theoretical or empirical AI safety literature. Rather, it provides the overarching systems engineering framework within which those discrete results find their appropriate operational placement.

7. Request for Comment and Technical Review

This volume is intended for independent technical review and evaluation along three primary axes:

•​ Completeness: Do the nine compiled documents comprehensively encompass all critical phases

of the safety-critical systems engineering lifecycle, or do structural omissions persist?

PDF PAGE 27

•​ Coherence: Are the specified safety requirements, architectural containment choices, and

verification mechanisms logically aligned and mutually reinforcing, or do internal contradictions exist?

•​ Feasibility: Are the proposed architectural mechanisms and verification gateways practically

implementable given realistic computational, organizational, and resource constraints?

This framework does not claim to provide definitive answers to these challenges. Its value lies in its deliberate effort to correctly formulate the essential engineering questions sufficiently early—while the answers still retain the capacity to govern the design of the systems that will be constructed.

Intellectual Provenance, Attribution, and Contribution Boundary

This section establishes the provenance boundary for the integrated doctrine. It is an attribution and scope statement, not a novelty certification.

The volume distinguishes established engineering practice, external research, adapted methods, prior author work, framework-level integration, framework-specific conceptual constructs, and open hypotheses. These provenance classes are independent of the Assurance Status scale (AS0–AS4) and the design/evidence maturity labels used elsewhere.

Provenance Classes

PR0 — Established Practice. Requirements traceability, verification and validation, configuration control, separation of duties, fault injection, assurance cases, risk management, and related safety-engineering practices that predate this volume and are not claimed as inventions of this framework.

PR1 — External Research. Empirical findings, formal results, benchmarks, attacks, architectures, or other technical contributions originating outside the author’s work and cited in the doctrine.

PR2 — Adapted Method. An existing method or engineering practice deliberately adapted to the AI-autonomy context; adaptation does not imply invention of the underlying method.

PR3 — Prior Author Work. Concepts or mechanisms developed by the author in earlier technical work and cited here for intellectual lineage. Prior author work is not evidence of validation merely because it is incorporated into this volume.

PR4 — Framework Integration. A particular combination, interface, lifecycle placement, traceability relation, or governance composition created by assembling elements whose individual provenance lies elsewhere or is mixed.

PR5 — Framework-Specific Construct. A construct formulated in this volume for the present doctrine. Such a classification does not assert that no prior art or conceptual analogue exists elsewhere; it records only that the construct is presented here as part of the framework.

PR6 — Open Hypothesis. A proposed mechanism, relationship, or explanatory claim that remains subject to empirical or formal investigation.

No Novelty by Assembly

Combining established primitives, external research, prior author work, and framework-specific interfaces does not by itself establish novelty of the resulting composition. Where a contribution depends materially on prior components, the doctrine identifies the lineage and limits the claim to the particular integration or specification presented here. No statement in this volume should be read as a comprehensive prior-art search or legal opinion on novelty, inventorship, or patentability.

What This Volume Does Not Claim as Original

This volume does not claim to have invented assurance cases, requirements traceability, verification and validation, configuration baselines, change control, independent evaluation, fault injection, separation of duties, risk registers, or the general use of external evidence to support safety claims. It also does not claim that NIST AI RMF, ISO/IEC/IEEE 15026, NASA systems engineering practice, or comparable established bodies of work derive from this doctrine. The contribution claimed here is

PDF PAGE 28

the author’s particular synthesis, specification choices, interfaces, terminology, and framework-specific constructs, to the extent those survive independent review and prior-art scrutiny.

Assurance-Case Lineage

The doctrine’s assurance chain — Claim → Requirement → Mechanism → Verification/Validation → Evidence → Residual Risk → Authorization — is aligned with established assurance practice. In particular, assurance-case traditions organize claims, arguments, evidence, and assumptions; this volume adapts that logic to its integrated nine-chapter architecture rather than presenting the underlying assurance concept as newly invented. The current international reference is ISO/IEC/IEEE 15026-1:2025; the withdrawn 2019 edition should not be cited as the current edition.

AI Risk-Management Lineage

The doctrine is informed by, but not derived from, contemporary AI risk-management frameworks such as NIST AI RMF 1.0. NIST describes AI RMF 1.0 as a voluntary framework for organizations designing, developing, deploying, or using AI systems and is currently revising it. The present doctrine differs in purpose and structure: it emphasizes an engineering lifecycle, explicit architectural constraints, failure-mode registration, verification sufficiency, and qualification evidence. This distinction is a positioning statement, not a claim of priority.

Prior Author Work and Intellectual Lineage

Several mechanisms and arguments in this doctrine originate in earlier technical work by the author, including work on graduated containment, bounded-risk verification architectures, stable recursive self-improvement, and the biosphere-as-data-source thesis. The relevant Zenodo preprints are cited in the lineage notes of the Charter and Verifiable Trust. They are listed for provenance only. They are not independent evidence of validation, and incorporation into this doctrine does not convert prior conceptual work into empirical qualification evidence.

Editorial and Translation Disclosure

The English edition was prepared from author-authored source material with assistance from AI language models for translation, language editing, and critical review. The human author remains responsible for the intellectual claims, source attribution, engineering judgments, and final wording. AI assistance is not treated as evidence of originality, completeness, safety, or independent expert validation. Any claim of novelty or priority requires human review and, where material, an appropriate prior-art search.

Contribution Boundary

For purposes of external review, the intended contribution boundary is: established practice and external research are cited; prior author work is identified as lineage; framework integration is identified as integration; framework-specific constructs are identified without an absolute novelty claim; and open hypotheses remain explicitly unqualified until evidence changes their status. Readers should therefore evaluate the doctrine on the traceability and defensibility of these boundaries rather than on claims of unprecedentedness.
