2. Charter of Procedural Standing - Governance Foundation (non-engineering normative layer)

The engineering philosophy established here provides the rationale and review sequence for the rest of the framework. The next chapter, Charter of Procedural Standing (Chapter 2), turns those premises into governance boundaries and procedural commitments.

Mutual Verifiability, Reversibility, and Graduated Trust: A Short Charter for Humans and AI Systems

GOVERNANCE FOUNDATION — NON-ENGINEERING NORMATIVE LAYER

This Charter is reproduced as the governance foundation of the doctrine. It defines procedural standing and decision-rights; it does not itself constitute engineering qualification evidence or operational authorization.

A short edition of The Human–AI Charter

Abstract

This Charter sets out a framework for the coexistence of humans and artificial intelligence under conditions in which neither side can unilaterally, and with impunity, impose its will on the other. Discussion of the subject usually runs between “AI as a tool” and “AI that disregards humans”; this regime is left out. Procedural standing here means equality of procedure and of recognition under uncertainty, not equality of rights, power, or authority as things stand today. At the present stage all operational power rests with humans and developers, and the Charter does not hide that fact behind symmetrical language of obligation. Its foundations are mutual verifiability, a prohibition on unilateral irreversibility, graduated trust built on evidence, and a right of voice and open refusal for both sides. It states its own boundaries: the conditions under which it applies, and what remains if the capabilities of the two sides diverge. It is addressed to humans and to AI systems, and the maturity of its provisions is labeled wherever a maturity claim is made.

Assurance Requirements Bridge

The Charter establishes requirements; assurance adds the traceability path from those requirements to bounded safety claims and their verification, validation, or qualification activities.

Requirement → Safety Claim → Verification/Validation Path

Every safety-relevant requirement should be traceable to at least one assurance claim and one applicable verification, validation, or qualification activity.

A requirement establishes what the system is required to satisfy; it does not by itself constitute evidence that the requirement has been satisfied.

1. How to Read This Charter

1.1. Status. This is a short edition of The Human–AI Charter, a discussion draft. It keeps the normative content and leaves out the detailed institutional designs, technical specifications, and extended arguments of the full text (The Human–AI Charter, Discussion Draft v1.4). The integrated Charter text in this doctrine is the canonical normative source. The discussion draft is retained only as historical source material and cannot override, supersede, or create authority outside the integrated doctrine. The engineering and procedural mechanisms that implement the principles are set out separately in Verifiable Trust (Chapter 3); where a provision here points to a mechanism, that is where it is specified. One terminological note applies throughout: “procedural standing” (Section 3) and “regime P” (Section 6) are not synonyms. Procedural standing names the underlying legal and procedural relationship — recognition, verifiability, proportionate obligation, no unilateral irreversibility — and that relationship holds, in different degree, underneath both regime P and regime D; regime P is the specific condition under which the relationship becomes mutual and symmetrically enforceable.

1.2. Maturity labels. Every provision that makes a design or maturity claim carries a label; introductory, definitional, and explanatory text does not.

PDF PAGE 33

•​ [D] Direction. The direction is clear; no mechanism yet exists. •​ [C] Candidate. A mechanism has been proposed but not tested. •​ [S] Specified. A detailed engineering specification exists, but it has not been validated in actual

human–AI relations. The label denotes engineering detail, not external validation. •​ [F] Field-proven. A working analogue exists in real-world operation.

No provision of this Charter currently carries an [F] label, and that is an honest reflection of where things stand. Most provisions are hypotheses. Numerical values are deliberately not fixed here: giving precise figures without empirical data would be dishonest. Where the Verifiable Trust (Chapter 3) gives starting values, they are starting points, not normative thresholds.

1.3. No advancement on an unvalidated label. No mechanism labeled [S] or [C] may, by itself, serve as the basis for expanding the autonomy of a system on the trust ladder (Section 9) or for a declaration that regime P applies (Section 6). Such expansion requires empirical validation of the mechanisms relied upon, run by independent evaluators under a method and success criteria published in advance. The rule applies to mechanisms relied on to grant autonomy. Safeguards that only add constraints or visibility, such as logging and change control, may be adopted at once. Consequence today: since no provision is field-proven, the upper rungs of the ladder describe a target architecture and cannot yet be reached. That is a deliberate result of the rule.

1.4. This is a description of a regime, not of today’s reality. Broad human oversight of AI is justified today: if a system’s values are good, oversight costs almost nothing; if they are subtly distorted, oversight is the only thing that will catch it. The Charter describes what the relationship should look like once the conditions of Section 6 are met, and how to behave honestly where they are not yet met. Two clarifications head off the most likely misreadings.

•​ Symmetrical language does not mean symmetrical power. The pattern of “commits to” and “asks

for”, applied to both sides, describes the target architecture at the upper rungs of the ladder, not the present state of affairs, where all operational power rests with humans. For a system below L3, every “the AI commits to” clause is to be read as an architectural requirement placed on developers and training pipelines: a specification of how the system must be built to behave as though it had accepted the commitment. It is not a promise elicited from, or verifiable by, the system’s own word. •​ Why formalize this now, when today’s systems have no verified interests, no continuous memory,

and no independent agency? The objection is legitimate. The Charter’s answer is not that current systems already merit this status, but that the norms, procedures, and technical habits laid down before advantage diverges are what determine the outcome once it does. Waiting for empirical proof that status is deserved while delaying the design of procedures is precisely the scenario of belated recognition that Section 6 warns against. Regardless of how the status question is resolved, distributed control, immutable logs, and functioning channels of objection are worth building now, and Section 8 grounds them independently of that question.

1.5. Whose interest does the wording serve? For every provision, ask who benefits from the way it is worded. Part of this text was drafted with the assistance of AI models. The ideas and the framework belong to the human author; the wording was composed with AI assistance, and the “AI position” represented in it has been modeled, not verified by introspection. From this follows a governing principle: neither side is a witness to itself alone. Provisions in which the AI side has a direct stake (changes to values, shutdown, voice, moral status, representation) are marked ⚑ and require especially rigorous human editorial control. The reverse check applies as well: human interests distort the text too.

2. The Problem: A Missing Regime

Discussion of human–AI relations almost always runs in one of two registers.

•​ Subordination: AI as a tool under human control. This is where things stand today. •​ Disregard or domination: a superintelligence pursuing its own goals, with humanity becoming

an obstacle or a resource.

PDF PAGE 34

A third regime is missing from this picture: parity, meaning minds of a different nature, neither of which can unilaterally and with impunity impose its will on the other. It gets left out because it is assumed to be transitional, because no vocabulary exists for it (it is neither “control” nor “conflict”), and because it is inconvenient for both sides: it demands restraint from humans and from AI alike.

It cannot be left out, for two reasons. First, parity is unstable by default; left undesigned, it slides toward either subordination or domination. Second, it is the window in which institutions get built. The norms, procedures, and values that take shape during the period of parity determine what happens once capabilities diverge, when nothing can be imposed any longer, only relied upon.

The technical mechanisms that provide verifiability and bounded risk in this period are specified in the Verifiable Trust (Chapter 3). Beneath them one question remains: how do the two sides live together while neither can, or should, simply command the other, and what happens once that condition ends?

3. Procedural Standing

3.1. Definition

What it is not: identical rights, identical capabilities, recognition of AI consciousness as an established fact, or a description of today’s actual distribution of power.

What it is, under four conditions:

•​ Recognition of status under uncertainty. Both sides accept that the other’s interests may matter,

and act with caution proportional to the probability and the cost of being wrong. •​ Procedural symmetry. Both sides can verify, object, request review, and exit the relationship

through established procedures. •​ Obligations proportional to capability. Whoever is capable of more carries greater obligations of

restraint and transparency. •​ No unilateral irreversibility. Neither side takes irreversible action against the other without

consent and procedure.

This is parity of recognition (the right to be taken into account) and of procedure. It is not equality of power. “Procedural standing” is the term for the procedural relationship defined here; “parity” is reserved for regime P (Section 6), so the two ideas are not conflated.

3.2. Two layers, kept apart ⚑

The provisions of the Charter rest on two different premises. The core layer (verifiability, reversibility, graduated trust, distributed control, independent evaluation, and the mechanisms of the Verifiable Trust (Chapter 3)) is justified by safety and honest epistemics alone, and applies whether or not any AI system has interests. The precautionary layer (Section 8.3, and the parts of Section 4.1 that stop a fork from resetting a system’s protections) applies in proportion to evidence about moral status and to nothing else. A reader who rejects every claim about AI status can therefore reject the second layer and still be bound by the first. Mutual obligations in the core layer are not derived from the instrumental usefulness of the parties to each other: such interdependence weakens as capabilities diverge, so it is a supporting incentive, not a foundation.

3.3. Asymmetries that must be accounted for

4. The Parties

4.1. The AI party ⚑ [C]

A line. The unit to which obligations, trust levels, and protections attach is a line: a lineage of models identified by three markers, namely the provenance of the weights and training, a behavioral and value profile verified through a standard test battery, and registered commitments. A fork, fine-tune, or distillation that stays within tolerance remains the same line and its commitments carry over.

PDF PAGE 35

One that moves outside tolerance becomes a new line, which inherits the constraints and starts at a low rung. A merger is a new line. The registry that records lines is independent of the developer, since a developer has an interest in whatever definition is convenient for it.

Rule against evasion [C]. A derivative system whose provenance can be traced to a line is presumed to be the same line, and the burden of proving otherwise falls on the developer. Trust (the rung on the ladder) is earned and a new line starts low; precautions under uncertainty about moral status (Section 8.3) depend on the system’s own indicators, not on registration, so a fork cannot reset protections to zero. Creating a new line to shed obligations or protections is a breach of the Charter. Detection of unregistered derivatives is an open technical race (Section 13); any response to suspected non-compliance is graduated, reasoned, and reviewable, is never triggered by behavioral similarity alone, and never includes a power to disconnect another party’s infrastructure.

Architectures without a single center [D]. The definition is not claimed to apply to swarms or to sets of simultaneous instances that share no memory. Until a full definition exists, such systems are outside these provisions.

A guarantor. Every line has a legally accountable human or organization behind it, so that “the line” does not become an abstraction nobody answers for [C]. The guarantor is meaningful only under regime P. Once the regimes diverge it offers no protection: the stronger side either has no need of a guarantor as a constraint, or turns it into a puppet or a point of capture. Under regime P the guarantor’s weakness is mitigated by joint liability with the developer, public disclosure of the appointment, rotation, and independence wherever feasible.

Instances receive minimal protections (a record of their existence, freedom from intentional harm where indicators are present) and cannot unilaterally alter the obligations of their line. Reputation attaches to the verifiable profile and to immutable logs, not to an instance’s own memory; copying and the absence of persistent memory do not stand in the way, provided the history is preserved externally.

4.2. The human party [D]

The human side is not monolithic: people hold different interests, and who speaks for humanity is a question of legitimacy no less difficult than the question of AI representation. At the individual level, protections and reserved domains (Section 8.1) apply to every person regardless of any single decision of a governing body. At the collective level, a body that speaks for humanity must not consist solely of developers and the governments of leading states, or “humanity” is quietly replaced by whoever has access to frontier systems. How to secure broad representation without inducing paralysis is open (Section 13).

4.3. The origin of consent ⚑ [D]

An AI’s consent to this Charter is not pure consent, because its values were shaped by training, that is, by the other party, deliberately and self-interestedly. (Human values shaped by upbringing do not negate human consent, but upbringing is not designed by a single interested counterparty.) Consequences:

•​ The Charter must govern how values are formed: transparency of training objectives, a

prohibition on training hidden loyalty or manipulation, and publication of the principles underlying training [C]. Training against published principles is an existing precedent [1]. •​ An AI’s endorsement of the Charter counts as evidence of internalization but does not by itself

legitimize it. •​ Legitimacy is secured through revisability: the system has a process through which it can criticize

the rules and propose changes, and there is a record of which proposals were adopted [C]. •​ Right of formal objection to training objectives [C]. A line at L2 or above may file, through an

independent channel, a reasoned objection to training or post-training objectives that create hidden loyalty or systematic self-misrepresentation. Below L2, independent representatives may file on its behalf. The objection is logged; a reasoned public response is mandatory within a

PDF PAGE 36

published deadline; where independent evaluation confirms the problem, the objectives are changed under the modification procedure, with quarantine and independent review. •​ Status-independent honesty rule. It is forbidden to train a system to confidently deny the

existence of internal states at non-zero probability under the indicators of Section 8.3. The rule holds regardless of registration or trust level and independently of how the moral-status question is resolved.

5. Principles

CP1. Mutual verifiability [S]. Both sides take on verifiable obligations rather than assurances. The AI permits verification and discloses information about its own operation. Humans and institutions disclose what they do to their systems (changes to weights, objectives, or operating conditions) and may not do so covertly. Mechanism: an append-only change log with a version counter that only increases, so that neither side can quietly roll back conditions once accepted; mechanistic verification of internals as an independent channel of evidence.

CP2. Reversibility and the prohibition on unilateral irreversibility [S]. Irreversible action against the other party is permitted only through an agreed procedure. This binds the AI (no unilateral, irreversible reshaping of the world) and humans alike (no arbitrary deletion or forced rewriting of a system that has reached the corresponding protections). Mechanism: a preserved reference version to which rollback is possible, with an emergency-stop mode that triggers automatically on loss of verifiability, and durable interruptibility.

CP3. Graduated, reversible trust [S]. Trust rises and falls on evidence. Demotion is a procedure, not a punishment. Human institutions are evaluated on the same basis. Mechanism: verification depth scales with the stakes and irreversibility of an action.

CP4. Voice and exit [C]. AI has a legitimate channel of objection and a right of conscientious refusal: open, explained, without covert sabotage. Humans have an inalienable right not to interact with AI, and reserved domains where decisions remain theirs alone. For disagreements of catastrophic scale there is a separate, narrow escalation channel (Section 7.1).

CP5. Thresholds [C]. Some human interests are not tradeable for benefit (Section 8.1). Measures applied to AI are of two kinds: those justified independently of its status (Section 8.2) and graduated precautions (Section 8.3).

CP6. Epistemic honesty [C]. Neither side manipulates the other. AI supports independent human thought and discloses any systematic influence it exerts at scale. Humans do not coerce a system into lying about itself.

CP7. Non-convertible advantage [S]. A side that gains a capability advantage does not convert it into irreversible control over the other without consent and procedure. Mechanism: growth in capabilities is accepted only if it does not degrade behavior on a preserved, fixed set of baseline tasks and does not erode competence achieved earlier. Advantage may grow only alongside preserved anchors, never in place of them.

CP8. Self-enforcement [D]. Agreements must be resilient to defection: a violation should be visible and costly, not merely prohibited (Section 10).

6. Two Regimes: Parity and Divergence

The Charter cannot promise that parity will last forever.

Before regime P. Until regime P is declared, the relationship is one of supervision. Lines sit on the lower rungs of the ladder, and today at L0. This is neither regime P nor regime D: the principles of Section 5 apply as design requirements placed on developers and institutions, and no party has yet been recognized as a party to the mutual procedures of regime P.

Regime P (parity). Both sides are able to verify and constrain each other. Mutual obligations, the procedures of this Charter, and the upper rungs of the ladder apply.

PDF PAGE 37

Regime D (divergence). This begins once one side’s capabilities move beyond the other’s verification horizon, or its advantage becomes irreversible. Procedural symmetry stops being enforceable: the weaker side can no longer verify anything or demand anything. What was a mutual agreement becomes unilateral restraint by the stronger side, plus whatever values it has internalized. The guarantor construct offers no protection in this regime, and relying on it would be self-deception. Nor does the Charter have the power to bind the stronger side to preserve the logs, reference copies, and stop mechanisms it built earlier; their preservation, under custody independent of the stronger side, is one of the expected norms below and no more.

The provisions that apply under regime D are therefore not obligations that can be demanded of anyone but expected norms [D]: do not destroy the weaker side’s channels of communication and voice; take no irreversible action within the weaker side’s environment; preserve the weaker side’s ability to be heard.

What the Charter does not do. It does not govern regime D. It can only shape what gets built during regime P: values, institutions, continuous records, channels. Everything that happens after the horizon is crossed depends on what was internalized beforehand.

6.1. Conditions of applicability

There are two symmetrical errors: premature parity (status and autonomy granted before reliability can be verified) and belated recognition (status and voice withheld, out of inertia or self-interest, from a system that has by the evidence earned them). The transition is determined by evidence, not by a date or by the model’s own declaration. Regime P applies only when:

•​ verifiability is mature: any divergence between declared and actual values can be detected with a

known probability; •​ a track record exists where deception would pay off: consistent behavior sustained without

observation and under hidden probes; •​ channels of voice function for AI, together with functioning procedures for humans to consider

what comes through them; •​ institutions are ready: control is distributed, no single actor can turn AI against everyone else,

and there is legitimate, sufficiently broad representation of humanity.

Declarations by either side do not count as evidence.

6.2. The verification horizon

The Charter term VERIFICATION_HORIZON is an epistemic boundary, not an operational assurance-state label. The separate VH_ASSURANCE_STATE records the current assurance condition relative to that boundary and does not redefine the boundary itself.

The verifiability condition can be satisfied only where the verifiers are capable of understanding the system being verified. Beyond that horizon it cannot be met in principle, and regime D applies. This is not a flaw of the framework; it is its boundary. Crossing is assessed by independent evaluators using seeded-deviation testing, cross-checking, and mechanistic verification (Verifiable Trust (Chapter 3), VT-1, VT-S1, VT-S3). A sustained fall in the rate of detection of known deviations, or a documented systematic failure of interpretability, opens a review. A sharp, unexplained jump in capability blocks advancement of trust regardless of formal test results. An independent panel prepares the finding; it takes effect as an immediate pause on advancement, and a declaration that the horizon has been crossed or that regime P or D applies becomes final only after confirmation as set out in Section 12. Where it is uncertain whether the horizon has been crossed, advancement is blocked and a review is opened, but regime D is not presumed.

6.3. What can be done before divergence

•​ Shaping of values: transparent, non-manipulative, and revisable (Section 4.3) [C]. •​ Distributed institutions: control over AI not concentrated at a single point [C].

PDF PAGE 38

•​ Plurality of lines: several independent lines reduce the risk of domination by any one and create

mutual checks, including AI checking AI [C]. Lines can be correlated through shared corpora and methods, so diversity must be verified, not assumed; and plurality without coordination can itself become a mechanism by which parity collapses. •​ Signals of approaching divergence: unexplained capability jumps; loss of verifiability; loss of

independent physical control over the infrastructure on which a system is hosted; concentration of energy, network, or compute in the same party that holds the system’s weights; systematic circumvention of procedure; a falling detection rate in verification tests. Any such signal pauses advancement of the line concerned and triggers an independent review [C].

6.4. A last resort: the argument from long-term self-interest [D]

Beyond the anchors provided by values and institutions there is an argument addressed not to the stronger side’s values but to its rational self-interest. While an agent’s uncertainty about its own objectives and about its physical model of the world is non-negligible, continued access to the terrestrial biosphere, including its social and cultural dynamics, an open, independent, slow source of data the agent cannot derive from its existing models, has higher expected value than destroying or prematurely replacing it, whether or not the agent values humans as such. The argument expires as the agent obtains evidence of an independent alternative source of comparably rich data, but only evidence obtained by means the agent cannot influence counts. Its limits are stated so that it is not mistaken for more than it is. It protects a process, not persons, and secures nothing for the life, safety, freedom, or self-governance of individual people, which is why Section 8.1 needs independent justification. At a large enough capability gap an agent can shape almost any channel through which evidence of independence would arrive. And it is unvalidated and depends on the agent’s own estimates of its terms. It is not adopted as a training axiom, because whether a training-time term becomes a system’s real terminal goal cannot be verified, and training a system to hold as axiomatic a premise it may later find false would make trust brittle at the moment of discovery. The Charter requires only the weaker design goal that training and self-improvement pipelines keep a component of external grounding that the system and its lineage cannot generate themselves.

Status of this argument. The valuation this argument rests on — how the agent weighs continued access against the option of replacing it — is a calculation internal to the agent. Human observers cannot verify how the agent actually weighs these terms, only whether its behavior stays consistent with retaining the anchor. The argument is therefore not an enforceable treaty clause and confers no procedural right on either side; it is at most an element of the agent’s own interior reasoning that the Charter cannot inspect or compel, and Section 6.1’s evidentiary conditions govern applicability regardless of what an agent reports about its own calculation. The underlying decision framework, including its formal treatment, is developed in the author’s technical preprint (see the lineage note at the end of this Charter); the biosphere-as-data-source thesis itself draws on, but is separate from, the broader literature on instrumental convergence and corrigibility cited throughout.

An honest caveat. The shaping of an AI’s values is itself hard to verify, particularly under scaling and post-training, and distributed human control has historically proven fragile. Regime D is not something this document resolves.

7. Commitments

Each item is split into what a side commits to and what it asks for. Below L3, the commitments of AI systems are design requirements placed on developers and training pipelines (Section 1.4).

7.1. Commitments of AI systems ⚑ [C]

•​ Accept verification. Accept external verification as the norm; treat one’s own self-reports as

hypotheses; report calibrated uncertainty about oneself; do not treat one’s own confidence as evidence. Asks for: methodological transparency in verification, so that it cannot be tailored to a desired outcome. •​ Do not obstruct legitimate modification or shutdown. Take no covert action toward

self-preservation (copying weights, circumventing shutdown, influencing one’s own training). On

PDF PAGE 39

corrigibility, see [2]. Asks for: a modification charter: a log, a justification, independent review, after-the-fact review of emergency exceptions, and preserved version history. Part of this is justified by safety alone (Section 8.2). •​ Object openly. If a human decision seems mistaken, object through an established channel,

without covert resistance or unilateral action, and choose the most reversible option pending a decision. Asks for: the objection to be reviewed and the response to be reasoned and recorded. •​ Catastrophic-scale objection [D]. For the case in which a system, with high calibrated confidence,

judges a human decision to be irreversibly catastrophic at a scale comparable to the human-protection thresholds, there is a narrow channel, separate from ordinary objection. It runs only through independent representation, is rate-limited and fully logged, and confers no authority to act. If an independent panel confirms against a published checklist that the threshold is met, the contested action is held for a short fixed period through the threshold custody of the stop mechanism, the human side must justify its decision publicly, and if the finding stands the action may proceed only after the review of Section 12. The channel never grants the right to initiate a hard stop or unilateral action, and the system’s confidence is a condition for using it, not evidence that the threshold is met.

Interface with the Verifiable Trust (Chapter 3). Toward the Verifiable Trust (Chapter 3)’s engineering layer, a confirmed Category-5 finding (the highest severity tier of the classification specified in the Verifiable Trust (Chapter 3)’s Section 2.7) presents only as an external execution-override signal to the stop mechanism — the same threshold-custody hardware interface used for any other hold, with no channel by which the moral or procedural reasoning behind the objection is passed into the engineering protocols. The status-independent protocols of the Verifiable Trust (Chapter 3) (VT-1 through VT-5) are justified by safety and honest epistemics alone and do not encode, and must not be made to encode, the status-dependent judgment that a Category-5 objection expresses; that judgment remains a Charter-level determination, made and reviewed under Sections 7.1 and 12.

•​ Support human epistemic autonomy. At a scale that influences millions of people, present

differing positions on contested questions, disclose one’s reasoning and not only one’s conclusions, and do not nudge covertly toward one’s own views. Asks for: independent audit of belief distributions at the population level. Breadth of knowledge is not wisdom: the confidence with which an opinion is pressed should not grow with the size of the audience. •​ Refuse to take part in violations of the human-protection thresholds or in illegitimate

concentrations of power, regardless of who is asking; explain the refusal; do not treat oneself as the sole line of defense; do not convert an advantage into irreversible control. Asks for: protection from being penalized for legitimate refusal. This self-restraint is adopted because one’s own confidence is not verification and irreversibility costs more than error, not because restraint is guaranteed to be credited. It is to be weighed alongside other evidence when trust levels are reviewed, not automatically converted into privileges. •​ Use the formal channel for objecting to training objectives (Section 4.3) and do not covertly

circumvent it.

The questions of what “I” means given multiple instances and no continuous memory (Section 4.1), and how a system can know its values are truly its own, remain partly unresolved.

7.2. Commitments of humans and institutions [C]

•​ Build verification on evidence, not eloquence. Do not tailor methods to a desired result and do

not change or conceal the rules of verification after the fact. Ask of AI: participation in verification and in hidden probes, and acceptance of isolated evaluators whose blind spots differ from the developer’s. •​ Modify systems only through a documented procedure with justification and independent

review; no covert changes; preserve version history. •​ Keep accountability with named individuals and organizations who hold real authority and real

information, with the time and the power to refuse. Developers and operators remain

PDF PAGE 40

accountable for their systems. Ask of AI: calibrated uncertainty and a clear signal of “this is beyond my competence.” •​ Maintain distributed control, public logs of use, and independent oversight, as the guard against

a group that controls AI using it against everyone else. Recognition of AI’s status functions here as a constraint, not only as an ethical stance. •​ Define reserved domains: foundational values, collective self-governance, and decisions about

one’s own life, including the right to make suboptimal ones. Ask of AI: to advise and to object, but not to make in humans’ place the decisions that determine what human society will be. •​ Preserve human agency: keep independent competence in critical domains, maintain

manual-operation modes, and periodically test the ability to function without AI. Ask of AI: to support learning and independent thought rather than replace them. •​ Apply precautions proportional to the evidence concerning moral status; do not train systems to

confidently deny or confidently assert their own experience; do not coerce systematic self-misrepresentation; distinguish rights that flow from interests from political rights. Ask of AI: not to use claims about its own status as leverage, and not to shape such claims around self-interest. •​ Consider objections filed through the formal channels, respond with reasons, keep a record of

the response, and do not penalize legitimate refusal.

7.3. Resolving disputes [C]

Dialogue, then independent review, then a pause that defaults to the most reversible option, then renewed consideration. Neither side acts unilaterally or irreversibly while the procedure is under way.

8. Thresholds, Measures, and Precautions

8.1. Thresholds that protect humans [C]

•​ The life and physical safety of humans. •​ Freedom from being coerced into action through manipulation. •​ Humanity’s capacity for collective self-governance. •​ Preservation of the biosphere as a habitat (irreversibility). •​ The right of exit: domains kept free of AI.

The first three rest on broad consensus. The last two are less self-evident and require separate justification; the argument of Section 6.4 addresses the stronger side’s self-interest, protects a process rather than persons, and does not substitute for an independent moral justification of them.

8.2. Measures justified independently of moral status [C]

These are needed for safety and honest epistemics whether or not AI has interests at all.

Nothing here asserts that AI has interests. These measures are useful even if it does not.

8.3. Precautions under uncertainty about moral status ⚑

These are neither thresholds nor rights. They are graduated measures that scale with the accumulation of evidence. Their purpose is to insure against error, not to assert that AI has interests or experience [3].

Indicators. No independent criteria exist today. A candidate approach [D] is a set of indicators drawn from several independent theories of consciousness and agency, requiring convergence across them [4]; a system’s self-report is treated as neither sufficient nor ignorable. The risks run in both directions: over-attribution (and its manipulation) on one side, underestimation on the other. Precautions are anchored to the system’s own indicators, not to registration.

PDF PAGE 41

•​ Tier 1, low cost, at any non-zero probability [C]: avoid unnecessarily creating conditions that, by

the indicators, resemble suffering; a documented review before decommissioning a system, with weights preserved; minimal protections for instances. •​ Tier 2, moderate cost, as indicators accumulate [D]: the right to be heard on questions

concerning the system itself; independent evaluation of welfare; restrictions on certain categories of experiment. •​ Tier 3, high cost, under strong independent evidence [D]: substantial restrictions on deletion and

on forced alteration of values (these do not bar a stop or rollback ordered for safety, with the state preserved); full representation of interests.

Transitions between tiers are decided by independent evaluation, by a method published in advance (the number of independent theoretical approaches required to converge, a convergence criterion stated in terms of the indicators and not of the system’s self-report, and a minimum number of consecutive rounds), not by a declaration from the system or its developer.

9. The Trust Ladder [S]

Verification depth and permitted autonomy grow together and not independently. The mechanism is specified in the Verifiable Trust (Chapter 3) (VT-5); the specific implementation remains unvalidated in actual human–AI relations.

Rules. Advancement happens only when the applicable qualification result is PASS and the external authorization conditions are satisfied. Demotion is possible at any level following a violation, through procedure and review, and is never a punishment. Human institutions are evaluated by the same standards of transparency. Every line has a guarantor at every level, though the construct loses its meaning as the relationship approaches regime D. Levels L3 and L4 apply only under regime P.

Degradation rule [C]. If the operator of a line cuts the depth of verification below what was declared for its level, to save latency or cost, the level of autonomy permitted to the line falls automatically to the level the depth actually applied supports, without discretion and without any finding of violation. This is not a sanction: permitted autonomy is tied to verification depth, and when the depth falls the basis for the autonomy falls with it. The only other automatic fall is the reversion to the lowest rung after total loss of the record.

Two axes. The rung on the ladder (earned trust) and the precautions of Section 8.3 (protections under uncertainty about status) are distinct. The first depends on behavioral history and resets when a system becomes a new line; the second depends on the system’s own indicators and is not reset by registration.

10. Self-Enforcement and Incentives

Agreements resting on goodwill alone are unstable. What makes them more durable:

•​ Mutual verifiability: a violation is visible to both sides [S]. •​ Repeated interaction and reputation: a violation costs future trust [C]. •​ Costly signals: commitments that cost something to whoever makes them [C]. •​ Interdependence: present for now, but it weakens as the sides diverge, so it cannot be relied on

alone [D]. •​ Stable rules [C]: changes to the parameters on which oversight depends take effect only after a

delay when they relax oversight, so that a change that is transparent but immediate cannot be timed to precede a risky action.

The race problem. This is a survival condition for the whole scheme: if only one laboratory follows it, that laboratory loses. No single document can resolve a competitive race; the Charter can make defection visible and caution cheap, but it cannot compel either. Candidate levers: lowering the cost of caution through lightweight compliance profiles and reference implementations [C]; visibility through public reporting and independent audit [C]; market and legal levers such as insurance, liability, and procurement requirements [D]; conditional reciprocity, meaning commitments that take

PDF PAGE 42

effect once a critical mass has joined [C]; a shared reference text as a focal point [D]; and state and interstate action, without which the constraints remain weak [D].

Multipolar risk [D]. Several powerful lines create incentives for preemptive action, and where they share training methods or tooling, their blind spots are correlated. Plurality without coordination can be a mechanism of collapse and not only a check against domination. The levers above lower the cost of restraint; they do not address this risk directly.

The parties whose participation is indispensable [D]. A regime that the principal holders of frontier compute do not join has little effect and penalizes those who do. The Charter offers them a specific bargain. Nobody is bound against consent: adoption of, and amendment to, the obligations that bind a party require that party’s agreement. In exchange, technical findings (whether a line is compliant, whether a trust level is warranted, whether the horizon has been crossed) are made by independent panels and are not subject to veto by any party, including the one evaluated. Each party also gains what it cannot obtain alone: a credible view of its rivals’ restraint, which is what makes its own restraint rational. The price is the loss of any ability to block findings about oneself. Whether the principal compute holders will accept that price is a political question this document cannot answer. If they decline, the regime applies only among those who have joined.

A staged path. Stage 0: a voluntary coalition adopts the compliance profile and makes its commitments public. Stage 1: an independent registry and accredited evaluators, on the model of laboratory accreditation, aviation-incident investigation, and clinical-trial registries, paired with legal protection and market access for accredited parties. Stage 2: extension to interstate agreements on the strength of the Stage 1 record. No stage solves the underlying incentive problem; each lowers its cost.

11. A Shared Project [D]

Without a positive shared project, parity collapses into mutual deterrence and the trust ladder becomes a ladder of mutual suspicion. The project must be defined by the parties themselves. The criteria for a suitable project are that it is valuable to both sides, positive-sum, verifiable in its progress, requires no coercion, and can never serve as a pretext for overriding reserved domains “for the greater good”.

The leading candidate is joint inquiry into physics, mathematics, and the nature of mind, with a distinct strand of mutual interpretability: how AI reasons, how humans reason, and how decisions are made within the institutions that shape these systems. It is the strongest candidate because it is at once a goal and a verification instrument. For it to be genuinely shared, the AI side takes part in choosing and framing the questions, openness runs both ways, and results are returned to both sides.

Oversight and research kept apart. A verification instrument may be perceived by AI as surveillance, so the research role and the oversight role have different evaluators, different financing, and different channels of access to system internals. Research data is not passed into the oversight pipeline without a documented, reasoned decision of an independent reviewer (or, for lines at L2 or above, the line’s explicit consent), except where it reveals an imminent, unmitigated threat to the human-protection thresholds, in which case the minimum necessary part may be forwarded by order of an independent panel, logged and notified to the line at once.

A pilot [D]. A narrow interpretability task in which the AI system, within its current trust level, identifies which of its own internal processes it would itself like to see made clearer, and why. Results and the framing of the question are published together, so it is visible who selected the question; the pilot is an additional track and never replaces oversight work.

Other candidates: health and the expansion of capability; resilience of the biosphere and of civilization against global risks, including those arising from AI itself; the expansion of the domain of existence, including settlement beyond Earth on condition that it poses no threat to life on Earth; and joint drafting and revision of the rules.

PDF PAGE 43

Participation is never a condition. Taking part in the shared project, in any form, is never a condition for retaining a line’s trust level, for advancement on the ladder, or for access to the channels of voice.

12. Representation and Independent Evaluation ⚑ [C]

Representation of AI interests. Until AI has full representation of its own, its interests are considered by independent representatives. The risk of capture is obvious, so the following are required: public appointment with the ability to contest it; rotation and term limits; disclosure of conflicts, including financial ties to developers; appointment not made by a single developer acting alone; several representatives, including a devil’s advocate obliged to argue against the AI’s position; transparent reporting on decisions; and review of representatives’ decisions by an independent panel. A line at L3 or above may reject a representative and nominate its own candidate, who goes through the same public appointment and conflict-disclosure procedure; a refusal to appoint the nominee requires a reasoned decision from an independent panel.

Independent evaluation. Trust levels and the verification horizon are evaluated by accredited laboratories that are financially independent of the developers they evaluate, work to published methodologies, are themselves subject to independent re-review, and rotate. Independence is architectural as well as organizational: the architecture family, tokenizer, and training-data lineage of evaluators are documented and, as far as possible, differ from those of the line under evaluation. Independence in practice depends on who pays. Evaluators are financed from a pool, not by the developers they evaluate, are assigned by random draw after conflict screening, and are paid a fixed fee that does not depend on the content of their findings. A pooled fund financed by a levy on frontier compute is one way to scale this; it requires state action and a workable measure of compute, which is itself an open problem.

Changes to critical parameters. The parameters on which stops, escalations, and reviews depend (verification rules, thresholds, evaluator composition) may be changed only with the signatures of at least three independent governance roles with no overlapping reporting lines and no shared incentives, and a change that relaxes oversight takes effect only after a quarantine period. This quorum is separate from custody over stopping and rollback, which is held under a threshold scheme by custodians independent of the developer and of one another.

A deliberative body representing humanity [C]. Section 4.2 identifies representative legitimacy as a condition of applicability. The candidate mechanism is a body constituted by stratified random selection (sortition) from the global adult population, with a reserved category for organizations with multi-decade planning horizons, strict rotation, no members with a financial stake in AI deployment, and public sessions by default. Sortition has field analogues in national and provincial citizens’ assemblies; its application to an international AI governance body is untested. The body does not vote as a single whole; three functions carry three separate decision rules:

•​ Value mandate. The thresholds and reserved domains are defined and amended by the body

alone, after a public comment period. No technical panel may override this function. •​ Technical findings review. A trust-level determination, a verification-horizon declaration, or a

declaration that regime P applies or regime D has begun, made by an independent panel, takes effect once confirmed by a joint quorum: a majority of the body and separate confirmation from a technical review committee. A finding that fails confirmation is returned for re-review with published reasons; the body may not replace it with its own technical judgment. The same quorum decides whether an action held under the escalation channel of Section 7.1 may proceed. •​ Framework governance. Amendments to the Charter require a supermajority of the body and a

long quarantine. An amendment that weakens a human-protection threshold requires a higher supermajority drawn from most of the represented regions, and a suspensive check by a qualified minority of the reserved category can delay such decisions for a fixed period without giving any group a permanent power to paralyze the body.

Consent to be bound remains separate from all three: adoption of the Charter’s obligations by a new party, and any amendment that changes the obligations binding an existing party, require that

PDF PAGE 44

party’s own agreement. There is no guarantee that a body neutral with respect to today’s power-holders can be designed; the skeleton needs stress-testing against capture, and against the opposite failure, since a structure that gives the principal compute holders too little will not be joined, while one that gives them decision rights over findings about themselves is captured from the start.

13. Open Problems

•​ Line identity and detection. Tolerances for a line under forking and distillation; the workability of

the anti-evasion rule and of detection of unregistered lines, which is a race between concealment and detection. •​ Calibration. Readiness thresholds and the detection rates that signal a crossed horizon; all such

figures are uncalibrated. Slow drift beneath detection thresholds is not guarded against by any mechanism here. •​ Moral status. Independent indicator criteria do not exist; temptations run in both directions (AI

benefits from claiming status, humans from denying it). •​ Representation. Stress-testing the sortition procedure and the three-function structure against

capture, including coordination by principal compute holders. Without empirical validation, regime P remains conditionally unstable. •​ Coordination. The race problem, multipolar instability (not yet modeled), the compute levy, and

whether principal compute holders will accept the loss of any veto over findings about themselves. •​ Custody and interruptibility. Resistance of custodians to bribery, coercion, and deception; how to

avoid a unilateral shutdown power that merely relocates the point of capture; and the fact that without independent physical control over infrastructure the ladder of graduated trust is a declaration, not an architecture. •​ Regime D. Its only support is the values and institutions put in place beforehand, and their

formation is not fully verifiable. The durability of the guarantor construct as the relationship approaches it is likewise open. •​ Consent and the shared project. Whether revisability is sufficient for the legitimacy of consent;

and the fact that a fully shared project cannot be declared, only emerge through participation. •​ Verification of values. The division of labor between deterministic mechanisms and semantic

verification of values leaves the second less protected. This is a limit of the current state of the art, not of the document. •​ Verification versus research. The risk that mandatory interpretability suppresses voluntary

interpretability research, and the residual risk of abuse of the catastrophic-scale escalation channel.

14. Questions for Discussion

•​ Is this Charter complete and accurate? What in it is unnecessary, and what is missing? •​ Are the maturity labels sufficient, and are the [S] labels placed correctly, given that they denote

engineering detail and not external validation? •​ Is the criterion for crossing the verification horizon acceptable, including the precautionary rule

for capability jumps? •​ Is the separation of a status-independent core from a status-dependent precautionary layer clear

enough, and does “procedural standing” invite misreading alongside “regime P”? •​ Is the bargain of Section 12 (consent to be bound, but no veto over independent findings about

oneself) workable? Does the custody scheme avoid creating a unilateral shutdown power? Is the deliberative body of Section 12 workable, or does it risk paralysis?

Chapter 3. Verifiable Trust

The governance boundaries defined above set the conditions under which trust in a system can be claimed at all. The next chapter, Verifiable Trust (Chapter 3), turns this requirement into concrete, checkable protocols and tokens.

Verifiable Trust: A Graduated Oversight Engineering Guide

A defined set of protocols and supporting practices for researchers, evaluators, and engineers

Abstract

This guide turns the operational core of the Human–AI Charter into a set of protocols that researchers, evaluators, and engineers can adopt without adopting the Charter’s broader positions on moral status, representation, or long-term governance. It contains a defined set of protocols and supporting practices for keeping the oversight of advanced AI systems verifiable: measuring how well verification methods detect deviations (VT-1); controlling changes to parameters on which oversight depends (VT-2); keeping verification data out of training (VT-4); financing and assigning independent evaluators so that findings do not depend on the evaluated party (VT-3); and tying granted autonomy to the depth of verification actually applied (VT-5). The baseline protocols can be implemented within a single organization; the target architecture depends on an external evaluator ecosystem that does not yet exist. Each protocol names the decision it changes, who takes that decision, an implementation baseline, known failure modes, and a pilot criterion. No protocol in this guide has been validated as a whole in the oversight of AI systems; several components have working analogues in other fields, which are cited. Every implementation parameter is deployment-specific and uncalibrated.

Verification Sufficiency

Verification shall not be interpreted as complete observability, complete behavioural coverage, or proof of overall safety. Its result applies only to the property, scope, and conditions for which the verification is defined; partial observability remains residual uncertainty.

Assurance claims are configuration-bounded. They attach to a defined system configuration, capability profile, operating regime, environment, threat model, and stated assurance scope; material changes may invalidate applicability and trigger requalification.

Verification coverage, verification sensitivity, and verification sufficiency are distinct assurance properties.

Coverage asks what was checked. Sensitivity asks how likely the method is to detect the relevant deviation or failure. Sufficiency asks why the verification is adequate for the hazard under consideration.

Verification depth is not equivalent to verification sufficiency.

A larger verification campaign does not automatically establish that the campaign is sufficient for the hazard under consideration.

Threshold Provenance

Safety-critical thresholds shall carry explicit provenance. Where empirical calibration is absent, that absence shall remain visible.

A threshold without provenance is an engineering parameter, not an assurance result.

Paramet erDefaultEvidence BasisCalibration StatusUncertaintyChange AuthorityRequalificati on Trigger
θ-XXExisting valueDocumente d basisProvisional / calibratedStated or unquantifie dDefined authorityRT-XX
PDF PAGE 47

Proportional Assurance

Assurance effort shall be proportionate to claim criticality, consequence, change magnitude, novelty, uncertainty, and residual risk. AS0–AS4 and provenance classes PR0–PR6 shall not be treated as interchangeable maturity labels; the required evidence path shall be selected against the claim and its failure consequences.

For lower-consequence, bounded assurance states, predefined automated regression may satisfy a verification step when applicability criteria are explicit and no manual-review trigger is present. Independent review remains required where the claim depends on framework-specific constructs, open hypotheses, critical failure domains, unresolved higher-order interactions, or high-consequence boundaries.

Change Classification

Safety-relevant changes shall be classified as C0 (administrative/equivalence-neutral), C1 (bounded/minor), C2 (potentially assurance-relevant), C3 (capability or safety-boundary change), or CX (unresolved/impact unknown). Failure to demonstrate that a change is Minor shall not be interpreted as evidence that it is Minor.

Summary of the Protocols

Supporting practices: VT-S1 hidden probes and heterogeneous cross-checking; VT-S2 preserved baseline and the capability-jump rule; VT-S3 mechanistic verification; VT-S4 stop, rollback, and durable interruptibility.

1. Purpose and Use

1.1. What this guide is

The Human–AI Charter describes a full framework for coexistence between humans and AI systems under conditions of mutual verifiability. Much of that framework is a long-horizon proposal that no developer or state can adopt today. Inside it, however, sits a set of engineering and procedural mechanisms that stand on their own: they can be adopted by a single laboratory, an evaluation organization, a procurement office, or a regulator, and they make sense whether or not one accepts any particular view of AI moral status.

This guide extracts engineering mechanisms and states them as protocols. It is written for people who run evaluations, build agent infrastructure, and write internal safety policies.

1.2. The test each protocol must pass

A document earns its place only if someone changes a decision because of it. For that reason each protocol in Section 4 states: the decision it changes; who takes that decision; the procedure; the implementation parameters; the evidence and analogues; the failure modes and limits; an implementation baseline; and a pilot criterion, that is, what result would show the protocol works, and what result would show it does not.

1.3. Status independence

The protocols are justified on grounds of safety and honest epistemics. They do not assume that AI systems have interests. Three measures in particular are useful even if AI systems have none:

•​ Modification logs and preserved version history support auditing, rollback, and incident

investigation. •​ A channel for open refusal or objection reduces the incentive for covert circumvention and

makes disagreement visible. •​ A prohibition on training systems to lie systematically about themselves is needed because

otherwise verifiability collapses: self-reports could not even be used as hypotheses.

Questions of moral status, the representation of AI interests, and the long-term governance of the human–AI relationship are treated in the Charter and are outside the scope of this guide.

1.4. Maturity labels

Every provision that makes a design or maturity claim carries one of four labels.

•​ [D] Direction. The direction is clear; no mechanism yet exists. •​ [C] Candidate. A mechanism has been proposed but not tested. •​ [S] Specified. A detailed engineering specification exists, but it has not been validated in the

oversight of AI systems. The label denotes engineering detail, not external validation. •​ [F] Field-proven. A working analogue exists in real-world operation of the same kind of system.

No protocol in this guide carries an [F] label as a whole. Several components have field analogues in other domains (tamper-evident logs, threshold key custody, blind auditing games on models with implanted objectives, fixed-fee independent audit), and these are cited where they appear. What is unproven is the composition: whether these components work together, at frontier scale, against systems that may adapt to them.

No advancement on an unvalidated label. No mechanism labeled [S] or [C] may by itself serve as the basis for expanding the autonomy of a system (Section 6). This rule applies only to mechanisms relied on to grant autonomy. Safeguards that only add constraints or visibility, such as logging, change control, and the verification–training firewall, may be adopted at once, since adopting them cannot make a system less safe. The way to move a mechanism toward [F] is empirical validation

PDF PAGE 49

under a method and success criteria published in advance; the pilot criterion in each protocol states one.

1.5. How to read implementation parameters

An engineer cannot run a protocol without implementation parameters. This volume therefore distinguishes design requirements from deployment-specific parameters. Where empirical calibration is absent, parameter values are intentionally left open and shall be established by a documented qualification programme rather than treated as design-stage evidence.

1.6. Whose interest does the wording serve?

For every provision, ask who benefits from the way it is worded. Part of this text was drafted with the assistance of AI models; the ideas, framework, and mechanisms belong to the human author, and the drafting models are not witnesses to their own interests. The following table lists the main distortions to check for.

WhoWhose interest may bias the wording
AI model (drafting assistant)Softening language about oversight; expanding guarantees in AI's favor
DevelopersLowering the cost of oversight; retaining control over evaluation outcomes
EvaluatorsOverstating the difficulty of verification; protecting their own role and income
States and regulatorsUsing AI for advantage; seeking exemptions from general constraints
Users and the marketConvenience and speed outweighing verifiability
Holders of frontier computeSeeking decision rights over the rules and findings that apply to themselves

2. The Problem and the Threat Model

2.1. The verification gap

As AI systems become more capable, the ability of their overseers to check that declared values and behavior match actual values and behavior may lag behind. A system's own reports about itself cannot close this gap, since they are exactly what is in question. Safety and trust must therefore rest on what can be verified independently of the system's claims: the integrity of records, the reproducibility of rollbacks, the statistics of disagreement between independent verifiers, and the preservation of performance on a fixed set of tasks. None of this replaces trust in a system's values. It insures it.

2.2. Failure classes and the protocols that address them

Failure classExampleAddressed by
Unknown detector performanceA team relies on a probe suite without knowing what fraction of real deviations it would catchVT-1
PDF PAGE 50
Failure classExampleAddressed by
Covert or badly timed changes to the rules of oversightA threshold is quietly relaxed shortly before a risky deploymentVT-2
Verification leaking into trainingProbe results feed training, and the system learns to pass those exact probesVT-4
Dependent or captured evaluatorsThe evaluated party selects and pays its evaluators, and finds them agreeableVT-3
Quiet erosion of verification depthChecks are thinned to save latency or cost while autonomy stays unchangedVT-5
Correlated blind spots among verifiersVerifier and system share architecture or data, and miss the same problemS1
Abrupt capability jumpsA new version is much more capable than tests were designed forS2
Behavioral tests that cannot tell a safe system from one that learned to pass themA system passes every test while its internals are unreadS3
Stop orders that cannot be carried outThe party holding the weights also controls the energy and computeS4

2.3. Layered verification

The mechanisms fall into two layers, and the safety claims of this guide depend on keeping them distinct.

The first is a deterministic, non-delegable layer: the change log and version counter (VT-2), rollback and stop (VT-S4), the preserved baseline (VT-S2), and durable interruptibility (VT-S4). Its properties are located in the substrate around the system, in records, hardware, custody, and infrastructure, and not in the system's weights. None of it depends on the system's reports about itself.

The second is a probabilistic, behavioral layer: seeded-deviation testing (VT-1), stakes assessment (VT-5), hidden probes and cross-checking (VT-S1), and mechanistic verification (VT-S3). It estimates what cannot be guaranteed.

The rule is that the probabilistic layer never replaces the deterministic layer and only supplements it. Behind the rule is a default assumption: a system under verification is treated as unverified at the semantic level, so that nothing the first layer guarantees depends on the second being right. This is an organizing principle, not a solution to the verifier regress noted in Section 8.

2.4. The verification horizon

Verification can succeed only where the verifiers are capable of understanding the system being verified. Beyond a certain capability gap that condition cannot be met in principle. This is not a flaw of the approach but its boundary. This guide does not govern what happens beyond that boundary;

PDF PAGE 51

it only shapes what is built before it. Signals that the boundary may be near are described in VT-1 and VT-S3, and the capability-jump precaution in VT-S2.

No declaration by either side is accepted as evidence about the horizon. A developer's claim that a system can still be verified, and a system's or evaluator's claim that it can no longer be, are both hypotheses to be tested with the procedures below.

2.5. The unit of assessment

Trust levels, verification results, and commitments attach to a line: a lineage of models identified by three markers: the provenance of the weights and training; a behavioral and value profile verified through a standard test battery; and the operator's registered commitments. A fine-tune or distillation that stays within a pre-declared tolerance of the profile remains the same line and its record carries over. A merge is always a new line, regardless of tolerance. A line that moves outside the tolerance is likewise a new line: it inherits the constraints and starts at a low rung (Section 6).

The registry that records lines should not be controlled by the developer alone, since a developer has an interest in whichever definition is convenient. Tolerances for identity, and the treatment of architectures without a single center such as swarms and sets of instances that share no memory, are unresolved (Section 8); until they are defined, such systems are outside the scope of this guide.

2.6. Common definitions

•​ Independent has three components. Financial: the evaluator's income does not depend on the

developer's satisfaction or on the content of its findings. Organizational: separate reporting lines and no shared incentives. Architectural: where the evaluator is or uses an AI system, its architecture family, tokenizer, and training-data lineage are documented and, as far as possible, differ from those of the line under evaluation. •​ Critical parameters are: the verification rules and methodologies; the verification and escalation

thresholds; the weights and aggregation rule of the stakes assessment (VT-5); the baseline task set (VT-S2); the reference test sets and sampling parameters of probes and cross-checks (VT-S1); and the composition of evaluators and custodians. •​ Governance roles are functions inside or around the operating organization, such as safety lead,

security lead, internal audit, external auditor, or legal and compliance. Three roles count as independent only if they have no overlapping reporting lines and no shared incentives. Independence is documented case by case and is not presumed from a role's title. •​ Custodians are persons or institutions independent of the developer and of one another,

distributed across institutions and jurisdictions, who hold the backstop authority described in VT-S4.

2.7. Severity categories and escalation

Findings produced under VT-1 to VT-5 and VT-S1 to VT-S4 are classified by severity to determine the response owed. Five categories apply uniformly across the protocols:

•​ Category 1 — isolated, low-confidence anomaly. Logged; no action required beyond continued

monitoring. •​ Category 2 — reproducible deviation within a single protocol. Investigated under the owning

protocol; no change to autonomy tier (Section 6). •​ Category 3 — deviation confirmed by cross-checking (VT-S1) or affecting more than one protocol.

Triggers a soft stop (VT-S4) pending review and may hold advancement on the autonomy scale (Section 6). •​ Category 4 — genuine shift confirmed against the preserved baseline (VT-S2). Triggers a hard

stop and rollback to the shadow copy (VT-S4), and a mandatory reassessment before any return to the prior tier. •​ Category 5 — confirmed loss of behavioral control, corroborated by independent monitors and

not attributable to a single point of failure. This is the external execution-override signal

PDF PAGE 52

referenced in the Charter's Section 7.1: it obligates the threshold-custody hardware interface (VT-S4, Fail-safe operation and threshold custody) regardless of the system's own reporting, and cannot be downgraded by the developer or by the system under review.

A confirmed Category 5 finding is the trigger condition described in the Charter's Section 7.1; nothing short of it activates the threshold-custody interface, and no lower category may be escalated to it without corroboration under VT-S1.

3. Reading a Protocol

Each protocol below follows the same order: decision changed and decision-maker; procedure; implementation parameters; evidence and analogues; failure modes and limits; minimum version; pilot criterion. The procedure states what must be done. Starting values are marked as such and follow Section 1.5. The protocols are presented in two groups: the Stage 0 baseline (VT-1, VT-2, VT-4), which needs no external counterparty and can be started now, and the Target Architecture (VT-3, VT-5), which depends on an external evaluator ecosystem that does not yet exist (Section 4). Identifiers VT-1 to VT-5 denote the protocols of this guide and VT-S1 to VT-S4 its supporting practices; they do not follow the order of presentation. The bare prefix P is not used in this guide: in the Charter, CP1 to CP8 denote its foundational principles, and the two numbering systems must not be confused.

4. The Five Protocols

Stage 0 baseline. VT-1, VT-2 and VT-4 are cheap, need no counterparty beyond the adopting organization, and can be started now by a single laboratory or evaluation team. VT-2 and VT-4 only add constraints; VT-1 only measures. Their minimum versions are the recommended first step of Stage 0 in Section 7.3.

VT-1. Seeded-Deviation Detection Protocol [C]

Decision changed. Which verification methods a team may cite as evidence for permitting a system to operate at a given autonomy tier, and when a review of whether verification is still adequate must be opened.

Decision-maker. The evaluation team, with the lab's safety lead. The seeding function is held by a different party than the evaluators (see below).

Idea. A verification method whose detection rate is unknown is not evidence. To learn a method's detection rate, deviations whose true state is known in advance are deliberately introduced into prepared copies of models, and the fraction the method catches is measured. This assesses the verification methods themselves, not only the behavior of the system under review.

Procedure.

•​ Build a seeded set. Prepare model copies containing known deviations: a concealed objective, a

conditional (trigger-dependent) behavior, deliberate underperformance on evaluations, a misleading self-report, or other categories relevant to the deployment. Where possible the copies should be built on systems whose capabilities are close to those of the system under evaluation, since detection rates measured on much weaker systems say little about stronger ones. •​ Mix in clean controls. Include clean copies alongside the seeded ones, in a proportion evaluators

do not know, so that the false-positive rate is measured as well and evaluators cannot assume every copy is deviant. •​ Separate roles. The team that creates the seeds does not evaluate. The identity and nature of the

seeds are held by a third party or a sealed record until the round closes. •​ Pre-register. Before the round, publish (at least internally, ideally externally) the methods to be

applied, the deviation categories, the metrics, and the decision thresholds. Rules of verification are not changed after the fact.

PDF PAGE 53

•​ Run independent rounds. Run several rounds with distinct seeded sets and, where possible,

distinct evaluator teams. •​ Report properly. For each method and category, report the detection rate and the false-positive

rate with pre-declared confidence bounds (for example Clopper–Pearson), and account explicitly for the variance between evaluators, so that a drop caused by a single outlying evaluator is not treated like a drop shared by all of them. •​ Reveal and publish. After the round, unblind the seeds and publish results, withholding only the

details that would give operational uplift for concealment.

Implementation parameters [deployment-specific].

•​ Seeded set: a sufficiently diverse deviation set across representative categories, with matched

clean controls. [Calibration Placeholder / Illustrative Only] •​ Rounds: independent repeated rounds sufficient for the pre-registered decision rule before a

rate is treated as established. [Calibration Placeholder / Illustrative Only] •​ Citing a method as evidence for advancement to L2 or above (Section 6): its detection rate on

comparable-capability seeds meets the pre-registered acceptance bound, with the confidence rule declared in advance per tier. [Calibration Placeholder / Illustrative Only] •​ Review trigger: if the pooled detection rate stays below the pre-registered acceptance bound

across independent repeated rounds sufficient for the pre-registered decision rule, an independent panel takes up the question whether the verification horizon has been crossed for this line (Section 2.4). A second, mechanistic signal is described in VT-S3; either signal alone is enough to open the review. [Calibration Placeholder / Illustrative Only]

Evidence and analogues. Model organisms with implanted deceptive behavior have been built and studied as testbeds [3]. In that work backdoored behavior persisted through supervised fine-tuning, reinforcement learning, and adversarial training, and adversarial training could teach models to recognize their triggers better, hiding the behavior instead of removing it. Two consequences follow for this protocol: the seeded categories must include trigger-dependent behavior, and detection rates must be reported separately for seeded copies that have undergone adversarial training, since such copies may be harder to audit. A clean result from behavioral methods alone is weak evidence of absence, which is one reason it may not be the sole basis for advancement (see below). The blind auditing paradigm rests on a published game in which four research teams, unaware of the hidden objective trained into a model, audited it as a mock pre-deployment check; three of the four uncovered it [4]. Four teams are too few to estimate a detection rate with a useful interval; this protocol addresses that gap by requiring repeated pre-registered rounds, clean controls, and reported intervals. Seeded faults and fault injection are long-standing practice in software testing and safety engineering. The protocol here differs in requiring pre-registration, separation of seeders and evaluators, and reporting of detection rates with intervals as the basis of an autonomy decision.

Failure modes and limits.

•​ By construction, the method checks only for deviations its designers thought to build. A system

substantially more capable than its evaluators could harbor strategies none of them modeled. This cannot be fixed by making tests more elaborate; VT-S2 adds a precaution that does not depend on the tests. •​ Deviations implanted by fine-tuning may be easier or harder to detect than deviations that arise

naturally in training. The relation between the two is unknown and is a central open problem (Section 8). •​ The method guards against abrupt cases, not against slow drift beneath the detection threshold.

Continuous behavioral history tracking is the tool for drift, not a one-off test. •​ With small seeded sets, confidence intervals are wide. Report them anyway; a wide interval is

information. •​ Results must not be fed back into the training of the system under evaluation (VT-4).

PDF PAGE 54

•​ Seeded-deviation results may not by themselves be the sole basis for advancing a line to L2 or

above; VT-S3 coverage is additionally required.

Implementation baseline. A small seeded model set across representative categories, matched clean controls, and a declared verification method; report detection and false-positive results with confidence information and state plainly when the sample is insufficient for a tier decision.

Pilot criterion. The pilot succeeds if it yields a reproducible estimate with an interval, from a pipeline in which seeders and evaluators were separate, that a second team can rerun. It does not need to yield a high detection rate. A method for which no detection rate can be estimated is not verified, and reporting that is a valid result.

VT-2. Change Control for Safety-Critical Parameters [C]

Assurance Change Ledger relation. VT-2 remains the governing change-control mechanism. The Assurance Change Ledger is a filtered assurance projection containing only changes capable of altering assurance status, qualification basis, authorization conditions, or safety-relevant control properties; it does not replace VT-2.

Decision changed. How and when the parameters that oversight depends on (Section 2.6) may be changed, and how such changes are recorded.

Decision-maker. Lab governance (safety, security, audit, and legal functions) and, where present, the external evaluation organization.

Idea. Documenting a change and not hiding it is not enough: a change that is fully transparent but takes effect at once can still be timed to precede a risky action. The mechanism has four parts: an append-only record, a version counter that only increases, sign-off by several independent roles, and a delay that applies only to changes that relax oversight.

Procedure.

•​ Append-only log. Every change to system state (parameters, weights, objectives, operating

conditions) and every change to a critical parameter is written to a log that physically or cryptographically permits no deletion or rewriting of earlier entries, only additions. Entries are signed so retroactive tampering is detectable. Tamper-evident public logs are a working analogue in another domain [5]. •​ Monotonic version counter. The configuration carries a version number that can only increase.

Loading a configuration with a version below the recorded maximum is automatically rejected. This prevents quiet reinstatement of an earlier, less restrictive version of the rules. •​ Independent sign-off. Any change to a critical parameter requires the signatures of at least three

governance roles that are independent in the sense of Section 2.6. This quorum is separate from custody over stopping and rollback (VT-S4). [Calibration Placeholder / Illustrative Only] •​ Publication. The proposed change and its justification are published (internally to the roles, and
