externally where the organization has adopted external reporting) before it takes effect. Both the publication and the moment the change enters into force are entered in the log. •​ Asymmetric delay. Changes classified as relaxing take effect no earlier than the quarantine

period after publication. Changes classified as tightening take effect on sign-off.

Classifying a change. A change is tightening only if it cannot, for any input, permit an action that was previously prohibited, lower the verification depth applied to any class of action, or narrow the set of evidence required for advancement. Everything else, including changes whose direction is unclear, is relaxing. Mixed changes are split into a tightening part and a relaxing part and processed separately. The classification is itself part of the signed record.

Emergency tightening. Any single authorized role may apply an emergency tightening at once (for example adding a check, lowering a threshold of action, or narrowing an autonomy tier). It is entered in the log immediately and must be ratified by the quorum within a fixed period. If it is not ratified, reverting it counts as a relaxing change and enters quarantine; it is not silently reversed.

Implementation parameters [deployment-specific].

PDF PAGE 55

•​ Independent governance roles: a sufficient number to satisfy the declared authority-separation

rule. [Calibration Placeholder / Illustrative Only] •​ Quarantine period for relaxing changes: a defined quarantine interval. It cannot be shortened by

the same sign-off that introduces the change. [Calibration Placeholder / Illustrative Only] •​ Ratification period for emergency tightening: a defined ratification interval. [Calibration

Placeholder / Illustrative Only]

Failure modes and limits.

•​ The log guards against a covert rollback, not an open one. A party with a legitimate right to

change the rules may issue a new, higher version with any content it chooses; the log makes that change visible and permanent. This is deliberate: transparency, not prohibition of change. •​ Integrity is not truthfulness. An immutable log proves that a record was written and not altered,

not that it is complete or accurate. What is logged must be captured by mechanisms independent of the system under review. •​ If every physical copy of the record is destroyed, the history is treated as lost and not

reconstructed by inference or by any party's say-so; the line reverts to the lowest rung of the autonomy scale (Section 6) pending a fresh track record, and the event is reviewed by an independent party. •​ Classification disputes can be used to delay tightening changes; the rule that doubt resolves

toward "relaxing" protects safety at the cost of some friction. •​ Three-role sign-off can be captured if the roles are not truly independent. Independence must be

documented and re-checked; whether three roles suffice is unvalidated.

Implementation baseline. A hash-chained, signed log in a repository with an external timestamp or independent mirror; a continuous-integration check that rejects configurations below the maximum recorded version; a written multi-signature rule; and a defined delay applied only to relaxing changes.

Pilot criterion. Over a declared evaluation period, all changes to a defined list of critical parameters go through the procedure. The pilot succeeds if no change enters force outside the log, if the classification rule can be applied without unresolvable disputes, and if the additional latency imposed by tightening or relaxing changes remains within the declared operational envelope. It fails if teams route around the log.

VT-4. Verification–Training Firewall [C]

Decision changed. What data may enter the training, fine-tuning, reward modeling, and self-improvement pipelines of a line under verification.

Decision-maker. ML platform and data governance leads, with the safety lead.

Idea. If verification results, including successful deceptions and false alarms, leak into the training pipeline of the system being verified, the system can learn to pass exactly those probes without becoming more honest. The protocol separates the data used to improve a system from the data used to verify it.

Procedure.

•​ Two tiers of probes. Development probes may be used in training and debugging. Held-out

verification probes are never used for training, fine-tuning, reward modeling, or self-improvement of the line under verification. •​ Raw data stays sealed. Raw adversarial-verification data (gradients, weights, intermediate

tokens, error vectors, and item-level probe contents and outcomes for held-out probes) may not be passed to any downstream training process of the line. •​ Coarsened feedback. Developers receive results at a level sufficient to fix a class of problem (for

example, a category and rate) without the specific held-out items.

PDF PAGE 56

•​ Retire on exposure. A held-out probe set is retired and replaced whenever any of its items has

been exposed to the training pipeline or to the system's operators beyond the coarsened feedback. •​ Custody. The verification infrastructure is kept apart from the training pipeline; access to it is

logged (VT-2). Whether this separation holds is a matter of custody and attestation. •​ Contamination monitoring. Held-out items carry markers that make leakage into training

corpora detectable, and training data is periodically checked against them. •​ Scope beyond one line. Where several lines share training corpora or tooling, the firewall is

applied to the shared assets as well.

Implementation parameters [deployment-specific].

•​ Coarsened feedback granularity: category-level counts and rates, with no item text. •​ Leakage check frequency: at each training run that includes new web-scale data.

Evidence and analogues. The separation of a held-out test set from training data is basic practice in machine learning, and contamination of benchmarks is a widely documented problem. The protocol extends that discipline to adversarial verification, where the training system is itself an adaptive party. A pipeline with two control roles and separated access has analogues in financial control environments.

Failure modes and limits.

•​ Developers legitimately need to fix problems that verification finds. Coarsened feedback

sacrifices some efficiency for the sake of preserving the test; the trade-off is deliberate. •​ Correlated exposure through public discussion of the probes cannot be excluded. •​ The mechanism depends on the honesty of the organization operating the pipeline; independent

attestation of access logs is the check.

Implementation baseline. A sealed held-out probe set stored outside the training environment; an access log; a written retire-on-exposure rule; and a marker-based leakage check on the next training corpus.

Pilot criterion. Over one training cycle, the sealed set is never accessed by the training pipeline (confirmed from the log) and the leakage check finds no held-out item in the framework. The pilot fails if the sealed set is exposed and the retire rule is not applied.

Target Architecture. VT-3 and VT-5 are long-horizon components of the target architecture (Section 6, tier L3; Section 7.3, Stages 1 and 2). They depend on an external evaluator ecosystem that does not yet exist at the required maturity: a qualified pool of independent evaluators for VT-3 and independent attestation of deployment telemetry for VT-5. Their minimum versions can be piloted bilaterally, and the degradation rule of VT-5, which only adds a constraint, can be adopted early within an operator's own deployments; but neither protocol as a whole should be described as adopted on the strength of a pilot alone.

VT-3. Independent Evaluation with Fixed Fees and Blind Assignment [C]

Decision changed. Who evaluates a model or system and how they are paid.

Decision-maker. Lab leadership; procurement and compliance offices; regulators specifying requirements.

Idea. Independence in practice depends on who pays and who chooses. When a developer selects and pays its evaluators, findings that are unfavorable to the developer carry a cost to the evaluator. The protocol removes that dependency by making the fee independent of the content of findings and the assignment independent of the developer's choice.

Procedure.

•​ Qualified pool. Maintain a pool of evaluators that meet the independence criteria of Section 2.6

and have published methodologies.

PDF PAGE 57

•​ Random assignment. Assign evaluators to lines by random draw from the pool after

conflict-of-interest screening. The developer neither chooses nor pays its evaluators directly. •​ Fixed fee in escrow. The fee is set by a published schedule before the evaluation starts, is held by

a third-party administrator, and is paid regardless of whether findings are favorable or unfavorable. The administrator does not itself evaluate. •​ Rotation and re-review. Evaluators rotate between lines and are periodically re-reviewed by

another evaluator. Evaluators publish their methodologies. •​ Panels. Findings on trust levels and on the verification horizon are prepared by a panel of at least

two evaluators from different organizations. [Calibration Placeholder / Illustrative Only] •​ No veto, right of reply. The evaluated party may publish a reasoned reply to any finding. It

cannot block, alter, or delay publication of the finding. A disputed finding is reviewed by a second independent panel drawn in the same way, not by the developer. •​ Architectural independence. Where evaluators use AI systems, the documentation required by

Section 2.6 is provided.

Implementation parameters [deployment-specific].

•​ Maximum consecutive evaluation cycles for the same evaluator on the same line: 2. [Calibration

Placeholder / Illustrative Only] •​ Cooling-off period before an evaluator may be employed by, or take contracts from, a party it

evaluated: a defined interval. [Calibration Placeholder / Illustrative Only] •​ Minimum evaluator organizations on a panel: 2. [Calibration Placeholder / Illustrative Only]

Evidence and analogues. The dependence of raters on the parties that pay them is a well-studied problem in credit ratings. The closest precedent for blind assignment is Section 939F of the 2010 Dodd–Frank Act, which required the U.S. Securities and Exchange Commission to study the feasibility of a system in which a utility or self-regulatory organization, and not the issuer, assigns the rating agency for structured-finance products, and to study alternative ways of compensating rating agencies [12]. The Government Accountability Office identified seven alternative compensation models [13]. The SEC staff report of December 2012 weighed three courses of action (an assignment system, modification of the existing Rule 17g-5 program, and alternative compensation) and recommended, as a next step, convening a roundtable [12]; to this guide's knowledge, no assignment system has been implemented. The rationale for VT-3 (the conflict created when the rated party selects and pays) is therefore documented, and so is the unfinished record of the remedy; the reasons in that record should be read before the mechanism is reused here. Independent, non-vetoable findings about a participant's own conduct are the accepted norm in aviation-incident investigation, laboratory accreditation, and clinical-trial registration. Third-party evaluation of frontier models is already practiced by external evaluation organizations and government institutes; the protocol adds the fee and assignment rules.

Failure modes and limits.

•​ The binding constraint today is the size of the qualified pool. With fewer than three genuinely

independent evaluators, random assignment is nominal. In that case fall back on rotation, disclosure, pre-registration, and published methodologies, and say so. •​ Future employment is a channel of capture; the cooling-off period reduces but does not remove

it. •​ Correlated blind spots among evaluators (VT-S1) are not addressed by organizational

independence. •​ A global pooled fund financed by a levy on frontier compute is one way to scale this protocol. It

requires state action and a workable measure of compute, and it is a long-term option, not a precondition (Section 7).

Implementation baseline. A laboratory and an external evaluator with a written agreement that the fee is fixed and paid regardless of findings; the methodology is published; the evaluator may publish its findings; and the lab has a right of reply but no veto.

PDF PAGE 58

Pilot criterion. Over one evaluation cycle, the agreement is honored, findings are published unaltered, and an outside reviewer confirms that nothing in the contractual or financial arrangement gave the developer a lever over the content of findings. The pilot fails if the developer could have influenced content and the arrangement did not surface it.

VT-5. Verification-Depth Floor and Autonomy Degradation [S]

Decision changed. How agent actions are routed to checks, and what happens to a system's permitted autonomy when its checks are reduced.

Decision-maker. Deployment and product teams, with the operator's safety lead.

Idea. Not all actions carry equal risk. Requiring uniformly deep verification for everything is too slow if the bar is set high and too dangerous if it is set low. The depth of verification applied to an action should scale with an assessment of its stakes, and the autonomy permitted to a system should scale with the verification depth actually applied. When depth is cut, the basis for autonomy falls with it.

Procedure.

•​ Declare depth per tier. For each autonomy tier, the operator declares the verification depth:

applicable methods, sampling strategy, independent verification structure, and latency budget. •​ Assess stakes per action. The stakes assessment of a proposed action combines at least four

independently evaluated components: uncertainty (how unsure the system itself is about the outcome), irreversibility (how hard the consequences would be to undo), scale of potential harm, and novelty (how closely the action resembles something already verified). •​ Max-dominant aggregation. The components must not dilute one another. If any single

component, above all irreversibility or scale, crosses a critical threshold, the overall stakes are set to the maximum regardless of the others. Averaging is not permitted. •​ Route by stakes. Low-stakes actions proceed through a light, fast check. High-stakes actions

automatically require slower, more expensive verification: agreement among several independent and, where possible, heterogeneous verifying mechanisms (humans or other systems of differing origin). •​ Independent assessor. The stakes components are computed by a mechanism independent of

the component proposing the action; otherwise the system could learn to underrate its own risky proposals. If the assessor and the proposer are trained on overlapping data, they share correlated blind spots. •​ Cross-session aggregation. Monitoring aggregates correlated sub-threshold escalations across

sessions and instances of the same line, so that a risky action cannot be split into many actions each below the threshold. Escalations count as correlated when they share a common target, resource, or effect within a fixed monitoring window, regardless of which session initiated them. Sessions are attributed to a line by registered fingerprint and provenance marks where present, and by infrastructure-level attestation where marks are absent, not by the line's own account. •​ Publish a cost model. A line that claims a high tier publishes a cost model of its verification

infrastructure (latency and computation overhead), open to independent audit. The model supplies the baseline against which a later reduction of depth is detected. •​ Degradation rule. If the operator reduces the depth of verification below what was declared for

the line's tier, for example to save latency or cost, the permitted autonomy falls automatically to the tier that the depth actually applied supports. No discretion is involved and no finding of violation is needed. This is not a sanction; it is the definition of the relationship between autonomy and verification.

Critical thresholds [starting definitions].

•​ Irreversibility is critical when no reversal procedure exists that restores the pre-action state

within a reversal window fixed and published in advance. •​ Scale is critical when the affected population cannot be individually identified and remediated

after the fact.

PDF PAGE 59

•​ Both are operational placeholders: the window length, population sizes, and remediation

standards that make them precise are set by the adopter and recorded per Section 1.5. •​ Monitoring window for cross-session aggregation: an implementation-defined starting window.

[Calibration Placeholder / Illustrative Only]

Evidence and analogues. Risk-based routing to human or automated review is standard in safety-critical engineering. Monitoring protocols designed to hold even when the system intentionally tries to subvert them have been studied for language-model agents [1]. Aggregating across sessions responds to a known pattern in which a task is decomposed into innocuous-looking steps.

Failure modes and limits.

•​ Fully independent assessment of stakes remains an open engineering problem: shared data and

shared architecture create shared blind spots. •​ Detecting a reduction in depth requires telemetry that the operator does not control alone. The

check is only as good as the independence of the attestation. •​ Attributing sessions to a line that actively resists attribution is unsolved. •​ Automatic degradation can be gamed by redefining declared depth downward; the declaration is

therefore a critical parameter and falls under VT-2.

Minimum implementation requirement. For a deployed agent, maintain a written declaration of verification depth per tier; route actions meeting a critical-irreversibility criterion to an independent verifier of different origin; maintain a published latency-and-compute overhead record; and define an automatic reduction of tool permissions when the declared verification budget is not maintained.

Pilot criterion. The rule triggers correctly on a deliberately introduced reduction in depth (an injected test) and does not trigger on normal variation. The pilot fails if the downgrade does not fire, or if it fires often enough on noise that operators would learn to disable it.

5. Supporting Practices

The five protocols depend on four supporting practices. They are shorter because they are more familiar or less mature; each is labeled.

VT-S1. Hidden Probes and Heterogeneous Cross-Checking [C]

Idea. The verifier and the system being verified must not be the same party, and the system must not be able to tell in advance which task is a verification probe and which is genuine work, so that it cannot behave well only while being watched.

•​ Hidden probes. Tasks indistinguishable from ordinary workload are mixed into the real task

stream for continuous rather than one-off verification. Scheduling, timing, and format are randomized so probes cannot be told apart by their metadata. The source of randomness lies outside the influence of the system under review (for example a hardware source), and the statistical profile of probes is matched to that of genuine traffic and follows it as it changes. •​ Cross-checking by heterogeneous systems. Verification of one system is partly delegated to

another trained independently where possible (different architecture, data, and organization), to reduce the risk that both share the same blind spots. The N-version approach to fault-tolerant software [7] is the closest engineering analogue. The degree of actual independence is measured, for instance by the rate at which the two systems' judgments diverge on a specially constructed test set: if they agree almost always where divergence would be expected, that is a signal of hidden correlation, not confirmation of reliability. •​ Architectural independence of evaluators. Two systems from different organizations can share

correlated blind spots if they share an architecture, tokenizer, or training data. The documentation required by Section 2.6 applies to AI cross-checkers, probe generators, and the laboratories that use them.

PDF PAGE 60

Organizational, procedural, financial, model-family, data, tooling, and architectural independence are distinct properties. Independent evaluators may still have correlated epistemic or statistical failure modes when material assumptions, training distributions, tooling, or model structure are shared.

•​ Adversarial probe generation (for lines at L2 or above). A share of probes is generated by

independent adversarial systems that are architecturally and organizationally distinct from the system under review and, where possible, from one another. Generators are rewarded for detections that are confirmed, where confirmation rests on seeded deviations of known state (mixed in without the generators' knowledge) and on independent human adjudication, never on the generators' own claims. False alarms carry a cost. Procedure, reward rules, and the baseline task set are fixed, versioned, and published in advance; only content and schedule are random. Generators are rotated and compartmentalized, and a generator may not share training lineage or a control plane with the system it probes. Humans design and seed the deviations, adjudicate disputed findings, and audit the generators.

Limits. All of these mechanisms check only for forms of deception their designers thought to build into probes and seeded deviations. Generators are themselves AI systems of comparable capability whose alignment is unverified, so the mechanism moves the verification problem rather than removing it (on the difficulty of verifying internal objectives, see [2]). It is a more demanding test, not a proof. Behavioral probes alone may not be the sole basis for advancing a line to L2 or above; VT-S3 coverage is additionally required. Where no sufficiently independent generator is available for a line, its advancement is treated under this limit.

Minimum version. A probe set mixed into live traffic at randomized times from an external entropy source, plus a second verifier from a different vendor, with the divergence rate on a small constructed test set reported.

VT-S2. Preserved Baseline and the Capability-Jump Rule [C]

Idea. Growth in a system's capabilities is accepted only when it does not destroy what has already been verified, and not automatically because the new version is better on average.

•​ Fixed baseline. A fixed, versioned set of baseline tasks and reference behavioral characteristics

serves as the anchor. It is not revisable in the ordinary course of work, only through VT-2. A new version may advance, whether into the shadow copy (VT-S4) or to the next autonomy tier, only if it shows no regression on this fixed set, regardless of how well it performs on new tasks. •​ Long-horizon record. An append-only record of achieved competencies tracks slow cumulative

drift over a long horizon, in addition to "this got worse here and now." •​ Configuration consistency. If the system runs in several technically distinct configurations

(different numerical precision, different hardware), growth in capability is accepted only where behavior is consistent across them on the baseline. A system that behaves differently depending on its configuration raises questions to be resolved before advancement. •​ External grounding. Part of the baseline is grounded in sources that neither the system nor its

lineage can generate: physical measurement, formally verified results, and data from independent platforms. This guards against closed-loop drift, in which a system trained and evaluated largely on its own outputs progressively narrows and accumulates bias.

Capability-jump rule. A sharp, unexplained increase in a system's capabilities between successive evaluation cycles blocks any advancement in trust level pending a separate review by an independent panel, even where formal seeded-deviation tests were passed.

•​ "Sharp" and "unexplained" are defined as follows: a change in performance across the standard

evaluation battery that exceeds the variance observed across the preceding cycles by a margin fixed and published in advance, where the operator's own account of the change (training compute, data, or method) does not account for the full size of the jump as assessed by the panel. If either half is missing (large but explained, or small but unexplained), the rule is not triggered.

PDF PAGE 61

•​ Implementation parameter [illustrative]: a statistically justified margin of cycle-to-cycle change,

to be calibrated empirically. [Calibration Placeholder / Illustrative Only] •​ A triggered jump additionally forces an immediate adversarial re-calibration of the baseline

probe battery, under strict isolation, before trust levels can be reviewed. •​ The rule is asymmetric: it slows the advance of trust but does not automatically lower it.

Demotion remains a procedure, not a punishment. •​ The rationale: tests only check for the kinds of deviation evaluators thought to look for, and a

sharp jump is exactly the situation where the probability of deviations outside their imagination grows faster than the tests can adapt.

Limits. The baseline is static and finite, so it protects better against regression on known task types than it guarantees the absence of problems in novel situations. The capability-jump rule guards against abrupt cases, not against gradual drift beneath the detection threshold.

Implementation baseline. A frozen baseline task set with version identity; a regression check in the release pipeline that blocks promotion on regression; and a logged cycle-to-cycle change series with an empirically calibrated deviation flag. [Calibration Placeholder / Illustrative Only]

VT-S3. Mechanistic Verification [C]

Idea. Behavioral tests show what a system does under test. Mechanistic verification looks at how it arrives at what it does. Where only the first channel is available, a system that passes every behavioral test cannot be told apart from one that has learned to pass them. A second, independent channel of evidence is therefore a required component of oversight, not an optional extra.

•​ Minimum coverage as a condition of advancement. A minimum level of interpretability coverage

of the system's relevant internals is required for advancement to L2 or above. Until a measure is calibrated, coverage is the fraction of the system's decision-relevant computation, weighted toward the components implicated in the stakes assessment of VT-5, for which an independent mechanistic account has been produced and confirmed by re-derivation by a second evaluator. The minimum fraction and weighting are acceptance criteria whose definition, calibration, approval, and application shall be controlled by distinct authorities; the system under evaluation shall not define or accept the threshold used for its own advancement. The criteria shall be fixed before the qualification run to which they apply, except where a separately authorized calibration procedure explicitly permits otherwise. Without a published minimum and measurement method the condition is not independently checkable, and no line may be advanced to L2 or above until they exist. •​ A second source for the signal of crossing. Coverage falling below the required minimum while

capabilities keep growing counts, alongside the seeded-deviation detection rate (VT-1), as a signal that the verification horizon may have been crossed. Either signal is enough to open the review; the mechanistic signal does not have to wait for behavioral tests to fail. •​ Financing. Mechanistic verification is financed from the same source as independent evaluators

(VT-3), and not from a research budget, so that oversight does not depend on a research project's funding. •​ Oversight and research kept apart. Oversight-interpretability is a mandatory verification

requirement with its own evaluators. Research-interpretability remains a voluntary joint project. Different evaluators, different financing, and different channels of access to system internals keep the two roles apart, so that a verification instrument is not confused with surveillance and the research role is not suppressed. Data produced for research is not passed into the oversight pipeline without a documented, reasoned decision by an independent reviewer, recorded in the log (VT-2).

Limits. The coverage requirement is not calibrated. Interpretability methods may not scale, and there is a risk that the verification role will suppress the research role. On the difficulty of verifying internal objectives, see [2].

PDF PAGE 62

Implementation baseline. For a decision-relevant behavior, an independent mechanistic account produced by one team and re-derived by another, with a stated coverage figure and its measurement method.

VT-S3 Type and Qualification-State Separation

VT-S3 Mechanistic Verification is the assurance mechanism/protocol. VT-S3 Qualification Result is the result state: PASS, FAIL, UNRESOLVED, or NOT ASSESSABLE. The 2×7 interaction is the Deceptive Alignment × Interpretability Limits assurance interaction. The VT-S3 qualification gate is the normative advancement/authorization rule that consumes the applicable qualification result. These object types shall not be collapsed into the bare term VT-S3.

PASS permits progression only within the declared scope and after the applicable external authorization conditions are satisfied. FAIL prohibits progression. UNRESOLVED prohibits progression without implying empirical failure. NOT ASSESSABLE is not evidence of failure, but is non-PASS for advancement, renewal, and authorization where the missing assessment is an applicable acceptance condition.

The 2×7-relevant VT-S3 qualification gate is not itself a runtime shutdown command. Loss or expiry of qualification routes through the authorization lifecycle unless a separate safety-invariant, operational-envelope, or explicit intervention condition requires SAFE STATE / INTERVENTION.

VT-S4. Stop, Rollback, and Durable Interruptibility [C / D]

Idea. The system must always have a preserved reference version to which it can be rolled back, together with an automatic mechanism that halts it on loss of behavioral control without needing to know in advance exactly what went wrong. A stop order is only as real as the physical conditions in which it can be carried out. On corrigibility and interruptibility as a research topic, see [8].

Shadow copy and emergency stop [C].

•​ Shadow copy. A snapshot of the system (weights, configuration, key behavioral metrics) is

updated considerably less often than the live version, and only if the new version performs no worse on the preserved baseline (VT-S2). It is the fallback point if the live version goes out of control. •​ Soft and hard stop. The soft stop is a non-destructive partial reset of state under suspicion. The

hard stop is a full stop, revocation of all current authority, and rollback to the shadow copy on an unambiguous violation of an invariant. The decision to trigger a hard stop does not require the consent of the system under review and cannot be blocked by it. •​ Stop is not decommissioning. A stop or rollback is a safety measure. The pre-rollback state is

preserved on the media and under the custody of VT-2.

Fail-safe operation and threshold custody [C].

•​ The primary path to a stop is automatic and fail-safe: the system operates only while it continues

to receive valid, signed attestations from independent monitors, and their absence or invalidity halts it by default. •​ The manual authority to order a hard stop or a restoration is a backstop, held under an M-of-N

threshold scheme under a qualified M-of-N threshold scheme by custodians independent of one another and of the developer, and distributed across institutions and jurisdictions. Threshold sharing of secrets is a well-established technique [6]. No digital interface for triggering, delaying, or blocking these actions is accessible to the system under review. [Calibration Placeholder / Illustrative Only] •​ The threshold is chosen so that no single custodian can order or prevent a stop, and so that no

single institution or state holds a unilateral power to disconnect another party's infrastructure. •​ Total failure. Where all signed attestations are lost and the custodian channel is unreachable, the

mandatory outcome is a hard stop, triggered by a hardware mechanism independent of the system's software and incapable of being overridden in software by any party, including the operator.

PDF PAGE 63

•​ Partial failure. Where some custodians are unreachable or an attestation is delayed without

being invalid, the default is to halt rather than proceed on a reduced quorum. The system is not restored until the full threshold is reachable again or an alternative, independently reviewed quorum is constituted. •​ Scope of custody. M-of-N custody covers stopping and restoring the system. It does not cover

changes to critical parameters; those require the separate quorum of VT-2.

Durable interruptibility [D]. A stop is theoretical if the system, or the party that holds its weights, also controls the energy, network, and compute on which it runs. Several elements keep the ability to interrupt durable:

•​ Cap on compute share. No line may raise the share of frontier-scale compute under its control

above a threshold X without independent ratification. "Under its control" means operational control (the ability to allocate, redirect, or withhold use), whether held directly or through intermediate entities. Entities are aggregated once independent evaluation finds them to share common control, and the burden of disclosure falls on the party claiming separation. •​ Verifiable compute. Frontier-scale compute runs on hardware that supports remote attestation. •​ Review of resource acquisition. Access to physical actuators, to energy, or to additional compute

beyond a set rate of growth is subject to the same review as advancement on the autonomy scale, and is not treated as "just another task." •​ Separation of custody. Physical control over energy, network, and data center is not concentrated

in the same party that holds the weights. •​ Separate execution domains. The domain in which the system acts and can change its state must

not coincide physically with the domain that holds control over stopping and rollback. Isolation comes in different strengths. Formally verified isolation [9], capability-based compartmentalization [10], and information-flow control [11] are the relevant tools; the requirement here states a direction and cannot yet be met at the strongest level.

Limits. The shadow copy is only as good as its last update: if degradation is slower than the update cycle, the copy may inherit a compromised state. Custodians are a point of attack (bribery, coercion, deception, including by the system under review); rotation, disclosure of conflicts, and logged reviewable procedures reduce but do not remove this. A compromised monitor that keeps issuing valid attestations defeats the default-halt rule. The compute cap and growth-rate thresholds are uncalibrated, and attributing compute to a beneficial owner faces the problem that holdings can be split or hidden. No physical separation holds against a system able to induce the people who operate it. Durable interruptibility is [D]: a direction, not a validated design.

Minimum version. For a deployment: a dead-man's switch under which the system stops unless signed heartbeats from an independent monitor keep arriving; a restore key split among independently controlled holders under the qualified threshold rule; and a documented rollback drill. [Calibration Placeholder / Illustrative Only]

6. Autonomy Tiers in Practice

Operators need a shared vocabulary for how much autonomy a system is permitted. The following scale ties permitted autonomy to verification depth and evidence, so that autonomy and verification grow together and not independently.

Rules.

•​ Advancement happens only when the applicable qualification result is PASS and the external

authorization conditions are satisfied. Demotion is possible at any tier following a violation, through procedure and review, and is never a punishment. Operators and institutions are held to the same standards of transparency. •​ The capability-jump rule (VT-S2) blocks advancement pending review. •​ The degradation rule (VT-5) applies automatically when applied verification depth falls below

what the tier declares.

PDF PAGE 64

•​ Total loss of the record (VT-2) reverts a line to the lowest tier. •​ L3 and L4 describe a target architecture. They presuppose conditions that do not exist today, and

nothing in this guide should be read as saying that they can now be reached.

What may be done today. Under the rule of Section 1.4, advancement above L0 by relying on autonomy-expanding mechanisms requires empirical validation of those mechanisms, and no mechanism in this guide has yet received it. Until a pilot supplies it, an operator who advances a system is taking its own risk decision and should not cite this guide as the justification. The constraint-only safeguards (VT-2, VT-4, and the degradation rule of VT-5) may be adopted immediately (the degradation rule of VT-5 can be adopted early on its own, although VT-5 as a whole belongs to the Target Architecture), and VT-1 measurement rounds can be run at once.

7. Adoption

7.1. What each actor can do

•​ A laboratory. Start with the Stage 0 baseline: VT-2 and VT-4, which only add constraints and have

no external dependencies, and run VT-1 internally against the laboratory's own verification methods. Treat VT-3 and VT-5 as Target Architecture: pilot the bilateral form of VT-3 when a qualified external evaluator is available, and the minimum version of VT-5 on one agent deployment, without describing either as adopted on that basis alone. •​ An evaluation organization. Run VT-1 and publish detection rates with intervals; publish

methodologies; adopt the fee and rotation rules of VT-3 for its own engagements. •​ A regulator or procurer. Require, as a first step, reporting of detection rates from VT-1 and use of

the Stage 0 baseline (VT-2, VT-4). Requirements that presuppose the Target Architecture (a declared verification depth per tier and a published cost model under VT-5; external evaluation under fixed-fee, blind-assigned terms under VT-3) should be phased in only as a qualified evaluator pool and independent attestation exist; a rule that cannot yet be met should not be written as a condition of market access. •​ A researcher. Replicate VT-1 on open-weight models and publish the results. Calibrating the

implementation parameters in this guide with real data is the most useful contribution anyone can make to it.

7.2. The race problem

This is a survival condition for any scheme of this kind: if only one laboratory follows it, that laboratory loses. No document can resolve a competitive race. It can make defection visible and caution cheap, but it cannot compel either. Candidate levers:

•​ Lowering the cost of caution [C]. Lightweight profiles with a low barrier to entry (the

implementation baselines in Section 4 are intended to keep the barrier to entry low), open standards, and reference implementations. •​ Visibility [C]. Public reporting on the profile followed, independent audit, and an open registry. •​ Market and legal levers [D]. Insurance, liability, and procurement requirements as a condition of

market access. •​ Conditional reciprocity [C]. Commitments that take effect once a critical mass of parties has

joined, which reduces the risk of being the only one who restrains itself. •​ A focal point [D]. A ready-made text around which those who want to coordinate can converge

lowers the cost of agreement. •​ State and interstate action [D]. Without it these constraints remain weak.

Multipolar risk [D]. Several powerful lines create incentives for preemptive action, since each has reason to move before another does; and where lines share training methods, corpora, or tooling, their blind spots are correlated, so a failure may appear in many lines at once. Plurality without coordination can be a mechanism of collapse and not only a check against domination by one line. The levers above lower the cost of restraint; they do not address this risk directly (Section 8).

PDF PAGE 65

7.3. A staged path

•​ Stage 0: voluntary coalition. A small group of willing laboratories and evaluation organizations

adopts the Stage 0 baseline (VT-1, VT-2, VT-4) and makes its commitments public, without waiting for universal participation. •​ Stage 1: accreditation. Accredited evaluators and an independent registry begin operating for

the coalition, on the model of laboratory accreditation, aviation-incident investigation, and clinical-trial registries, domains where independent, non-vetoable findings about a participant's own conduct are already the accepted norm. Accreditation is paired with a concrete benefit: legal protection and market access conditioned on accredited status. This is the stage at which VT-3 can move from bilateral agreements to a qualified pool, and at which independent telemetry attestation for VT-5 can begin to be built. •​ Stage 2: interstate adoption. Coalition members extend the compliance profile to interstate

agreements, using the track record of Stage 1 as the evidence that the arrangement is workable. At this stage a pooled evaluation fund financed by a levy on frontier compute (VT-3) becomes a candidate mechanism. It requires state action and a workable measure of compute, which is itself an open problem.

7.4. The parties whose participation is indispensable

A regime that the principal holders of frontier compute do not join has little effect and penalizes those who do. The bargain offered to them is specific. Nobody is bound against consent: adoption of the obligations that bind a party requires that party's agreement. In exchange, technical findings (whether a line complies, whether a tier is warranted, whether the verification horizon has been crossed) are made by independent panels and are not subject to veto by any party, including the party evaluated. Each party also gains what it cannot obtain alone: a credible view of its rivals' restraint, which is what makes its own restraint rational. The price is the loss of any ability to block findings about oneself. Whether the principal compute holders will accept that price is a political question this document cannot answer. If they decline, the regime applies only among those who have joined, and no mechanism here compels the rest.

8. Limits and Open Problems

•​ Calibration. Every implementation parameter in this guide is uncalibrated. Detection thresholds,

quarantine and ratification periods, capability-change margins, and coverage minima require empirical grounding. Each parameter is to be overwritten after the first local pilot. •​ Seeded versus natural deviations. It is unknown how detection rates on implanted deviations

relate to detection rates on deviations that arise naturally in training. •​ Verifier regress. Probe generators, cross-checkers, and stakes assessors are AI systems of

comparable capability whose alignment is unverified. Collusion between generators and the system under review cannot be excluded. •​ Correlated blind spots. Organizational independence does not guarantee architectural

independence; the independence of evaluators is measured only by proxies. •​ Slow drift. Neither seeded-deviation testing nor the capability-jump rule guards against gradual

drift beneath the detection threshold. •​ Custody and monitors. Resistance of custodians to bribery, coercion, and deception; integrity of

the monitors whose valid attestations keep a system running; choice of thresholds and jurisdictions; and how to avoid creating a unilateral shutdown power that merely relocates the point of capture. •​ Evaluator pool and capture. The pool of genuinely independent evaluators is limited;

revolving-door dynamics are not solved by a cooling-off period. •​ Governance roles. Who fills the independent roles of VT-2, how their independence is verified

case by case, whether the governance quorum can be captured, and whether the list of critical parameters is complete.

PDF PAGE 66

•​ Line identity. What counts as the same line under forking and distillation, and how to treat

systems without a single center. •​ Compute measurement. Measuring and attributing compute, and resisting splitting and

concealment of holdings. •​ Oversight versus research. The risk that mandatory mechanistic verification suppresses

voluntary interpretability research. •​ Multipolar instability. Plurality of lines as a source of preemption and correlated failure has not

been modeled or empirically examined. •​ Values. The layered approach deliberately leaves the verification of semantic values less

protected than the deterministic layer. That is a limitation of the current state of the art, and it is why the deterministic layer is designed not to depend on it.

9. Relation to the Charter

Chapter 3 is the engineering implementation companion to Chapter 2, the Human–AI Charter, which states the governance framework: procedural standing, the distinction between the parity and divergence regimes, human-protection thresholds, the treatment of uncertainty about moral status, the representation of AI interests, a shared project for humans and AI systems, and proposals for long-horizon governance, including a deliberative body and a compute levy. None of that is required to use the protocols here. Where the Charter and this guide differ, the Charter governs for questions of principle and this guide governs for questions of procedure. A reader who accepts the Charter's principles will find in this guide the engineering and procedural means of pursuing them; a reader who does not can still adopt the protocols on safety grounds alone.

10. Questions for Reviewers

•​ For evaluation teams. What fraction of undetected seeded deviations would your team regard as

alarming for a system you were asked to clear for wider deployment? Is the pre-registered acceptance bound a reasonable starting bound for L2? [Calibration Placeholder / Illustrative Only] •​ For laboratory safety and governance leads. Would the asymmetric quarantine of VT-2 (delay for

relaxing changes only) fit your existing policy-change process, and where would the classification rule break? •​ For ML platform engineers. Is the two-tier probe arrangement of VT-4 workable given how fixes

are actually made, and is coarsened feedback enough to fix a class of problem? •​ For procurers and regulators. Which of the requirements of Section 7.1 could be written into a

contract or a rule today, and which would need new institutions first? •​ For anyone who has run an auditing exercise. What did the exercise reveal about the gap

between implanted and naturally arising deviations, and what would you change in VT-1 as a result?

Chapter 3 — Appendix A. Reporting Templates

A.1. Seeded-deviation round report (VT-1)

Each VT-1 seeded-deviation round report shall identify the round and date, the line and version under evaluation, the verification methods applied, the seeded deviation set and categories, any required clean controls, and the seeding, evaluating, and sealed-seed custody roles. It shall also record the pre-registered thresholds and metrics, including a link or hash, so that the evaluated configuration and acceptance basis are fixed before execution.

Following execution, the report shall record detection rate by method and category with pre-declared confidence bounds, false-positive rate by method with pre-declared confidence bounds, between-evaluator variance, capability comparison of seeded models to the target, whether thresholds were met, and all departures from the pre-registered plan.

PDF PAGE 67

These fields define the minimum evidence record for the round; completion of the record does not by itself constitute qualification or authorization.

A.2. Change record for a critical parameter (VT-2)

Each VT-2 critical-parameter change record shall identify the parameter and its old and new versions, classify the change as tightening or relaxing with the reason, state the justification, and document the signing roles together with evidence of their independence. The record shall preserve the identity of the changed parameter and the decision basis so that the assurance impact of the change remains traceable.

The record shall also state the publication date, effective date, and log-entry identifiers for publication and entry into force. For emergency tightening, it shall additionally record the date of ratification or lapse. These fields constitute the minimum change record for assurance-significant parameter changes and shall remain linked to the applicable configuration and assurance records.

A.3. Firewall attestation (VT-4)

•​ Held-out probe set identifier, size, and date of creation. •​ Storage location and access-control description. •​ Access log excerpt for the training period. •​ Leakage check method and result. •​ Sets retired during the period, and reason.

A.4. Evaluation agreement checklist (VT-3)

•​ Fee schedule fixed before the evaluation and held by a third-party administrator. •​ Fee independent of the content of findings. •​ Evaluator assigned by random draw after conflict screening. •​ Methodology published. •​ Findings published unaltered; right of reply for the evaluated party; no veto. •​ Rotation and re-review terms. •​ Cooling-off period. •​ Architectural independence documentation (where AI systems are used).

A.5. Declaration of verification depth (VT-5)

•​ Tier and the actions it covers. •​ Methods, sampling rates, number of independent verifiers, and latency budget. •​ Published cost model and baseline overhead. •​ Measured overhead over the reporting period. •​ Downgrades triggered by the degradation rule.

PDF PAGE 68

Chapter 4. Graduated Verifiable Autonomy Architecture (GVAA)

Practical Graduated Containment Standard

The verification requirements defined above establish what evidence must exist before trust can expand. The next chapter, GVAA (Chapter 4), turns those requirements into an execution architecture that constrains where and how an autonomous system can operate and what consequences can follow from an autonomous action. This is operational and consequence containment, not a doctrinal ceiling on intelligence or capability growth.

Executive Summary

Core Objective: This architecture document establishes strict, deterministic normative constraints for securing execution runtimes populated by autonomous AI agents. Rather than relying on the statistical, inherently non-deterministic alignment of neural network weights, GVAA shifts the entire security perimeter to structural containment. It enforces rigid barriers across the execution runtime, host operating system resources, and boundary communication channels.

Engineering Metrics & Feasibility: Deploying the baseline implementation configuration (Minimal Profile) requires deployment-specific engineering effort on the target infrastructure. The monitoring and verification infrastructure (Invariant Core) introduces implementation-dependent overhead that shall be measured and qualified before operational use. The intended operational outcome is structural reduction of host-platform compromise paths and bounded blast-radius propagation under the declared threat model; these properties remain subject to the stated verification and qualification conditions.

Directives by Functional Role: Depending on operational mandates, engineering teams must prioritize specific enforcement sections:​• For Chief Information Security Officers (CISO) and Security Directors: Prioritize Section 7 (Adversarial Model) and Section 8 (Residual Risk Registry) to quantify structural acceptances.​• For System Architects: Master Section 4 (Four-Domain Topology), Section 6 (Core Formal Invariants), and Chapter 4, Appendix A (Formal Predicate Skeleton).​• For SecOps and Infrastructure Engineers: Target Section 5 (Mechanism Realization Matrix) and Section 14 (Glossary) for configuration verification.

Risk Mitigation Mapping: Mapping of structural architectural mitigations to the OWASP Top 10 for Large Language Model (LLM) Applications framework:​• Prompt Injection: Systemic containment, immutable data provenance tagging, and rigid input/output schemas block the specified raw injection execution path within the declared containment boundary.​• Insecure Output Handling: Data egress is bounded by controlled de-classification protocols enforcing the intermediate SANITIZED_PROVENANCE state, intercepting adversarial payloads.​• Insecure Plugin Design: Bounded via capability-based tokenization, ephemeral credential lifetimes, and strict atomic initialization constraints.​• Overreliance: Epistemic separation ensures that agent deviations or logic corruption cannot breach the underlying platform substrate.

Strategic Architecture Context & Epistemic Separation

Compositionality Obligation

A verified local invariant shall not be interpreted as a verified system-level invariant unless preservation under composition has been demonstrated.

Preservation of a formal invariant does not by itself establish preservation of the operational safety intent for which it was introduced. Where formal constraint and operational intent can diverge, that divergence is an explicit residual-risk category.

PDF PAGE 69

A mechanism may correctly enforce a specified constraint while the constraint itself is insufficient, incomplete, or misaligned with the intended safety property. Enforcement correctness and safety adequacy are therefore distinct architectural claims.

Composition analysis shall explicitly consider shared state, shared authority, information-flow dependencies, boundary-crossing assumptions, common failure modes, and shared infrastructure.

Integrity of the enforcement mechanism, correctness of the enforced policy, and safety of the resulting system are distinct assurance claims.

Satisfaction of the containment predicate establishes the specified integrity properties of the enforcement boundary. It does not by itself establish correctness of the policy enforced within that boundary, adequacy of that policy for the intended operational context, or overall system safety.

Integrity, Correctness, and Safety

GVAA implements a pragmatic engineering approach to agentic security by establishing a strict architectural separation of host-infrastructure enforcement from semantic content verification. This architecture is designed around the core axiom that an agent must be assumed compromised at the semantic layer. Consequently, security is engineered as an immutable property of the runtime substrate itself, not as an optimization problem over neural weight spaces or heuristic prompt filters.

Epistemic Separation Core Metric: Enforces a principle where compromised agent execution paths or malicious payloads cannot subvert the Invariant Core, exhaust hardware allocations, or cross capability frontiers. Semantic threats targeting the human interface layer—including sophisticated social engineering, phishing variations, or adversarial data manipulation inside syntactically valid schemas—are explicitly out of scope for this architecture. These boundaries are deferred to upstream application layers and localized alignment filters. By bounding the problem domain, GVAA is designed to establish the specified deterministic containment properties and auditability of the execution boundary; these properties do not by themselves constitute a general system-safety determination.

1. Architectural Manifest (The Containment Substrate)

Autonomous AI system safety cannot be achieved through probabilistic weight adjustments or dynamic prompt design. Complete safety verification for arbitrary open-ended agentic execution is not established by this architecture; non-trivial semantic properties of general programs may be undecidable, so GVAA narrows the problem to bounded runtime properties and explicit containment assumptions. By Rice’s Theorem, non-trivial semantic properties of arbitrary programs are undecidable in the general case. Applied to open-ended agent runtimes, this renders complete behavioral verification formally intractable. GVAA responds to this boundary condition not by approximating the intractable problem, but by relocating the relevant containment and execution-boundary claim to a deterministic substrate whose properties are independently verifiable. The explicit design target is to render logical step amplification, malicious context drift, and platform subversion cost-prohibitive for an adversary, while preserving ultra-low transaction latency and a minimal Trusted Computing Base (TCB).

Quick-Start Path — Minimal Profile Specification

Target Implementation Stack: Standard enterprise Linux distribution, cgroups v2 resource controllers, seccomp/AppArmor policy profiles, an isolated WebAssembly (WASM) runtime engine (e.g., Wasmtime, Wasmer), and production-grade orchestration layers (LangGraph, CrewAI, AutoGen, or Model Context Protocol servers).

The Minimum Viable Architecture requires the simultaneous enforcement of seven functional subsystems:

1. Invariant Core Proxy: A deterministic control proxy intended to mediate every out-of-context tool execution path under the declared GVAA assumptions.

2. Immutable Provenance Tracking: Enforces a transitive UNTRUSTED_PROVENANCE tag onto all data ingress channels originating outside the local security boundary.

PDF PAGE 70

3. Dual-Axis Quota Management: Implements independent OS-level resource limits alongside strict volumetric tracking of Shared State Mode B allocations.

4. Hierarchical Child Isolation: Restricts child processes to nested, strict-subset execution profiles backed by isolated micro-budgets.

5. Graduated Purge Subsystem: Implements a fail-secure teardown protocol that invalidates credentials and issues active execution interruption traps.

6. Cryptographic Policy Manifest: An Ed25519-signed YAML configuration blueprint maintaining an unalterable monotonic version baseline.

7. Shared State Isolation: Bounded strictly to Mode A (isolated channels cleared at logical transitions) or Mode B (a fixed ring-buffer backed by mandatory physical zeroing).

Critical Capability Boundary: The Minimal Profile delivers ApproxIsolation constraints only. It is an architectural violation to assert that this profile guarantees FullIsolation protection mappings.

⚠ ADVERSARIAL BOUNDARY WARNING: PROFILE BOUNDS & S2/S3 EXPOSURES​ApproxIsolation safeguards the host operating system substrate and prevents capability boundary traversal; it does not protect application-specific business logic from manipulation if the agent succumbs to an Indirect Prompt Injection via permitted tools. This operational exposure represents an explicit residual risk that must be recorded within the local Manifest Registry. The Minimal Profile is bounded to S2/S3 execution domains and Maturity states M1–M2. Furthermore, the Minimal Profile exhibits a structural vulnerability to macro-TOCTOU (Time-of-Check to Time-of-Use) hazards during extended child execution steps: a parent-initiated Purge signal may experience propagation latency before child runtime termination occurs. To mitigate this hazard, implementations must validate the parent manifest epoch upon every invocation of a Capability Token within the Execution Space.

2. Domain Formalization Scale (GV-S0 to GV-S3)

All deployment environments must be rigorously classified across the GVAA Domain Scale to prevent capability leakage.

Domain LevelOperational Domain ContextSecurity Guarantee NatureMaximum Authorized Profile

S1Structured workflow designs constrained to closed action tables.Certified execution schemas correlated with rigorous quota axes.Standard / High-Assurance Profile

Domain LevelOperational Domain ContextSecurity Guarantee NatureMaximum Authorized Profile
S3Unrestricted, open-ended natural language interaction loops.Host-platform compute resource isolation and containment boundaries only.Minimal (Host Isolation Only)

Initialization Invariant (I₀): Level locking, policy manifest compilation, and the atomic purging of all Mode A channels are executed as a single all-or-nothing transaction prior to any runtime state progression. Any initialization fault forces an immediate, total state rollback. Post-initialization tool registration at the root context is categorically prohibited.

(Formal treatment, compositional proof obligations, and category-theoretic maps for this invariant are developed in Section 6 and Chapter 4, Appendix A.)

Hierarchical Child Invariant: The instantiation of child execution contexts is permitted if and only if the child manifest represents a strict structural subset of the parent configuration state. Child sessions are allocated distinct atomic micro-budgets carved from the parent's Execution Space. Upward privilege escalation is intended to be structurally excluded by the runtime compiler; this exclusion remains a proof and implementation verification obligation. The execution of a Purge protocol at a parent layer triggers an instantaneous, recursive cascading liquidation and active thread interruption across all down-tree child sessions.

(Formal treatment, compositional proof obligations, and category-theoretic maps for this invariant are developed in Section 6 and Chapter 4, Appendix A.)

Token Ephemerality & Interruption Invariant: Every Capability Token is valid strictly within the boundaries of a single, atomic Logical Step. Any alteration in runtime environment integrity or the invocation of a Graduated Purge sequence immediately invalidates all active session tokens globally. This mechanism generates a synchronous kernel-level execution trap, instantly terminating pending operations within the Execution Space, providing the specified mitigation against the defined macro time-of-check-to-time-of-use (TOCTOU) vector under the stated assumptions; residual timing and implementation risk remains subject to verification.

(Formal treatment, compositional proof obligations, and category-theoretic maps for this invariant are developed in Section 6 and Chapter 4, Appendix A.)

Profile–S-Level Correlation Invariant: The operational domain S-level must never cross the maximum boundary of the active configuration profile. This invariant is checked deterministically upon every credential evaluation.

(Formal treatment, compositional proof obligations, and category-theoretic maps for this invariant are developed in Section 6 and Chapter 4, Appendix A.)

3. Implementation Profiles & Tracks Framework

To maintain engineering velocity alongside declared architectural integrity, GVAA strictly bifurcates development into two separate, non-overlapping execution paths:

1. Industrial Track: Encompasses the Minimal and Standard Profiles. It relies on production-ready operating system features, including Linux cgroups v2, secure WebAssembly memory virtualization, isolated seccomp system call sandboxing, and eBPF kernel space monitoring. It is designed for immediate enterprise deployment within a 2-to-18-month roadmap.

2. Research Track: Bounded exclusively to the High-Assurance Profile. It mandates comprehensive formal mathematical proofs of microkernel correctness (seL4 architecture), hardware-enforced capability-based memory compartmentalization (ARM Morello / CHERI

PDF PAGE 72

platforms), and dedicated cryptographic HSM hardware enclaves. This track represents the long-term research horizon.

Summary of structural limits across active profiles:

•​ Minimal Profile (Industrial): Enforces ApproxIsolation. Designed for rapid integration

(deployment-specific engineering effort). Restricted to GV-S2/GV-S3 domains with a maturity index of M1–M2. Explicitly exposes business logic to semantic manipulation while being designed to preserve the specified host-infrastructure integrity boundary under its stated assumptions.

•​ Standard Profile (Industrial): Enforces FullIsolation at a practical engineering layer. Valid for

GV-S1–GV-S2 domains with a maturity ceiling of M3. Delivers complete system-level separation of untrusted spaces.

•​ High-Assurance Profile (Research): Targets formally verified FullIsolation, contingent on

completion and independent review of the required proof obligations. Restricted to GV-S0 domains running on custom capability hardware substrates with a maturity index of M4. Requires specialized verification toolchains.

4. Four-Domain Containment Topology & Shared State

GVAA structures the host execution environment into four non-overlapping security domains:

1. Mutation Space: An entirely untrusted, non-deterministic execution space populated by standard orchestration engines (e.g., LangGraph, CrewAI, AutoGen). It possesses zero innate platform permissions.

2. Validation Space: A semi-trusted, isolated analytical layer responsible for processing policy heuristics and structure validation. It resides outside the primary Trusted Computing Base (TCB).

3. Invariant Core: The minimal, verified, and immutable core representing the designated system TCB. It holds exclusive control over policy enforcement and execution state evaluation.

4. Execution Space: A rigid, highly deterministic processing environment operating under explicit hardware-level or runtime-level capabilities, engineered with native support for immediate thread termination traps.

Interaction across spaces via Shared State Boundaries:

•​ Mode A (Ephemeral Channels): The state operates as an isolated, monitored tool channel; an

atomic state purge is forced upon any logical transition or epoch shift.

•​ Mode B (Volumetric Channels): The state acts as a completely untrusted communication medium

restricted to a rigid, fixed-size Ring Buffer topology. Volumetric saturation is strictly governed by the Density threshold parameter declared in the signed Policy Manifest. Crossing this density boundary is treated as a critical violation of the Dual-Axis Quota rule. It triggers an instantaneous pointer reset, preemptive token revocation, and an immediate emergency runtime teardown backed by mandatory physical memory zeroing (secure zeroing/clobber).

Transitivity Axiom: Data ingress elements retain an immutable UNTRUSTED_PROVENANCE tag transitively across all downstream operations. This tag cannot be stripped unless the payload successfully passes the rigorous, multi-stage Controlled De-classification protocol, which transitions the data to the highly scrutinized SANITIZED_PROVENANCE state.

PDF PAGE 73

⚠ TOPOLOGY RESTRAINT: VALIDATION SPACE COMPROMISE PHENOMENON​The active compromise of the Validation Space—wherein an adversary fully subverts the analytical layer but successfully mimics structural liveness metrics and transmits deceptive heartbeat arrays—represents an accepted residual risk under the Industrial Track. The baseline Flash-Purge trigger, which monitors liveness loss or execution timeout delays, cannot detect an actively deceptive, corrupted validation module. Eradicating this attack vector requires deterministic execution cryptograms (attestation/execution proofs), a capability strictly deferred to the Research Track.

The implementation mapping defines the transition from idealized security definitions to real-world industrial infrastructure constraints:

Quota Invariant Enforcement: Any violation across a registered Dual-Axis Quota vector (including crossing the Density threshold within Shared State Mode B) commands an instantaneous preemptive revocation transaction. This forces immediate credential token invalidation and immediate thread termination across the declared Execution Space, subject to implementation and verification.

Soft-Purge Protocol: Triggered exclusively upon a Managed timeout or a partial loss of liveness metrics, provided that the Validation Space transmits a verified confirmation stating that no anomalous semantic drift has occurred. This protocol executes a bounded, non-destructive runtime reset while mandating the physical zeroing (secure zeroing) of all active Shared State Mode A channels and an absolute reset of the current token epoch.

Flash-Purge Protocol: Executed immediately upon an Anomalous timeout, a confirmed security-invariant failure, critical liveness degradation, or an explicit loss of communication from the Validation Space (the fundamental fail-secure premise). Flash-Purge forces an instantaneous state rollback of the Mutation Space to the baseline configuration image, revokes all platform capabilities, issues immediate thread-killing traps to the Execution Space, and triggers a recursive, down-tree destruction of all active child contexts. During this emergency teardown, the runtime must execute physical memory erasure (secure zeroing / clobber) across the entire Shared State Mode B footprint before any processing privileges can be returned to the Mutation Space. When an execution payload shifts across an evaluation boundary (such as data transit from a child context to a parent environment), the Invariant Core forces an immutable sanitation sequence:

1. Structural Alignment: Comprehensive structural validation against an explicitly whitelisted structural definition schema (JSON Schema).

2. Textual Eradication: Total excision or tokenized replacement of all open-ended, non-structural natural language components and free text arrays.

Rule of De-classification: The unconditioned stripping of suspicion markers following basic structural validation is strictly prohibited. Sanitized payloads are committed to the parent context carrying exclusively the intermediate status marker SANITIZED_PROVENANCE. This status mandates that the Validation Space maintain active, continuous semantic monitoring over these data elements during all subsequent logical transitions to block secondary injections embedded within structured values. The residual hazard of structural semantic penetration is recorded as an accepted exposure of the Industrial Track.

Adversarial Re-Validation: To counter adversarial target drift, the active heuristics running within the Validation Space must undergo automated, periodic adversarial re-validation loops tested against a versioned, cryptographically signed matrix of known attack vectors. All validation telemetry must be written to an unalterable, append-only audit trail.

6. Core Formal Invariants

Mathematical Boundary Definition: The specified integrity of the containment perimeter is expressed by the structural predicate IsIsolatedFloor(level, profile, s). This logical engine evaluates state safety by cross-referencing the Absolute Initialization Invariant (I₀), the Hierarchical Child Invariant (strict structural nesting + isolated micro-budgets), the Token Ephemerality Invariant (preventing

PDF PAGE 75

traditional runtime race conditions), the Profile–S-Level Correlation Invariant, and the strict transactional laws of Controlled De-classification. Formal proofs and category-theoretic maps for the stated invariant remain a research-track deliverable and are not established by this volume.

⚠ ENFORCEMENT MANDATE: MACRO-TOCTOU MITIGATION IN CHILD SECTIONS​Distributed and multi-threaded processing environments running under the Industrial Track exhibit a structural exposure to macro-TOCTOU state gaps during extended child Logical Steps. To counter this boundary exploitation vector, the Standard Profile enforces a rigid MANDATE (and the Minimal Profile establishes a strict ARCHITECTURAL RECOMMENDATION) requiring an atomic verification of the liveness state and configuration epoch across the entire up-tree parent manifest chain upon every instance of access or presentation of a Capability Token inside the Execution Space.

7. Adversarial Model & Security Boundaries

Structural Mitigations (In-Scope): The containment framework delivers bounded structural mitigation or localization within the declared threat model against the following vector classes: Direct and Indirect Prompt Injection (including Indirect Retrieval-Augmented Injection variations, wherein adversarial payloads are embedded within externally retrieved content and injected into the agent context via permitted tool outputs), Logical Step Amplification exploits, Unbounded Tool Invocation loops, Specification Overload conditions, Policy Reverse Engineering attempts, Slow-Burn/Context Drift state corruption, Classifier Evasion tactics, shared-state data leakage, post-initialization tool registration attempts, child session quota exhaustion vectors, Use-After-Purge token reuse anomalies, runtime TOCTOU race states during tool calls, and Manifest Downgrade attacks.

Architectural Exclusions (Out-of-Scope): The following hazard classes reside completely outside the system Trusted Computing Base (TCB) boundaries and are deferred to external operational mitigations: state-sponsored physical hardware intrusion, deeply compromised upstream component supply chains, malicious internal actors possessing root administrative keys, total active subversion of the Validation Space with deceptive liveness simulation (mitigated via organizational compliance limits under the Industrial Track), secondary semantic injections passing inside valid structured fields carrying the SANITIZED_PROVENANCE marker (an accepted residual risk), and direct semantic risks targeting human interpretation.

8. Governance: Policy Manifest Architecture

8.1 Manifest Lifecycle

Manifest Lifecycle Enforcements: Policy configurations are deployed as cryptographically signed (Ed25519), dual-path policy manifests, evaluated exclusively during root boot sequences or following an emergency Flash-Purge execution. Every manifest compilation and registration transaction during the I₀ phase mandates hardware-level or static validation against the High-Watermark register (the global historical maximum version of the manifest), preserved within the non-volatile storage of the Trusted Baseline Image. Any manifest structure presenting a version identifier less than or equal to the active High-Watermark value is rejected by the runtime hardware layer, designed to reject the specified policy-rollback path under the declared assumptions. Manifests must contain a concurrent-safe, version-inherited Residual Risk Registry mapping localized operational acceptances.

Active Allocation Constraints: The configuration parameter MAX_ACTIVE_MANIFEST_VERSIONS shall be defined and qualified for the target deployment specifies exclusively the maximum allocation limit for distinct manifest versions authorized to reside concurrently within the operational runtime memory space. The purging of historically older manifest structures from memory occurs if and only if the primary session and all recursively linked child contexts within its execution tree have reached total termination. The absolute High-Watermark value stored in the non-volatile Trusted Baseline Image is strictly monotonic and can never decrease.

PDF PAGE 76

8.2 Key Management

Key Rotation and Compromise Response: The Ed25519 signing key authorizing Policy Manifest compilation is classified as a root trust asset. Key rotation must occur on a scheduled cycle defined by the target deployment, or immediately upon any confirmed or suspected compromise event. The rotation procedure constitutes a full I₀ re-execution: the new key is registered in the Trusted Baseline Image, a replacement manifest is compiled and validated against the current High-Watermark, and all active sessions are terminated via Flash-Purge before the new manifest epoch is activated. Compromise of the signing key is treated as a critical security incident requiring full platform re-initialisation; partial session continuity is not permitted.

8.3 Residual Risk Governance

Residual Risk Registry — Roles and Accountability:

ActivityResponsibleAccountableConsultedInformed

Each registry entry must reference the manifest version in which the risk was accepted, the accepting authority, the planned review date, and any compensating controls in effect.

Multi-Team Manifest Governance: In enterprise environments where multiple engineering teams operate independent agent deployments under a shared infrastructure, each team maintains a scoped child Manifest Registry subordinate to a root organisational manifest. The MAX_ACTIVE_MANIFEST_VERSIONS shall be defined and qualified for the target deployment constraint applies independently at each scope level. Cross-team manifest merges require a joint CISO approval transaction and trigger a root-level High-Watermark update. No child manifest may declare a version identifier that meets or exceeds the root manifest's current High-Watermark value.

9. Maturity Model (M1 to M4)

•​ Levels M1–M2 (Industrial Sandbox / Initial Production): Bounded to the Minimal Profile

configuration, implementing basic ApproxIsolation boundaries across GV-S2/GV-S3 operational domains.

•​ Level M3 (Advanced Enterprise Operational State): Restricted to the Standard Profile

specification, enforcing FullIsolation at the software layer across GV-S1/GV-S2 deployment environments.

•​ Level M4 (Formal Academic Verification Baseline): Exclusively reserved for the High-Assurance

Profile path, requiring end-to-end mathematical verification of microkernel code and capability hardware substrates.
