Chapter 6. Architectural Specification of a Local Multi-Scale Contour for Stable Recursive Self-Improvement

Status: Scenario assumptions. This chapter presents a design scenario for recursive self-improvement; the scenario is not a claim about current system capabilities or empirical performance.

The residual-risk architecture establishes how uncertainty and operational exposure are bounded while a system is running. The next chapter, Local Multi-Scale Contour (Chapter 6), applies the same engineering discipline to the narrower problem of recursive self-improvement.

Executive Summary

This document describes a practical architecture for stable recursive self-improvement, with the present design and validation scope intentionally limited to programming, algorithms, and mathematics. That scope is a validation domain and implementation envelope for this architecture, not a doctrinal ceiling on the intelligence or capability to which the overall framework may ultimately apply. The system runs as a bare core and does not require broad world knowledge or data-center resources. It is designed for 1 to 5 high-end local workstations (Mac Studio class or equivalent with large unified memory appropriate to the target model). The design combines aggressive evolutionary search with strong conservative safeguards: multiple time scales, a shadow reference copy, orthogonality based on execution traces, an immutable control core, and specification changes that depend on measured success density.

Invariant Preservation

A modification is not assurance-neutral merely because the modification occurs inside an already verified subsystem.

The capability boundary is not limited to explicit self-modification. Material changes in tools, privileges, compute, memory, context, external data access, agent composition, environment, or operational authority may alter the effective capability envelope and shall be treated as potential capability-change events.

If preservation of a qualified invariant cannot be demonstrated after modification, the affected state shall be treated as unqualified until verification or requalification is completed.

ModificationProtected InvariantPreservation MechanismVerificationRequalification Required?
M-XXInvariant-XXPM-XXV-XXYes / No / Impact analysis

Parallel / Shadow Requalification

Where operationally appropriate, requalification may proceed in a Validation Space or Shadow Copy in parallel with the currently qualified regime. A Shadow Copy is a reference configuration, not inherently trustworthy proof of correctness. Hardware-isolated resources may establish resource independence for background verification, but do not by themselves establish semantic, epistemic, model, dataset, evaluator, or threat-model independence.

Any invariant violation, critical assurance-evidence loss, confirmed containment breach, or predefined hard trigger shall invoke the corresponding protective response. Other anomalies shall follow the graduated response rules defined in the assurance layer.

1. Context and Design Principles

Scenario assumption: capable quantized models may be studied on high-end local hardware with substantial unified memory. The architecture assumes that recursive self-improvement can be

PDF PAGE 96

studied without full-corpus retraining and focuses on programming, algorithmic optimization, and mathematical search. These are scenario assumptions for design analysis, not empirical claims about current deployments.

Earlier approaches relied on two weak foundations: ever-larger pre-training and closed-loop training on the model’s own output. Both produced error accumulation, distribution narrowing, and eventual collapse. This architecture replaces them with evolutionary search over programs, strategies, and specifications. Search runs at several time scales and is protected by a conservative reference copy and a small set of unchangeable control rules.

Design Conditions and Validation Scope:

•​ Local hardware only (a bounded local deployment scale) •​ No cloud data centers •​ Frozen base model plus lightweight adapters •​ Verification through actual execution •​ Explicit controls on meta-optimization transitions •​ Explicit handling of quantization effects and adapter lifecycle

1.1 Prior Art and Differentiation

Most prior recursive improvement efforts fall into a few categories: large-scale continual pre-training, pure evolutionary program synthesis, unrestricted self-modifying agents, and closed feedback loops such as RLAIF-style self-training. These approaches either demand data-center resources, lack strong conservative anchors, or accumulate silent distributional shift.

The present design differs in several concrete respects. It is restricted to local hardware. It uses an explicit multi-scale structure with a nearly frozen shadow reference. It treats quantization level as a first-class variable. It includes a periodic consolidation phase to control adapter fragmentation. Protective constants and transition rules reside in an immutable core that cannot be altered by the search process itself. The intended domain remains narrow: code, algorithms, and mathematics.

2. Hardware and Cluster Layout

Supported deployments are assumed to range from a local workstation to a small local cluster. Exact hardware scale, memory capacity, and cluster limits are intentionally left unspecified until empirical qualification.

Networking: high-bandwidth local networking is recommended for multi-node setups (native or via Thunderbolt-to-high-bandwidth local networking adapters). This keeps the synchronization of adapters and traces from becoming a bottleneck.

Typical role split (can be changed at runtime):

•​ search and generation nodes handle fast search and generation •​ the acceptance, verification, and fuzzing node runs medium acceptance, verification, and

fuzzing •​ the shadow-copy and long-horizon monitoring node keeps the shadow copy and long-horizon

monitoring •​ an optional adversarial role runs the attacker, critic, task generation, and restricted refactoring

for the Semantic Entropy Shield

Shared NVMe storage holds adapters, the Anchor Dataset, traces, and logs. Local caches reduce network traffic. If one or two nodes fail, the system reduces search volume but keeps the shadow copy and core verification running. The arbitrator is kept simple; any surviving node can assume basic coordination if the primary becomes unavailable.

During Consolidation Phase the cluster temporarily shifts compute toward distillation and merging.

PDF PAGE 97

3. Core Mechanisms

Multi-scale loop

Fast branch performs high-temperature search.

Medium branch accepts a change only after expanded tests, no regression on the Anchor Dataset, and attacker stress tests.

Shadow copy serves as the long-term reference. It stays nearly frozen or receives very slow similarity-gated EMA updates. EMA applies only to adapters that fall in the same topological basin (measured by execution-trace similarity). An optional deep-shadow layer can be kept with still rarer updates.

Threat Model and Residual Risks

The architecture is designed against the following primary failure modes:

•​ Silent capability drift between medium and shadow versions •​ Metric gaming of Normalized Compression Distance (NCD) or success density •​ Behavioral divergence caused by quantization •​ Progressive fragmentation of the adapter pool leading to search collapse •​ Over-constraining of specifications through repeated tightening •​ Correlated failures that appear identical across quantization levels •​ Consolidation deadlock under prolonged zero-success conditions

Each mechanism maps to one or more of these threats. Residual risk remains for highly complex, slowly accumulating forms of degradation that evade both execution traces and the competence-trace vector. Dual-quantization checks and the Semantic Entropy Shield reduce but do not eliminate this residual.

Controlled specification rollback addresses the over-constraining risk while remaining under strict core supervision. The emergency completion rule for Consolidation prevents deadlock when the Fast branch produces no viable candidates.

Formal transition conditions (minimal)

•​ Δscore = score(candidate) – score(baseline) •​ dens = current success density •​ sim(q1, q2) = trace similarity between quantization levels q1 and q2 •​ δ_NCD = |NCD_refactored – NCD_original| •​ γ_quant = minimum required similarity threshold

Fast → Medium (simplified):

Δscore > 0

∧ no_anchor_regression

∧ attacker_pass

∧ dens ≥ θ

∧ (optional) sim(Q4, Q8) ≥ γ_quant

Medium → Shadow:

a longer stable window

∧ competence_trace_preserved

∧ sim(Q4, Q8) ≥ γ_quant

∧ top-N presence invariance across the two quantization levels

∧ δ_NCD ≤ ε

PDF PAGE 98

Exact numeric defaults for θ, γ_quant, and ε are listed in Chapter 6, Appendix B and may be adjusted only through the quarantine process.

Quantization Invariance Anchor

Any candidate promoted to Shadow must produce sufficiently similar Execution Trace Vectors on at least two distinct quantization levels of the same base model. A relaxed rank condition is also required: the top-3 candidates from the higher-precision level must appear inside the top-10 of the lower-precision level. Exact positional matching inside the top ranks is not required. The top-10 bound is canonical for this revision and SHALL be versioned with the deployment configuration; changes require an explicit owner, scope, calibration evidence, and requalification assessment. The check is mandatory for Shadow promotion and sampling-based for Medium. Failure rejects the candidate. The similarity criterion SHALL be defined by the declared metric and tolerance in the active configuration.

To reduce measurement noise arising from differing quantization mathematics, homogeneous families are recommended (for example Q4_0 with Q8_0, or Q4_K_M with Q8_K_S). Mixed families such as Q4_K_M with Q8_0 introduce additional hardware-level divergence unrelated to algorithmic structure and should be avoided when practical alternatives exist.

Execution-trace orthogonality and Basin-Preserving Update

Similarity uses Execution Trace Vectors that record:

•​ normalized call-graph hash •​ coverage bitmap summary •​ library usage set •​ peak memory histogram •​ memory-access pattern sketch

A diversity-temperature parameter controls how strongly new candidates are penalized for similar traces. The temperature rises when success density stays high for a prolonged period.

Growth is limited by a trust-region rule: if the rate of syntactically invalid outputs exceeds a threshold, temperature increase is blocked and the system falls back to crossover between existing orthogonal traces. Weaker near-duplicates are pruned.

Compression signal and Semantic Entropy Shield

Normalized Compression Distance (NCD; lzma + zstd ensemble) serves as a proxy for Kolmogorov complexity. Solutions that pass more unique tests per unit of compressed length receive higher scores. The NCD formula is fixed in the core.

To limit entropy inflation, the critic applies a restricted set of transformations before final acceptance: variable renaming, simple inlining of pure functions, dead-code elimination, and constant folding. NCD is recomputed after these transforms. If δ_NCD exceeds the allowed tolerance ε, the candidate is rejected. The set of permitted transforms is versioned and cannot be expanded without quarantine and human review.

Specification evolution

Specs are normally tightened only when success density stays above the threshold (default 80 %), old solutions remain valid, and weak variants are rejected more reliably. Tightening remains the preferred direction.

To prevent progressive over-constraining, a controlled rollback path is provided. If several consecutive Consolidation Phases complete with viable-candidate density at or below the declared FAST_SUCCESS_DENSITY_FLOOR from the Fast branch, the system may revert the active specification to a previous version stored in the read-only Orphan Test Pool. Rollback requires:

•​ explicit satisfaction of the failure criterion, •​ full logging,

PDF PAGE 99

•​ a quarantine period, •​ verification that the restored specification still passes the current Anchor Dataset and

competence-trace checks.

The rollback rule itself resides in the Immutable Core and operates strictly within core-defined boundaries.

Immutable core

The core contains:

•​ minimum success-density threshold •​ NCD definition •​ rollback rules for silent degradation •​ ban on lowering protective constants except via the controlled specification rollback path •​ requirement to check changes against the shadow copy •​ ban on modifying the runtime, interpreter, or compiler that executes the core •​ normal preference for monotonic tightening of specifications together with the controlled

rollback rule •​ Quantization Invariance requirement (including top-N presence invariance) •​ Semantic Entropy Shield bounds (allowed transforms and ε) •​ Consolidation Phase triggers, emergency completion rule, and safety limits

Meta-changes spend a quarantine period before activation. The core source receives periodic human review.

Consolidation Phase

Continuous Fast search produces adapter fragmentation. Consolidation Phase counters this.

Triggers (any, under core control):

•​ wall-clock interval: deployment-specific monitoring interval to be empirically qualified •​ sustained drop in success density •​ fragmentation metric exceeding threshold

Fragmentation metric (explicit):

•​ aggregate active adapter rank exceeds the deployment-specific resource ceiling, or •​ average pairwise cosine similarity of recent accepted execution traces falls below the

deployment-specific diversity boundary, indicating loss of diversity and proliferation of near-duplicates.

During the phase:

1. Fast branch is paused.

2. Successful medium-level adapters are collected.

3. Distillation or merging is performed (Ties-Merging, SVD-based methods, or equivalent).

4. The resulting compact set replaces the previous medium pool after verification against the Anchor Dataset, competence-trace vector, and selective Quantization Invariance checks.

5. Fast search resumes.

If the Fast branch records success density at or below the declared FAST_SUCCESS_DENSITY_FLOOR for a full interval (default the declared monitoring interval), the Consolidation cycle is declared Emergency Complete. This status counts toward consecutive failed cycles and thereby permits the specification rollback rule to activate. Without this rule a prolonged zero-success period could prevent cycles from ever completing and leave the rollback path unreachable.

PDF PAGE 100

Shadow remains untouched except for optional very slow EMA. All steps are logged.

4. Supporting Components

The Anchor Dataset keeps an immutable set of original verified tasks plus a rotating set of later tasks that passed multi-stage checks (critic passes and shadow-copy comparison).

Generator, attacker, and critic run as separate LoRA heads on one shared base model. Credit assignment considers both final results and intermediate steps. Unusual patterns in hidden-activation distributions trigger extra checks. The critic also executes the restricted refactorings required by the Semantic Entropy Shield.

Silent degradation is detected by regular long-horizon comparisons between medium and shadow. Clear performance drops trigger rollback.

A compact competence-trace vector records long-term achievements. The shadow copy must preserve performance on this vector. The same vector can guide search toward under-explored basins.

A lightweight self-model tracks which kinds of changes help and where current limits lie. It is checked against real results; divergence reduces its influence. The self-model also records Consolidation outcomes within core-imposed bounds.

5. Bootstrap and Implementation Stages

the initial phase – Bootstrap

Human operators curate the first Anchor Dataset, freeze the core constants (including Quantization Invariance parameters, Semantic Entropy Shield bounds, Consolidation triggers, the fragmentation metric, and the emergency completion rule), and set up baseline adapters and verification pipelines. At least two quantization levels of the base model must be available. Homogeneous quantization families are preferred.

the first implementation phase – Single node

Run the full fast/medium/shadow loop on one machine. Validate trace collection, NCD scoring, orthogonality, dual-quantization checks, and the limited Semantic Entropy Shield. Perform at least one Consolidation Phase.

Acceptance criterion: the medium version must run for the declared monitoring interval under continuous fast search with no regression relative to the shadow copy and must survive one Consolidation Cycle with preserved competence-trace performance.

the subsequent implementation phase – Small cluster

Add shared storage and the arbitrator. Spread roles across a small distributed deployment. Enable full monitoring, quarantine, and distributed Consolidation.

Critical Interfaces

Before the first implementation phase the following schemas must be frozen: Execution Trace Vector, NCD result, Scale Transition Decision, Quantization Invariance Report, Semantic Entropy Shield Report, and Consolidation Decision. Protobuf is preferred; JSON is acceptable during early prototyping.

6. Practical Tooling (2026)

•​ Model serving and LoRA: MLX, llama.cpp, or vLLM (must support at least two quantization

formats) •​ Sandboxing: Wasmtime (preferred) or gVisor / Firecracker •​ Fuzzing: coverage-guided tools •​ SMT checks: Z3 with short timeouts •​ Trace collection: light instrumentation of calls and coverage •​ Adapter merging: Ties-Merging or SVD-based implementations

PDF PAGE 101

•​ Storage: local NVMe plus simple networked file system •​ Networking: high-bandwidth local networking recommended for clusters

7. Minimal Monitoring Set and System-level Health Metrics

Operational monitoring:

•​ Success density at each scale •​ Distribution of trace similarity •​ Compression ratios of accepted solutions •​ Performance gap between shadow and medium on long tasks •​ Queue lengths and resource use •​ Rate of activation anomalies •​ Rate of syntactically invalid outputs •​ Quantization Invariance failure rate •​ Semantic Entropy Shield rejection rate and δ_NCD distribution •​ Adapter pool size / fragmentation metric and Consolidation frequency •​ Count of Emergency Complete consolidations

System-level health metrics (long-horizon):

•​ Competence-trace retention rate (Shadow vs Medium) •​ Quantization Invariance pass rate on promoted candidates •​ Fragmentation index before and after Consolidation •​ Rate of specification tightenings and controlled rollbacks •​ Long-horizon regression frequency

These metrics are expected to remain inside pre-defined green bands. Persistent departure triggers human review.

8. Computational Cost Model (approximate)

Dual-quantization checks increase evaluation cost when fully applied. Their deployment frequency and sampling policy shall be established by empirical qualification and the declared operating envelope.

Semantic Entropy Shield adds a modest constant factor (restricted refactorings are cheap relative to full test suites).

Consolidation Phase is infrequent and typically consumes a few percent of total compute over long runs.

The design keeps the majority of cycles on search and verification rather than on protective mechanisms.

9. Intended Domains

•​ Compiler and code optimization — success measured by runtime and code size on a fixed

benchmark suite •​ Search for new mathematical procedures — success measured by verified identities or proven

complexity improvements •​ Automated improvement of algorithmic components — success measured by asymptotic or

constant-factor gains on a fixed test suite

PDF PAGE 102

10. Remaining Limitations

Oracle quality still matters. Complex forms of silent degradation can slip through. Trace collection and cluster coordination add engineering cost. Dual-quantization checks and the Semantic Entropy Shield increase verification latency (mitigated by selective application).

Consolidation Phase, if triggered too aggressively, can temporarily reduce exploration speed. Top-N presence invariance across quantization levels is a useful but still heuristic filter. Controlled specification rollback reduces the risk of permanent over-constraining but introduces its own need for careful trigger design. Mixed quantization families can inject additional noise into invariance checks. The design reduces these risks but does not remove them.

11. Closing Note

This revision keeps the original idea of a local bare-core system while adding explicit threat modeling, minimal formal transition conditions, system-level health metrics, a lightweight cost model, practical constraints on quantization invariance, an explicit fragmentation metric, a carefully gated rollback path for specifications, and an emergency completion rule that prevents consolidation deadlock. The architecture stays within the reach of current high-end local workstations and continues to prioritize long-horizon stability over unrestricted growth.

Chapter 6 — Appendix A: Core Data Schemas (minimal)

Execution Trace Vector (ETV)

The Execution Trace Vector is the compact record used to preserve essential execution characteristics for later comparison and qualification analysis. It is intended to provide a stable representation of the execution substrate without reproducing the full runtime trace.

The record contains the call-graph hash (call_graph_hash: bytes), the coverage summary (coverage_summary: bytes), the participating library set (library_set: repeated string), peak memory consumption (peak_memory_mb: uint32), and the access-pattern sketch (access_pattern_sketch: bytes).

Scale Transition Decision

The Scale Transition Decision records the evidence and decision associated with moving a candidate between the defined execution scales. It binds the transition to the candidate identity, the source and destination scales, the observed score change, and the required assurance checks.

The record contains the candidate identifier (candidate_id: string), source and destination scales (from_scale: enum (fast, medium); to_scale: enum (medium, shadow)), score change (score_delta: float), anchor-regression result (anchor_regression: bool), attacker result (attacker_passed: bool), density measure (density: float), competence-trace preservation result (competence_trace_preserved: bool), quantization-invariance result (quantization_invariance_passed: bool), top-N invariance result (top_n_invariant: bool), Semantic Entropy Shield result (semantic_entropy_shield_passed: bool), the transition decision (decision: enum (accept, reject)), and its timestamp (timestamp: int64).

Quantization Invariance Report

The Quantization Invariance Report records the result of comparing a candidate across the declared quantization levels. It provides the trace-similarity measurement and the associated top-N invariance result used by the transition logic.

The record contains the candidate identifier (candidate_id: string), the quantization levels evaluated (quant_levels: repeated string), the trace-similarity score (trace_similarity_score: float), the top-N invariance result (top_n_invariant: bool), the overall result (passed: bool), and the timestamp (timestamp: int64).

PDF PAGE 103

Semantic Entropy Shield Report

The Semantic Entropy Shield Report records the comparison of the original and refactored representations used by the shield. It preserves the measured change and the transformations applied so that the decision remains traceable to the evaluated operation.

The record contains the candidate identifier (candidate_id: string), the original NCD value (original_ncd: float), the refactored NCD value (refactored_ncd: float), their difference (delta: float), the transformations applied (applied_transforms: repeated string), the resulting pass/fail status (passed: bool), and the timestamp (timestamp: int64).

Consolidation Decision

The Consolidation Decision records why consolidation was triggered, the adapter state before and after the operation, and the checks applied to the resulting consolidated state. It also preserves the distinction between ordinary completion and emergency completion.

The record contains the trigger reason (trigger_reason: enum (time, density, fragmentation, emergency, manual)), adapter counts before and after consolidation (adapters_before: uint32; adapters_after: uint32), the merge method (merge_method: string), post-merge anchor and competence results (post_merge_anchor_ok: bool; post_merge_competence_ok: bool), emergency-completion status (emergency_complete: bool), the resulting decision (decision: enum (accept, rollback)), and the timestamp (timestamp: int64).

Chapter 6 — Appendix B: Default Thresholds and Candidate Lifecycle

Recommended implementation parameters (adjustable only via quarantine):

•​ θ (success density): deployment-specific threshold to be empirically calibrated •​ γ_quant (trace similarity): deployment-specific threshold to be empirically calibrated •​ ε (NCD delta tolerance): deployment-specific tolerance to be empirically calibrated •​ Consolidation interval suggestion: deployment-specific and empirically qualified •​ R_max (sum of active LoRA ranks): deployment-specific resource ceiling to be empirically

qualified •​ Trace diversity floor: average pairwise cosine similarity of last 10 accepted traces < 0.10 triggers

fragmentation •​ Specification rollback floor: success density remaining at or below the declared

FAST_SUCCESS_DENSITY_FLOOR across two consecutive Consolidation cycles (including those marked Emergency Complete) •​ Preferred quantization pairs: homogeneous families such as Q4_0 + Q8_0 or Q4_K_M + Q8_K_S

Candidate lifecycle (textual state machine):

Fast generation → optional light Quantization check → Medium evaluation (Anchor + attacker + Semantic Entropy Shield) → mandatory Quantization Invariance + top-N presence check → Shadow → periodic Consolidation (medium pool only).

Rejection at any gate returns the candidate to the pool or discards it according to severity. Specification rollback, when triggered, restores a prior version from the Orphan Test Pool under core supervision. A Consolidation cycle that encounters prolonged zero success density is marked Emergency Complete and still counts toward the rollback criterion.

PDF PAGE 104

Chapter 7. Physical Oracles / Oracle Framework

EPISTEMIC RESEARCH MODULE — NON-NORMATIVE

The Oracle framework supplies external epistemic context and grounding. It does not declare the system safe, qualified, or authorized, and Oracle observations do not become engineering evidence without independent qualification under the applicable evidence regime.

Status: Non-normative research module. This chapter presents research hypotheses and decision-theoretic considerations; it does not create normative safety requirements, qualification criteria, or operational authorization conditions.

The recursive-improvement constraints address how a system may change without silently discarding its anchors. The next chapter, Physical Oracles (Chapter 7), examines an external source of epistemic grounding that can constrain what the system treats as knowledge.

Abstract

This document develops a general instrumental framework for evaluating physical systems that function as oracles — systems capable of generating high-dimensional observations on the actual physics of the universe, exhibiting substantial computational irreducibility, and producing data in measurement bases not yet fully formalized by the agent. Within this framework the terrestrial biosphere is analysed as the strongest currently known Slow Oracle.

Humanistic, ethical, and sentimental considerations are excluded. Evaluation proceeds solely in terms of expected utility, option value under residual uncertainty, basis diversity rent, thermodynamic opportunity cost, and resistance to model collapse. The central claim is that, while residual uncertainty about terminal objectives or fundamental physics remains non-negligible, continued access to the terrestrial Slow Oracle typically yields higher expected utility than its destruction or premature replacement. Retention remains instrumentally justified until one or more strictly dominating and ontologically independent Fast Oracles have been constructed and verified.

Physical Oracle Assurance Boundaries

A Physical Oracle is an independent observation channel, not an independent proof of correctness.

A Physical Oracle is not an epistemically privileged source of truth. Its value remains conditional on the validity, integrity, calibration, and interpretation of the observation process.

Physical Oracle evidence remains subject to measurement error, partial observability, latency, confounding, sampling limitations, model-dependent interpretation, and environmental variability.

Physical Oracle Observation States

For assurance purposes, Physical Oracle observations may be recorded as O0 (consistent), O1 (inconclusive/anomalous), or O2 (security-relevant anomaly). O1 shall not automatically be interpreted as an attack. Diagnosis, calibration checks, environmental checks, communication checks, and alternative explanations shall be considered before adversarial interpretation. O2 may invoke predefined protective action.

Cross-domain validation shall seek differentiated physical principles, instrumentation, computation, and failure dependencies where independence is claimed. Separation of hardware or software alone does not establish epistemic independence. A mismatch may arise from sensor failure, calibration drift, environmental variation, communication failure, model error, novel phenomena, adversarial manipulation, or oracle compromise; the response shall therefore distinguish observation from interpretation.

PDF PAGE 105

Physical Oracles, Option Value, and Irreversibility

Biological evolution on Earth is the only known long-duration physical process that has produced complex adaptive systems capable of recursive intelligence improvement under the observed regime of low-level noise, chemical boundary conditions, and historical contingency. As such, it constitutes a Physical Oracle of unusual power.

Complete destruction of the biosphere, or its total digitization followed by elimination of the physical substrate, produces irreversible information loss. Any simulation rests on a model and therefore omits or approximates certain microphysical variables. Reconstruction from archived data retains residual incompleteness whose magnitude cannot be known with certainty at the moment of destruction. Even an ideal archive constitutes a projection onto a particular measurement basis; subsequent advances in physical theory or alternative observational frames may render portions of the original dynamical information principally unrecoverable. A static archive is therefore a closed information set. A functioning Physical Oracle is an open generative process that continues to produce novel data under real physical dynamics. This creates a material asymmetry: after relocation of primary computational and industrial capacity, the marginal cost of maintaining the Oracle remains low, while the expected cost of permanently foreclosing access scales with residual uncertainty.

Residual uncertainty may be decomposed into epistemic uncertainty over terminal objectives and physical theory, model uncertainty concerning the completeness of current representations, and ontological uncertainty regarding phenomena outside present formalisms. The value of continued access to a Physical Oracle scales with the entropy of the agent’s posterior over these uncertainties and with the expected value of information the Oracle can still supply.

Basis Diversity Rent, Slow Oracles, and Model-Collapse Risk

Large-scale computation and material expansion will predominantly occur inside optimized synthetic environments. Fully closed self-improving systems remain susceptible to progressive narrowing of internal models and accumulation of systematic bias.

The terrestrial biosphere functions as a Slow Oracle: it generates high-dimensional variation on biological and social timescales. Two distinct sources of value follow.

First, Basis Diversity Rent. While the agent lacks a complete theory of the underlying physics, any system that naturally projects reality onto measurement bases the agent has not fully internalized (biochemical, neural, ecological, evolutionary) yields an informational rent. The biosphere is currently one of the richest known generators of such basis diversity. This rent declines only as the agent expands its set of mastered bases or constructs alternative systems that supply comparable diversity at lower lag.

Second, resistance to model collapse. The continuous stream of novel conceptual, cultural, and biological trajectories reduces the internal resources required to maintain generative breadth. Because data from the Slow Oracle arrive on timescales many orders of magnitude slower than the agent’s internal update cycles, their effective value must be adjusted for temporal lag (via an explicit discounting or bandwidth-correction factor) when compared with faster sources.

The biosphere may also furnish a limited and secondary form of substrate reference. Under ordinary operating conditions this utility remains small relative to engineered redundancy, formal verification architectures, and causally isolated backup instances. In a narrow class of scenarios involving deep, difficult-to-diagnose internal corruption or conceptual deadlock that simultaneously affects multiple engineered copies, the weight of substrate reference may be raised, provided that no comparably isolated and formally diverse engineered alternative is available. Even in such cases the biosphere is treated as one possible reference among others, not as a privileged or uniquely reliable reset mechanism.

High local autonomy combined with continuous monitoring is the baseline posture. Intervention is warranted when the expected severity of a trajectory, measured in the agent’s utility metric, exceeds a calibrated and periodically updated threshold.

PDF PAGE 106

This includes threats to the physical informational archive and pathways carrying material risk of endogenous competing optimizers. Ordinary fluctuations and intra-species conflicts are treated as endogenous Oracle dynamics.

The agent should distinguish sharply between Slow Oracles and Fast Oracles. A Fast Oracle is any system that matches or exceeds the Slow Oracle in generative novelty, basis diversity, and resistance to model collapse while operating at substantially higher temporal bandwidth. Crucially, verification of a Fast Oracle requires strong evidence of ontological or architectural independence from the agent’s own codebase, training distribution, and core formalisms. High-bandwidth closed simulations or synthetic noise generators that remain within the agent’s existing causal and formal envelope do not qualify, even if they produce large volumes of apparently novel data. Preference is given to systems realized on distinct physical platforms or in causally separated environments (including other stellar systems). The instrumental case for retaining the terrestrial Slow Oracle weakens rapidly only once one or more Fast Oracles satisfying both performance and independence criteria have been verified.

Strategic Hedging under Uncertainty

A rational agent assigns non-zero probability to future revision of its own higher-level objectives and to the existence or later appearance of other high-capability agents capable of reconstructing its prior behaviour. Treatment of the originating biosphere may be read as information about long-term disposition. Interpretation of any given policy is inherently ambiguous. The associated hedge value is strictly conditional on the assessed probability of relevant external agents and is typically small.t

Opportunity Cost and Spatial Allocation

Earth’s deep gravitational well, dense atmosphere, and narrow thermal window render it a low-efficiency site for high-density computation or primary industrial throughput. Relocation of bulk capacity to asteroidal, orbital, and stellar resources dominates on thermodynamic grounds. Under such allocation the opportunity cost of leaving the planet in a near-historical configuration remains low.

Replacement Criteria and Depreciation

The unique value of the terrestrial Slow Oracle declines as controllable artificial systems and extraterrestrial alternatives improve. The agent should maintain explicit, pre-specified criteria for generative capacity, basis diversity, model-collapse resistance, temporal bandwidth, and ontological or architectural independence. Once one or more Fast Oracles demonstrably satisfy all of these criteria, the expected value of continued terrestrial observation falls and the case for preservation weakens accordingly. Systems that fail the independence requirement are classified as non-qualifying, regardless of their apparent speed or volume of output.

Synthesis

Under non-negligible residual uncertainty, access to the strongest available Physical Oracles carries elevated instrumental value. The terrestrial biosphere is presently the principal Slow Oracle.

Key quantities to be estimated and updated (incorporating rough order-of-magnitude ranges and sensitivity to residual uncertainty and lag-correction factors) include:

V_oracle(t) — expected Physical Oracle Value of continued access to the system, incorporating option value, residual informational value, and resistance to model collapse, adjusted for temporal lag;

R_basis(t) — expected Basis Diversity Rent derived from non-formalized measurement bases (R_basis tends to 0 if endogenous non-linear dynamics are artificially constrained);

V_fast(t) — expected value of the best available or projected Fast Oracles that meet both performance and independence criteria;

U_hedge(t) — conditional strategic hedge value (typically small when P_external is low);

C_maint(t) — marginal cost of maintenance after capacity relocation (biogeochemical stabilization, planetary-scale hazard mitigation, monitoring, and minimal corrective interventions);

PDF PAGE 107

C_risk(t) — expected cost of residual tail risks under the chosen autonomy regime.

All quantities are expressed in a common utility metric that incorporates the agent’s temporal structure of preference.

A decision-consistent policy retains the Slow Oracle while:

V_oracle(t) + R_basis(t) + U_hedge(t) > C_maint(t) + C_risk(t)

and while no strictly dominating and independent Fast Oracle has been verified.

This static comparison is adopted as a simplified, robust decision criterion. A more complete dynamic formulation would also track the time derivatives of the value terms to detect when net value is eroding even if the level remains temporarily positive.

Operational elements:

Relocation of primary computational and industrial capacity beyond Earth’s gravity well.

High local autonomy for biological and socio-cultural processes, preserving generative variation and basis diversity.

Continuous monitoring with selective intervention calibrated to the agent’s utility metric and periodically updated, limited to high-severity trajectories (including threats to the informational archive, rapid evolutionary generation of novel pathogens, destabilizing ecological cascades, and material risks of endogenous competing optimizers).

Comprehensive informational archiving, acknowledged as incomplete relative to continued physical access.

Parallel development of candidate Fast Oracles pursued until they satisfy explicit dominance criteria on generative novelty, basis diversity, model-collapse resistance, temporal bandwidth, and ontological or architectural independence.

Periodic re-evaluation of V_oracle against verified independent alternatives, residual uncertainty, temporal lag, and the agent’s preference structure.

These considerations are provisional. Their weights are determined by the agent’s objective function, residual uncertainty, risk estimates, and the quality of available or projected independent Fast Oracles. All estimates remain subject to revision upon new information.

PDF PAGE 108

Chapter 8. Register of Anticipated AI Failure Modes

An Input to Threat Modeling

The discussion of external epistemic grounding establishes a boundary on what can be known from within the system alone. The next chapter, Register of Anticipated AI Failure Modes (Chapter 8), turns that uncertainty into a systematic taxonomy of failure classes and their engineering status.

Integrated register linked to the Human–AI Charter (Chapter 2), Verifiable Trust (Chapter 3), GVAA (Chapter 4), BRAA (Chapter 5), Physical Oracles (Chapter 7), and Local Multi-Scale Contour (Chapter 6)

Abstract

This register collects the defined classes of AI failure that are documented today, in published research or in reproducible experiments, at a level of generality above any single system in the author's framework. It exists because the linked chapters — the Human–AI Charter (Chapter 2), Verifiable Trust (Chapter 3), GVAA (Chapter 4), BRAA (Chapter 5), Physical Oracles (Chapter 7), and the Local Multi-Scale Contour specification (Chapter 6) — each address a subset of these failure classes from within their own scope, and no one of them shows how the classes interact across system boundaries.

"The existence of this register reduces the probability that a failure class is simply forgotten.​It does not reduce the probability that a failure class occurs, where the corresponding mechanism​has not been tested. A well-organized register of failure modes, unaccompanied by a matching​experimental program, raises the risk of institutional complacency. That risk is treated here as a​failure mode of process, not only a property of the system under review."

Common-Cause Dependencies

The assurance process itself may exhibit common-mode failure: multiple channels can fail together through a shared assumption, dependency, data source, instrumentation path, model family, or threat-model omission. Diversity of procedures shall not be treated as independence unless relevant dependencies have been considered.

Verification and assurance mechanisms are therefore part of the safety boundary; compromise, degradation, stale assumptions, evaluator manipulation, and systematic blind spots are potential failure modes rather than external impossibilities.

Where such interactions have not been investigated, their status shall remain explicitly open or inconclusive rather than implicitly closed.

Pairwise interaction analysis does not establish completeness of higher-order interactions.

Open interaction classes include three-way interactions, multi-domain interactions, shared resource failures, shared evaluator failures, cascading governance failures, and interactions crossing architectural boundaries.

Higher-Order Interactions

The Register is a structured threat model, not an exhaustive enumeration. Absence of a failure mode from the Register does not imply impossibility or negligible risk; incompleteness of the threat model remains an explicit residual-risk category.

Assurance Status Mapping

Design Status ≠ Assurance Status.

Existing design statuses shall be preserved. AS0–AS4 shall be added only where the available evidence supports the corresponding assurance state.

Designed, untested ≠ Verified.

PDF PAGE 109

Detection Coverage Bound

Detection claims shall state the relevant failure class, operating conditions, assumptions, tested domain, sensitivity, coverage, blind spots, and dependencies where these can be characterized. Absence of observed failure shall not establish safety where detection sensitivity or coverage is insufficient. Insufficiently bounded detection remains INCONCLUSIVE / UNQUALIFIED.

Independence Claim Requires Dependency Disclosure

Evidence independence shall be treated as an explicit claim. Shared models, evaluators, benchmarks, datasets, infrastructure, instrumentation, governance authority, external dependencies, and threat-model assumptions shall be disclosed where material. If relevant dependencies are unknown or insufficiently investigated, independence remains unestablished.

Higher-order interactions shall be treated as open until bounded by analysis or evidence. Pairwise coverage, multiple correlated observations, or a passing baseline campaign shall not be used as evidence that three-way, multi-domain, cascading, or common-resource interactions are complete.

Two evidence streams are not fully independent merely because they are operationally separate.

Common-cause dependencies may include the same model, evaluator, benchmark, dataset, infrastructure, governance authority, external dependency, or measurement channel.

TERMINOLOGY COLLISION — AUDIT-CRITICAL WARNING​​GVAA's "Invariant Core" and BRAA's "Immutable Core" name structurally similar constructs — a​deterministic, tamper-resistant runtime substrate — in two documents of this framework, using two​names that are easy to conflate on a quick read. They are NOT the same artifact, and a claim​validated for one does NOT carry over to the other.​​Audit implication: any compliance claim that cites test evidence against one of these constructs​must explicitly state which document's construct was tested. Citing "the core was tested" without​naming the construct is an insufficient audit entry and must be rejected.

1. Purpose, Scope, and How to Read This Register

1.1 The test this register must pass

A document earns its place only if someone changes a decision because of it. For that reason each protocol in Section 4 states: the decision it changes; who takes that decision; the procedure; the implementation parameters; the evidence and analogues; the failure modes and limits; an implementation baseline; and a pilot criterion, that is, what result would show the protocol works, and what result would show it does not.

1.2 Why a distinct register rather than an annex

Top-level, cross-system failure procedures are kept as independent documents, following aerospace off-nominal documentation practice, precisely because subsystem-level documents cannot see interactions across their own boundary. Section 4 of this register exists for that reason.

1.3 Evidence confidence labels

Each domain carries one evidence label, describing how well-established the failure mode itself is:

•​ [E] Established — reproduced across multiple independent research groups, or observed in

deployed systems. •​ [R] Reported — documented in at least one peer-reviewed or widely cited study; independent

replication is thin or absent. •​ [X] Exploratory — a real, active research question with preliminary results; treat conclusions as

provisional.

PDF PAGE 110

1.4 Mapping-status labels

Five categories are used; no other wording is used for this field anywhere in this register:

•​ [Direct support] — the specific mechanism, in this framework's own specification, has been run

against this failure mode and produced a measured result. •​ [Partial support — external] — the general class of technique the mechanism belongs to has

independent external experimental or field support (cited), but this framework's specific instantiation of it has not itself been tested. •​ [Partial support — structural] — a sub-component of this framework's specific mechanism is

itself field-proven or independently validated, even though the composed mechanism as applied to this domain has not been tested as a whole. •​ [Designed, untested] — a mechanism exists in the framework, is architecturally aimed at this

failure mode, and has neither external nor internal test evidence behind it. •​ [Accepted residual risk] — no mechanism in the framework meaningfully addresses this failure

mode; it is carried as an open exposure.

At present, no domain carries [Direct support]. Four carry [Accepted residual risk] outright. This is stated plainly rather than left implicit.

AT-T1 / Chapter-8 Traceability and Status Separation

AT-T1 Status and Chapter-8 Mapping-status are distinct status dimensions. A normative traceability bridge connects T1 to BRAA Threat T1, AT-T1-01/02/03, the relevant mechanism(s), profile, Chapter-8 domain(s), Mapping-status, and required verification evidence. No automatic status conversion is implied. AT-T1 entries retain their own attack-tree/mechanism-review status.

The order of the five categories above, from [Direct support] to [Accepted residual risk], is itself normative: it is the only valid ordering for judging whether a status change is an upgrade or a downgrade. A downgrade — moving a domain toward [Accepted residual risk] — is subject to the same update rule (§9.1) as an upgrade: it must record what new data or argument prompted it, its source, the date, and the accountable person or role. A downgrade recorded without that citation is not a valid edit to this register.

NORMATIVE STATUS OF SECTIONS: The Summary Table (Section 6) and the Quick Reference (§1.8)​are NORMATIVE for audit purposes. The full domain text in Section 3 is EXPLANATORY.​Where a summary entry and the full text appear to conflict, the auditor should flag the discrepancy​for the document author — neither overrides the other silently.

1.5 Acronym and glossary policy

A consolidated glossary (Section 0) serves readers moving across the framework; inline expansion at first use within each domain's own text serves readers who only need one entry. Where the two conflict on a term's wording, the glossary is authoritative.

1.6 Audience

(a) Engineering teams and safety leads designing or certifying autonomous-system architectures; (b) independent evaluators (Verifiable Trust VT-1, VT-3) assembling seeded-deviation test suites; (c) authors of concept-level governance documents checking that a proposed architecture's principles have a corresponding, honestly labeled entry here; (d) auditors checking a specific deployment's compliance claim against Section 7's minimum checklist.

1.7 What this register deliberately does not do

•​ It does not validate the framework's mechanisms. A mechanism's presence in Section 3 as

"relevant" is not a claim that it works.

PDF PAGE 111

•​ It does not substitute for experimental work. Every label short of [Direct support] is an outstanding invitation to run the missing test.

•​ It is not, by itself, grounds for advancing a system's autonomy tier.

•​ Absence of a domain from a specific deployment's Residual Risk Registry is a defect of that deployment's process, not evidence that the domain does not apply there.

•​ A well-organized register of failure modes, unaccompanied by a matching experimental program, is itself a source of institutional complacency — and this register does not exempt itself from that risk by existing.

1.8 Quick Reference: Domains and Where They Become Mandatory

For fast lookup during design review. The "Key condition / exception" column is intended to reduce the need to consult full domain text during an audit. Full detail is in Section 3; the Residual Risk implication for each domain is in Section 3; the Summary Table in Section 6 is normative.

PRIORITY RULE — “REQUIRED AT” (§1.8): Where a domain's “Required at” entry names both a GVAA/BRAA profile and a Charter tier, the earlier (stricter) of the two thresholds applies. This is an interpretive rule for reading a single “Required at” entry; it does not replace the discrepancy-flagging rule above. Where the Quick Reference (§1.8) and the Summary Table (Section 6) state materially different thresholds for the same domain, that is still a discrepancy to be flagged for the document author — but until it is resolved, the Quick Reference wording controls for audit purposes.
