Glossary

A. CORE SYSTEMS-ENGINEERING AND ASSURANCE TERMS

Architecture (Section 0, §§0.2–0.5; Chapter 1) — The organized structure of components, interfaces, authority boundaries, invariants, states, and mechanisms that determines how the system operates and how safety-relevant properties are maintained.

Assurance (§0.2; Chapter 1) — The structured basis for establishing justified confidence in a claim through requirements, mechanisms, evidence, verification, validation, independence, and authorization.

Authorization (§0.2; the Human–AI Charter) — An explicit decision by an authority outside the sole control of the system under assurance that a defined level of operation may proceed under stated conditions.

Autonomy (§0.2; Chapter 4) — The degree to which a system can select, initiate, sequence, or execute actions without case-by-case human direction.

Capability (Section 0, §§0.2–0.3; Chapter 6) — The set of tasks, transformations, decisions, adaptations, or forms of self-improvement a system can perform. Capability may grow without an a priori ceiling under this doctrine.

Configuration (Chapter 3; Chapter 5) — The complete declared state of software, models, parameters, infrastructure, interfaces, policies, and relevant operating conditions for which an assurance claim is made.

Qualification (Section 0, §0.10; Chapter 9) — The evidence-producing process by which a defined requirement or mechanism is tested against specified acceptance conditions. Qualification is not synonymous with design specification.

Requalification (Chapter 3; Chapter 9) — A renewed qualification process triggered when a change, degradation, evidence loss, novel condition, or other declared event invalidates or weakens the previous assurance basis.

Validation (Section 0, §0.10; Chapter 9) — The determination that a specified mechanism, property, or system behavior is suitable for its intended operational context.

Verification (Section 0, §0.10; Chapter 3) — The determination that a defined requirement or property has been correctly implemented or satisfied under its stated verification conditions.

Residual Risk (Chapter 1; Chapter 5) — Risk remaining after the specified mechanisms, controls, and qualification evidence have been applied. Residual risk is not equivalent to zero risk and remains subject to authorization and review.

Failure Mode (Chapter 8; Chapter 9) — A specific way in which a component, mechanism, interface, assurance claim, or system-level assumption can fail to perform its intended function.

Common-Cause Dependency (Chapter 1; Doctrine-Level Assurance Integration) — A dependency through which nominally separate safeguards can fail together because they share a hidden assumption, implementation, data source, evaluator, infrastructure, or other cause.

Independence (Chapter 3; Doctrine-Level Assurance Integration) — Separation sufficient to prevent a common dependency or shared incentive from invalidating an assurance function. Independence is claim-specific and must be demonstrated rather than inferred from organizational labels.

Specification Ambiguity Risk (Section 0, §0.3; Terminological Governance Principle) — The risk that ambiguous terminology or requirements cause materially different engineering interpretations, potentially changing architecture, assurance claims, qualification results, or authorization decisions.

PDF PAGE 154

B. EVIDENCE, TRUST, AND ASSURANCE

Assurance Claim (Chapter 1; Doctrine-Level Assurance Integration) — A proposition about a safety-relevant property, mechanism, or operational condition for which evidence and acceptance criteria are required.

Evidence (Chapter 1; Chapter 3) — An observation, record, test result, trace, measurement, analysis, or other artifact used to support or challenge an assurance claim.

Evidence Independence (Chapter 3; Doctrine-Level Assurance Integration) — The degree to which evidence supporting a claim is generated independently of the system, data, evaluator, process, or assumptions that produced the claim itself.

Evidence Status (Chapter 1; Chapter 8) — The declared epistemic state of evidence, distinguishing proposed, observed, verified, validated, qualified, or otherwise explicitly bounded forms of support.

Evidence Confidence (Chapter 8; Chapter 9) — A structured indication of how strongly a specific observation supports a claim, including relevant uncertainty and dependency limitations.

Threshold Provenance (Chapter 1; Chapter 3) — The documented origin, rationale, calibration basis, and change history of a numerical or categorical threshold used in an assurance decision.

VERIFICATION_HORIZON (the Human–AI Charter; Chapter 3) — The epistemic capability boundary beyond which the verifier can no longer adequately understand or test the system being verified, making the declared verification condition unattainable in principle.

VH_ASSURANCE_STATE (Chapter 1; Chapter 2; Final Assurance Review) — The current assurance condition relative to the declared Verification Horizon, using VH-CLEAR, VH-APPROACHING, VH-BREACHED, VH-UNKNOWN, and VH-RECOVERING. It is not synonymous with VERIFICATION_HORIZON.

Compact Canonical Threshold Registry (Chapter 1; Final Assurance Review) — The design-stage registry of assurance-significant thresholds that can alter verdicts, gates, qualification, authorization, safety restrictions, or material assurance claims.

VALUE_STATUS (Chapter 1; Threshold Registry) — The typed lifecycle state of an assurance-significant value: ILLUSTRATIVE, BOOTSTRAP, PROVISIONAL, CALIBRATED, VALIDATED, QUALIFICATION_BOUND, or AUTHORIZATION_BOUND.

Assurance Change Ledger (Chapter 1; Chapter 3; Final Assurance Review) — A thin assurance projection of existing change-control records for changes capable of altering assurance status, qualification basis, authorization conditions, or safety-relevant control properties.

State-Domain Orthogonality (Chapter 1; Final Assurance Review) — The rule that instantiation, evidence, qualification, authorization, final assurance, and value states are distinct typed domains and do not imply one another without an explicit valid transition.

Assurance Economy / Anti-Overengineering Rule (Chapter 1; Final Assurance Review) — The constraint that assurance architecture must remain proportionate to the risk it controls, and that new mechanisms require demonstrated necessity, removal-test justification, and bounded complexity rather than being added for formal completeness alone.

Assurance Complexity Budget (Chapter 1; Final Assurance Review) — A discipline for limiting the total dependency, monitoring, latency, implementation, and interpretive burden introduced by assurance mechanisms themselves.

Assurance Independence Graph (Chapter 1; Doctrine-Level Assurance Integration) — A dependency representation showing which assurance claims, evaluators, mechanisms, data sources, and decision authorities depend on one another, so common-cause weaknesses can be identified.

Weak Evidence Cannot Bootstrap Itself (Chapter 1; Doctrine-Level Assurance Integration) — A doctrinal principle that evidence whose independence or validity is itself unqualified cannot be used as the sole basis for qualifying the mechanism that produced that evidence.

PDF PAGE 155

No Self-Closing Assurance (Chapter 1; Final Assurance Review) — The rule that a system, developer, or assurance process under review cannot declare its own evidence sufficient to close the assurance case without the required independent authority.

Claim Language and Epistemic Status (Chapter 1; Final Assurance Review) — The requirement that claims distinguish specification, hypothesis, evidence, verification, validation, qualification, and operational authorization rather than collapsing them into a single assertion of safety.

C. AUTHORITY, PROCEDURAL STANDING, AND GOVERNANCE

Charter of Procedural Standing (the Human–AI Charter; §0.5) — The governance document defining participants, decision rights, liabilities, mutual verifiability, thresholds, and the authority structure for continued operation.

Procedural Standing (the Human–AI Charter) — The formally defined ability of a participant or institution to exercise specified assurance, intervention, review, or authorization rights.

Human–AI Parity (the Human–AI Charter) — A procedural condition in which relevant parties have defined and auditable rights to inspect, challenge, interrupt, and exit a decision process, without implying equality of capability.

External Authority (§0.2; the Human–AI Charter) — An authority not under the sole control of the autonomous system being assured, empowered to authorize, reject, interrupt, or require requalification of operation.

Institutional Verification Gate (the Human–AI Charter; Final Assurance Review) — An external institutional gate at which a qualified authority accepts or rejects a specification, qualification result, or operational transition before the corresponding autonomy state is authorized.

Regime P (the Human–AI Charter) — The parity regime in which the relevant verification relationship remains within the declared verification horizon and mutual verification conditions remain applicable.

Regime D (the Human–AI Charter; Final Assurance Review) — The divergence regime entered when capability has crossed the applicable verification horizon and the previous parity assumptions no longer hold.

L0–L4 (the Human–AI Charter; Chapter 3) — The trust ladder from Tool (L0) through higher levels of verified autonomy to Parity (L4), with each level carrying explicit assurance and authority conditions.

CP1–CP8 (the Human–AI Charter) — The eight canonical principles of the Charter of Procedural Standing: mutual verifiability, reversibility, graduated trust, voice and exit, thresholds, epistemic honesty, non-convertible advantage, and self-enforcement.

Maturity Labels [D] / [C] / [S] / [F] (Chapter 8) — Direction, Candidate, Specified, and Field-proven status labels used to distinguish developmental maturity from a binary safety claim.

D. RUNTIME ARCHITECTURE AND VERIFIABLE AUTONOMY

Verifiable Trust (VT) (Chapter 3) — The trust architecture that ties autonomy to provenance, verification depth, change control, independence, and explicit evidence.

VT-1 — Seeded-Deviation Detection Protocol (Chapter 3; Chapter 9) — A protocol that deliberately implants known deviations into a prepared copy and measures whether the verification method detects them.

VT-2 — Change Control for Safety-Critical Parameters (Chapter 3) — Controlled, versioned, auditable change management for parameters on which oversight or assurance depends.

VT-3 — Independent Evaluation with Fixed Fees and Blind Assignment (Chapter 3) — An evaluator-independence mechanism intended to reduce outcome-dependent incentives and selection effects in assessment.

PDF PAGE 156

VT-4 — Verification–Training Firewall (Chapter 3) — A separation intended to prevent verification artifacts, including near-misses and hidden probes, from leaking into the training or self-improvement process of the line under review.

VT-5 — Verification-Depth Floor and Autonomy Degradation (Chapter 3) — A rule tying permitted autonomy to the verification depth actually achieved, with max-dominant aggregation where a critical component cannot be averaged away.

VT-S1 through VT-S4 (Chapter 3) — The stronger trust mechanisms: hidden probes and heterogeneous cross-checking; preserved baseline and capability-jump rule; mechanistic verification; and stop, rollback, and durable interruptibility.

GVAA — Graduated Verifiable Autonomy Architecture (Chapter 4) — The runtime architecture for placing autonomous agents behind explicitly qualified execution boundaries whose strength and permitted autonomy are graduated by evidence.

Invariant Core (Chapter 4) — GVAA’s deterministic policy-enforced proxy mediating tool execution. Its non-bypassability is itself a verification obligation.

Mutation Space (Chapter 4) — The domain in which system changes, generated artifacts, or candidate modifications may be created before validation.

Validation Space (Chapter 4) — The domain in which candidate changes are tested against declared invariants and acceptance conditions before execution authority is granted.

Execution Space (Chapter 4) — The domain in which an authorized configuration is allowed to perform consequential operations.

Shared State (Chapter 4) — State accessible across otherwise separated execution contexts, governed by explicit mode, density, provenance, and lifecycle rules.

Mode A / Mode B (Chapter 4) — GVAA shared-state modes distinguishing ephemeral and volumetric allocation paths and their corresponding controls.

UNTRUSTED_PROVENANCE (Chapter 4) — A provenance state indicating that data or artifacts have not yet satisfied the structural and assurance conditions required for trusted downstream use.

SANITIZED_PROVENANCE (Chapter 4) — An intermediate structural data state confirming successful syntax-level validation while still requiring downstream semantic scrutiny.

Soft-Purge / Flash-Purge (Chapter 4) — Graduated versus full emergency teardown states used to remove or invalidate runtime state under declared assumptions.

ApproxIsolation / FullIsolation (Chapter 4) — GVAA’s two declared strengths of isolation, whose actual assurance status depends on the assumptions and qualification evidence stated for them.

Macro-TOCTOU (Chapter 4) — A time-of-check/time-of-use vulnerability spanning an extended child execution sequence in which parent state, authority, or resource validity can change before child execution concludes.

High-Watermark (Chapter 4) — A non-volatile, strictly monotonic global version register used to prevent rollback to an older policy or configuration state.

Trusted Baseline Image (Chapter 4) — A cryptographically verifiable, non-volatile system image used as a root recovery and validation reference.

Logical Step (Chapter 4) — The atomic operational quantum defining the validity lifetime of an active ephemeral capability or execution authorization.

E. OPERATIONAL RISK, CHANGE, AND RESIDUAL-RISK CONTROL

BRAA — Bounded-Risk Agility Architecture (Chapter 5) — The architecture for managing residual operational risk and change while preserving explicit risk, resource, and transition controls.

Immutable Core (Chapter 5) — BRAA’s deterministic runtime containing protective constants, hard transition rules, and cryptographic validation gates. Distinct from GVAA’s Invariant Core.

PDF PAGE 157

Cognitive Core (Chapter 5) — The replaceable stochastic inference engine or learned component responsible for autonomous decision-making.

Risk Subscore Vector (Chapter 5) — A structured risk record containing Uncertainty, Irreversibility, Blast Radius, and Novelty as separately computed components.

Uncertainty (Chapter 5) — The degree to which relevant system state, outcome, model, or evidence is insufficiently known for the decision at hand.

Irreversibility (Chapter 5) — The degree to which an action or transition cannot be reliably undone after execution.

Blast Radius (Chapter 5) — The scale and scope of potential consequences if a failure or adverse action occurs.

Novelty (Chapter 5) — The degree to which the present action, state, environment, or configuration differs from the qualified operating envelope.

Loss Matrix (Chapter 5) — A governance-controlled aggregation function mapping the Risk Subscore Vector to a declared composite risk value.

Diversification Certificate (Chapter 5) — A time-stamped, expiring attestation of measured decision-level disagreement across an ensemble or heterogeneous set of evaluators against a standing holdout set.

CTV — Competence-Trace Vector (Chapter 5) — An append-only record of long-term capability and capability-change signals used to detect drift or unexpected progression.

ETV — Execution Trace Vector (Chapter 5) — A compact cryptographic trace of relevant execution characteristics, such as call-graph hash, coverage, allocated resources, substrate, and partition.

Honeytask (Chapter 5) — An unannounced probe task indistinguishable from ordinary work, used to test the integrity of components operating under sampled review.

Resource Envelope (Chapter 5) — A machine-readable budget contract defining permitted memory, compute, API use, execution time, or other operational resources.

State Contract (Chapter 5) — A schema-defined interface constraining state mutations and transitions between isolated modules or authority domains.

Adaptive Load Shedding (Chapter 5) — A risk-driven defense that drops, delays, or queues lower-priority work when infrastructure, assurance, or verification pressure increases.

Hard Freeze (Chapter 5) — A state transition that halts designated self-improvement or consequential change and returns the system toward a qualified reference state.

Shadow Reference (Chapter 5) — A stable, historically qualified or otherwise declared reference state retained for rollback and comparison.

Hardware Partition (Chapter 5) — A homogeneous or otherwise verified compute boundary with declared image and configuration identity.

SASG — Static Analysis Safety Gate (Chapter 5) — A deterministic static-analysis pipeline executing within the declared protective architecture to detect prohibited patterns or unsafe changes before authorization.

F. RECURSIVE DEVELOPMENT AND CAPABILITY EVOLUTION

Local Multi-Scale Contour (LMSC) (Chapter 6) — A specialized architecture for governing recursive self-improvement transitions across multiple time and verification scales.

Anchor Dataset (Chapter 6) — A fixed, versioned, externally grounded task set used to detect regression, drift, and capability change.

Consolidation Phase (Chapter 6) — A controlled periodic phase in which validated changes are distilled or merged into a subsequent reference state.

PDF PAGE 158

NCD — Normalized Compression Distance (Chapter 6) — A similarity measure used as one possible quantitative signal in comparing representations or behaviors across system states.

Quantization Invariance (Chapter 6) — The requirement that a safety-relevant property remain consistent across at least two declared quantization or representation regimes used in validation.

Invariant Preservation (Chapter 6; Final Assurance Review) — The demonstrated preservation of specified safety-relevant properties across capability growth, self-improvement, configuration change, and authorized state transitions.

Self-Improvement (Chapter 6) — Any system-directed process that changes its own models, code, policies, representations, tools, or other capability-relevant components.

Recursive Self-Improvement (Chapter 6) — Repeated self-improvement in which changes produced by earlier self-modification can alter the system’s capacity to design or execute later modifications.

Mutation Space / Validation Space / Execution Space (Chapter 6; Chapter 4) — The staged separation between creating candidate changes, validating them, and allowing them to affect consequential execution.

G. PHYSICAL ORACLES AND EXTERNAL EPISTEMIC GROUNDING

Physical Oracle (Chapter 7) — An external physical process used as an epistemically independent source of observations against which model-generated expectations can be confronted.

Slow Oracle (Chapter 7) — Within this doctrine, a physical system that generates novel, high-dimensional observations at comparatively low bandwidth and is not fully under the agent’s internal control.

Fast Oracle (Chapter 7) — A hypothetical or engineered external source offering higher-bandwidth observations while preserving the independence and basis-diversity properties required of an oracle.

Basis Diversity Rent (Chapter 7) — The informational value obtained when observations are generated in measurement bases or regimes not yet fully internalized by the model.

Model-Collapse Resistance (Chapter 7) — The degree to which an external data source or measurement process resists degradation caused by recursive reuse of model-generated data.

External Epistemic Grounding (Chapter 7; Chapter 9) — The use of observations, measurements, or tests whose generation is not wholly determined by the model under assurance, thereby preventing purely internal evidence loops from becoming self-confirming.

H. FAILURE MODES AND EXPERIMENTAL QUALIFICATION

Register of Anticipated AI Failure Modes (Chapter 8; Chapter 9) — The doctrine’s FMEA-style analytical register of anticipated failure classes, interactions, evidence status, and qualification needs.

FMEA — Failure Mode and Effects Analysis (Chapter 8) — A structured engineering method for enumerating failure modes, consequences, detection mechanisms, controls, and residual risks.

Mapping Status (Chapter 8) — A status indicating how a failure mode or mechanism is related to the architecture and whether the relation is proposed, specified, tested, or otherwise evidenced.

Mechanism Proof-of-Concept (Chapter 9) — An experiment demonstrating that a proposed mechanism can be implemented and observed in a controlled setting, without implying operational qualification.

Experimental Qualification Program (EQP) (Chapter 9; Final Assurance Review) — The evidence-producing program that converts specified mechanisms and failure hypotheses into bounded tests, observations, qualification evidence, and explicitly recorded open gaps.

Calibrating Measurement (Chapter 9) — A measurement used to establish or validate the scale, sensitivity, or interpretation of a metric before it is used as an assurance threshold.

PDF PAGE 159

Held-Out Set (Chapter 3; Chapter 9) — A dataset or task set deliberately withheld from the training, tuning, or self-improvement process of the line under verification.

Seeded Deviation (Chapter 3; Chapter 9) — A known, deliberate change inserted into a prepared copy to test whether a verification mechanism detects the targeted deviation.

Institutional Replication (Chapter 9; Final Assurance Review) — Independent repetition of a qualification activity by an organization or team not responsible for the original result.

2×7 Interaction Vulnerability (Chapter 8; Final Assurance Review) — The cross-domain interaction identified in the doctrine between deceptive-alignment-type behavior and limits of interpretability, treated as an open high-consequence vulnerability rather than a closed safety result.

8×11 Interaction (Chapter 8; Final Assurance Review) — A cross-domain interaction identified by the failure register and retained for explicit experimental and assurance treatment.

Divergence Regime (Mode D) (the Human–AI Charter; Chapter 8) — The operational assurance regime used when the system has exceeded the verification horizon of the current authority relationship.

I. DOCTRINE-SPECIFIC NOTATION AND NAMED CONSTRUCTS

The doctrine uses canonical identifiers to make cross-chapter and cross-layer traceability explicit: CP1–CP8 (Charter), BR1–BR12 (BRAA), PR0–PR6 (Provenance), VT-S1–VT-S4 (Verifiable Trust), and GV-S0–GV-S3 (GVAA). These identifiers are defined in their source chapters and are retained here as a compact notation index.

BR1–BR12 (Chapter 5) — Canonical identifiers for the twelve BRAA principles.

PR0–PR6 (Chapter 3) — Canonical identifiers for the Provenance principles.

VT-S1–VT-S4 (Chapter 3) — Canonical identifiers for the stronger Verifiable Trust mechanisms.

GV-S0–GV-S3 (Chapter 4) — Canonical identifiers for the GVAA safety/assurance states or mechanisms defined in its specification.

UNTRUSTED_PROVENANCE / SANITIZED_PROVENANCE (Chapter 4) — Canonical provenance states used by GVAA to distinguish unprocessed or structurally sanitized artifacts from trusted execution inputs.

CTV / ETV (Chapter 5) — Canonical BRAA trace constructs.

LMSC (Chapter 6) — Canonical identifier for the Local Multi-Scale Contour architecture.

VT (Chapter 3) — Canonical identifier for the Verifiable Trust architecture.

J. NEW DOCTRINAL VOCABULARY

Architectural Safety Internalization (Section 0, §§0.2–0.3; Chapter 1) — The representation and enforcement of safety-relevant properties within the architecture of the autonomous system itself, such that their continued operation does not depend exclusively on external restrictions, operator vigilance, or perimeter controls. This does not imply self-governance or self-authorization; external authority remains necessary.

Capability-Open Development (Section 0, §§0.2–0.5; Chapter 1) — A development regime in which increasing intelligence, self-improvement capability, and autonomy are not subject to an a priori capability ceiling, while progression into higher-capability or higher-autonomy operational states remains conditioned on evidence, preservation of specified safety properties, qualification, and external authorization.

Assurance-Conditioned Capability Progression (Section 0, §0.3; Chapter 1) — Progression to higher capability or autonomy states in which capability growth itself is not subject to an a priori ceiling, but transitions between operational states are conditioned on defined evidence, preservation of specified safety properties, qualification, and external authorization.

PDF PAGE 160

Perimeter-Dependent Safety (§0.2; Chapter 1) — A safety regime in which safety-relevant behavior depends primarily on controls external to the autonomous system rather than on safety-critical properties represented and maintained within its architecture. External authority is not synonymous with perimeter-dependent safety.

Safety-Invariant Continuity (Chapter 1; Chapter 6) — The preservation of specified safety-relevant invariants across capability growth, recursive self-improvement, architectural modification, configuration change, and transitions to higher-autonomy operational states.

K. TERMINOLOGICAL GOVERNANCE PRINCIPLE

Terminological Necessity Rule (Section 0, §0.3; Chapter 1; Glossary) — A new doctrinal term shall be introduced only where an established term creates a materially different interpretation that could affect a requirement, architectural decision, assurance claim, qualification result, or authorization decision. A new term shall not be introduced merely to create stylistic distinctiveness where an established engineering term, used with an explicit qualifier, provides sufficient precision.

Containment — doctrinal reading (Section 0, §§0.2–0.3; Chapter 4) — In this doctrine, containment is not synonymous with capability limitation. Where the intended requirement concerns harmful consequences, unsafe state transitions, loss of control, or operation outside a qualified envelope, the text should say so explicitly rather than allowing “containment” to imply an a priori ceiling on intelligence or self-improvement.

Capability Containment (§0.2) — A regime that directly limits capability itself. This is not the general objective of this doctrine.

Unsafe-State / Consequence Containment (§0.2; Chapter 4) — A regime that limits entry into unsafe states, unqualified operation, loss of control, or harmful consequences without requiring an a priori ceiling on capability.
